Homotopy coherent Gysin functoriality
Frédéric Déglise, Niels Feld, Fangzhou Jin

Abstract
. We construct homotopy coherent Gysin pullbacks for arbitrary morphisms between smooth schemes from the geometry of higher deformation spaces to the normal cone. This addresses the long-standing problem of organizing Gysin morphisms into a genuinely higher contravariant functorial structure. The main input is a higher-dimensional refinement of the Fulton-MacPherson and Rost constructions: to a flag of closed immersions we attach a higher deformation space whose ordered boundary produces, by iterated residues and inflation, the higher simplices of a Gysin functor. We prove the face and degeneracy relations by introducing specialization flags and confluences, and then rigidify the resulting contractible choices to obtain an honest simplicial map. The construction applies to any weak Borel-Moore theory: a stable infinity-categorical coefficient system with smooth contravariance, localization, primitive closed pushforwards, 𝔸 1 \mathbb{A}^{1} -invariance and inflation maps. For such a theory 𝒢 \mathcal{G} , and every m ∈ 𝐙 m\in\mathbf{Z} , we obtain a contravariant Gysin functor 𝒢 m lci : N ​ ( Sm S ) op ⟶ 𝒞 \mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{lci}}:N(\Sm_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C} extending smooth pullbacks and closed-immersion Gysin morphisms, and coherent with graph factorizations in all simplicial degrees. As a main application, we apply this machinery to Rost-Schmid complexes of MW-homodules over a noetherian excellent base. We prove that these complexes define motivic objects in the 𝐏 1 \mathbf{P}^{1} -stable 𝔸 1 \mathbb{A}^{1} -derived category D 𝔸 1 ⁡ ( S , R ) {\Der_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}}(S,R) representing both cohomological and Borel-Moore Rost-Schmid theories. In particular, applying the construction to Milnor and Milnor-Witt K K -theory produces Rost-Schmid type extensions of Chow and Chow-Witt groups, equipped with localization long exact sequences and homotopy coherent lci pullbacks; on the diagonal these groups recover the usual Chow and Chow-Witt groups. For the standard examples we also establish stability under exceptional pullback.



Introduction

Gysin maps and the coherence problem
.
Intersection theory has notably been organized around the construction of
pullback maps for morphisms which are not necessarily flat. Such maps,
sometimes called Gysin maps, are the mechanism by which one turns geometric intersections
into functorial operations. In the classical theory two complementary methods
play a central role. The first is the moving-lemma method, going back to Chow:
one moves cycles into a position in which ordinary flat pullback is available.
The second is the deformation-to-the-normal-cone method, developed and
systematized in the Fulton-MacPherson approach and in Fulton’s intersection theory
book
[
Ful98
]
. This second construction has the decisive advantage of
working without a base field and in great generality, provided one works with
quasi-projective, or more generally smoothable lci, morphisms.

Rost later recast this circle of ideas in an axiomatic and cycle-theoretic
form for cycle modules
[
Ros96
]
. One of the important lessons of Rost’s
paper is that the construction of Gysin maps is not only a problem of defining
individual morphisms. Associativity of pullbacks already forces one to analyze
double deformation spaces and to compare several ways of passing through their
boundary strata. Thus the first non-trivial coherence problem for Gysin maps
is already visible in the construction of Chow groups with coefficients.

For motivic homotopy theory this coherence problem becomes unavoidable in all
degrees. A motivic spectrum over a base scheme is built out of Nisnevich
sheaves on the smooth site in an infinity-categorical sense. Therefore a
Gysin construction which is meant to produce motivic objects cannot merely
assign a pullback map to each morphism and check associativity up to an
unspecified homotopy. It must describe all higher compositions of Gysin maps
coherently. In the language of quasi-categories
[
Lur09a
]
, this means
that one should construct a simplicial functor

N(\Sm_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

with values in a suitable target infinity-category
\mathcal{C}
. Equivalently,
one must construct not only edges, but also higher simplices witnessing all
relations among iterated Gysin pullbacks.

In motivic homotopy theory, Gysin functoriality is usually accessed through
motivic fundamental classes, as in the construction of Déglise-Jin-Khan

[
DJK21
]
. This gives trace and cotrace morphisms with the expected
compatibility in the homotopy category. What is not provided, however, is a
higher coherent compatibility with composition: in general one does not obtain
from these fundamental classes a simplicial, or infinity-categorical,
contravariant functor encoding all higher associativity constraints.
To our knowledge, the first construction in the literature which explicitly
addresses this higher coherence problem is Levine’s work on the coniveau
homotopy tower
[
Lev06
]
. Levine’s method is model-categorical and
homological, and may be viewed as a far-reaching refinement of Chow’s moving
lemma. Its range is nevertheless constrained by the use of moving techniques,
in particular by the regularity and low-dimensional assumptions on the base.
By contrast, the Fulton-MacPherson deformation to the normal cone, although
central to intersection theory and to Rost’s treatment of cycle modules, had not
previously been developed into a higher coherent Gysin construction. The main
purpose of this paper is to supply precisely this missing deformation-theoretic
approach.

The geometric input which makes such a construction possible is the theory of
higher deformation spaces, recently developed by Adrien Dubouloz and Arnaud
Mayeux, together with the complementary language of multi-centered dilatations

[
May23
,
DMdS23
,
DM24
]
. These spaces provide the
missing higher-dimensional analogue of Rost’s double deformation space. Their
boundary geometry produces the higher Gysin simplices attached to a flag of
closed immersions; after introducing specialization flags and confluences, it
also becomes precise enough to control the simplicial faces and degeneracies up
to the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-homotopies required by the construction.

The geometric idea
.
Let

\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})

be a flag of closed immersions between smooth
S
-schemes. To such a flag
one associates a higher deformation space using the Dubouloz-Mayeux theory

D(\sigma)\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}.

Its coordinate boundary is an ordered strict normal crossings divisor. The
open stratum is canonically identified with

Z_{n}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n},

while the deepest boundary stratum is a vector bundle over
Z_{0}
. The
basic philosophy of the paper is that the higher Gysin simplex attached to

\sigma
is the iterated residue of this ordered boundary, followed by the
identification of the deepest stratum with
Z_{0}
through
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance
and by the inflation maps coming from the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-coordinates.

This point of view generalizes simultaneously the ordinary deformation to the
normal cone and Rost’s use of double deformation spaces. In dimension one the
boundary gives the usual specialization map. In dimension two it contains the
geometry needed for associativity. In arbitrary dimension it produces an

(n-1)
-simplex in a mapping space, hence a genuine higher coherence datum.
The role of residues is played by a total boundary construction associated
with the localization cube of the ordered normal crossings divisor.

A key difficulty is that coordinate panels of
D(\sigma)
are not literally
the deformation spaces of the simplicial faces
d_{k}\sigma
. Instead they are
deformation spaces of new flags, called specialization flags, which remember
additional normal-bundle information. These specialization flags are the
correct geometric objects for comparing panels with faces. The comparison with
actual faces uses
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance in an essential way. Dually, degeneracy
operators are governed by confluences: maps on the parameter affine spaces
which replace one deformation parameter by a product of two adjacent
parameters. The confluence construction is the geometric source of the
homotopies controlling degeneracies.

Thus the construction is not a formal repackaging of known Gysin maps. It is
a new construction of higher simplices out of the boundary geometry of
higher deformation spaces, together with a proof that these simplices satisfy
the face and degeneracy identities up to canonical, contractible choices.

Weak Borel-Moore theories
.
The deformation construction is designed to work from elementary input. We do
not assume a six-functor formalism. Instead we isolate the exact structure
needed to evaluate localization on higher deformation spaces.

Definition A

(Weak Borel-Moore theory, introductory form)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a stable
\infty
-category. A weak Borel-Moore theory on

\Sm_{S}
, with values in
\mathcal{C}
, is a family of objects

X\longmapsto\mathcal{G}_{m}(X),\qquad m\in\mathbf{Z},

defined on smooth
S
-schemes, together with the following structural data.
First, the objects
\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)
are contravariantly functorial for smooth
morphisms, in a homotopy coherent sense. Second, one is given primitive
covariant morphisms for trivialized regular closed immersions. Third, one has
localization fiber sequences for principal smooth divisors and for their ordered
normal crossings refinements. Fourth, vector bundle projections are required to
act by specified
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-equivalences. Finally, the theory is equipped with
inflation maps

\beta^{(n)}_{X,m}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{m+n}\bigl(X\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}\bigr)[-n]

compatible with smooth pullback, localization, residues, and permutations of

\mathbb{G}_{m}
-coordinates.

The precise list of axioms, including the required specified homotopies, is
Definition
2.2.12
. In particular, the primitive closed maps in the
axioms go in the covariant direction; the contravariant Gysin pullbacks for
closed immersions are not part of the input, but are produced by the higher
deformation construction.

The main construction theorem
.
Our first main theorem asserts that this procedure produces genuine homotopy
coherent Gysin functoriality. In the introduction we record the result in the
following streamlined form; the fully precise statement is
Theorem
5.7.2
.

Theorem B

(Higher coherent Gysin functoriality)
.

Let
\mathcal{G}
be a weak Borel-Moore theory on
\Sm_{S}
with values in a stable

\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
. Then, for every
m\in\mathbf{Z}
, there exists a
simplicial functor

\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{lci}}:N(\Sm_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

with the following properties.

(1)

On objects,
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{lci}}
is given by
X\mapsto\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)
.

(2)

If
p:Y\to X
is smooth, the edge
p_{m}^{*,\operatorname{lci}}
is the smooth
pullback already present in the weak Borel-Moore theory.

(3)

If
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
is a closed immersion between smooth

S
-schemes, the edge
i_{m}^{*}
is the closed-immersion Gysin pullback
constructed from higher deformation spaces.

(4)

For an arbitrary morphism
f:Y\to X
, write

\Gamma_{f}:Y\hookrightarrow Y\times_{S}X,\qquad q_{f}:Y\times_{S}X\to X

for its graph immersion and the projection to
X
. Then
f_{m}^{*,\operatorname{lci}}
is canonically
identified with the graph formula

\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)\xrightarrow{\ q_{f}^{*}\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(Y\times_{S}X)\xrightarrow{\ (\Gamma_{f})_{m}^{*}\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(Y).

More generally, for every factorization
f=p\circ i
with
i
a closed immersion and

p
smooth,
f_{m}^{*,\operatorname{lci}}
is identified with
i_{m}^{*}\circ p^{*}
, compatibly up to
contractible coherent choice.
These identifications are homotopy coherent in all simplicial degrees; the independence of
(closed-immersion)-smooth factorizations is made precise in
Corollary
5.7.5
.

Equivalently,
\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{lci}}
is a homotopy coherent contravariant Gysin
functor on all morphisms of smooth
S
-schemes.

The proof has two logically distinct parts. First, Sections
2
-
4

construct the closed-immersion theory. For a flag
\sigma
of closed
immersions, the ordered boundary of
D(\sigma)
gives a higher Gysin simplex

\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{simp}}(\sigma)\in\Map_{\mathcal{C}}\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{m}(Z_{n}),\mathcal{G}_{m}(Z_{0})\bigr)_{n-1}.

The face relations are proved by comparing panels with specialization flags and
then using
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance to pass to the corresponding simplicial faces.
The degeneracy relations are proved by analyzing confluences of deformation
parameters. These comparisons are canonical up to contractible choice.

Second, Section
4
proves a rigidification
theorem which turns such contractible-choice data into an honest simplicial
functor. The point is that the construction of higher Gysin simplices requires
controlled choices: inverse data for vector bundle projections, choices of
composites, and fillers for higher spines. The rigidification theorem shows
that these choices can be made coherently once and for all. This is one of
the formal cores of the paper.

The passage from closed immersions to arbitrary morphisms between smooth

S
-schemes is then obtained in Section
5
. Every simplex

X_{0}\longrightarrow X_{1}\longrightarrow\cdots\longrightarrow X_{n}

of
N(\Sm_{S})
has a canonical graph flag inside an iterated fiber product. The closed-immersion
simplices associated with this graph flag live on the product vertices, so they do not by
themselves have the correct faces on the original vertices
X_{i}
. Section
5

therefore constructs a prismatic comparison whose lower face is the closed graph simplex and
whose vertical edges are the smooth pullbacks from the original vertices to the product vertices.
The upper faces of these prisms are the raw lci simplices. A graph-composition theorem then
provides their coherent operator comparisons, and the abstract rigidification theorem turns this
simplexwise package into the contravariant functor on
N(\Sm_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}
.

Rost-Schmid complexes and MW-homodules
.
The main application is to Rost-Schmid complexes of MW-homodules. These
objects were introduced in
[
Fel20
,
Fel21
,
DFJ25
]
as a homological form of
Milnor-Witt coefficients over general bases, designed to unify Chow groups,
Chow-Witt groups, quadratic refinements, and unramified cohomology. This is part of the broader program surrounding Ayoub’s conjectural description
of the heart of the perverse homotopy
t
-structure on the motivic homotopy
category, a
t
-structure introduced in
[
Ayo07
,
BD17
]
.
See
[
DFJ22
]
.

Let
S
be noetherian excellent, equipped with a dimension function
\delta
, and let

\mathcal{M}
be an
R
-linear MW-homodule over
S
. The cohomological
Rost-Schmid complex

C^{\bullet}(X,\mathcal{M}_{m})

forms, after the normalization used in Notation
6.1.1
, a weak
Borel-Moore theory on
\Sm_{S}
. The main theorem therefore gives a homotopy
coherent contravariant functoriality for these complexes for all morphisms of
smooth
S
-schemes. This extends Rost’s construction from ordinary
associativity constraints to all higher coherences.

Then, one can deduce a representability theorem: the family of complexes

\mathbf{H}(\mathcal{M})_{m}(X)=C^{\bullet}(X,\mathcal{M}_{m})

with the inflation maps coming from the
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-coordinate defines an

\Omega_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}
-spectrum in the
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-derived category

{\Der_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}}(S,R)=\operatorname{Sp}_{\mathbb{G}_{m}}\bigl(L_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}\operatorname{Sh}_{{\mathrm{Nis}}}(\Sm_{S},\mathcal{D}(R))\bigr).

Thus one obtains a functor

\mathbf{H}:\operatorname{MW-Homod}_{S}\longrightarrow{\Der_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}}(S,R),\qquad\mathcal{M}\longmapsto\mathbf{H}(\mathcal{M}),

as stated in Theorem
6.2.3
. This is a
representability statement in a linear motivic category and not merely after
forgetting to a more abstract stable homotopy category; it keeps the explicit
Rost-Schmid complexes visible.

The representing object computes both cohomology and Borel-Moore homology.
For smooth schemes, cohomological Rost-Schmid groups are represented by

\mathbf{H}(\mathcal{M})
, and for schemes which are locally closed in smooth
schemes the corresponding homological Rost-Schmid groups are represented by
Borel-Moore homology. In particular, the construction is a homological theory
with localization long exact sequences. Off the diagonal degrees it provides a
higher Rost-Schmid theory, close in spirit to higher Chow and Chow-Witt
constructions such as those considered by Bachmann and Yakerson in
[
BY20
]
, but defined here through Rost-Schmid complexes and
Borel-Moore localization. On the diagonal it recovers the usual Chow and
Chow-Witt groups in the conventions of
[
DFJ25
]
.

Chow groups, Chow-Witt groups, and purity
.
The preceding representability theorem becomes especially concrete for the
MW-homodules associated with Milnor
K
-theory and Milnor-Witt
K
-theory.
It gives motivic objects representing Chow groups and Chow-Witt groups, and it
also places these groups inside a higher homological theory with localization
long exact sequences and homotopy coherent lci pullbacks.

Theorem C

(Chow and Chow-Witt theories from Rost-Schmid complexes)
.

Assume that
S
is noetherian excellent, equipped with a dimension function
and with a pinning
\lambda
. Let

\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{M},\lambda},\qquad\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{MW},\lambda}\in{\Der_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}}(S,R)

be the motivic complexes associated respectively with Milnor
K
-theory

\operatorname{K}^{\mathrm{M}}_{*}
and Milnor-Witt
K
-theory
\operatorname{K}^{\mathrm{MW}}_{*}
. For each
X
below and each line bundle
\mathcal{L}
on
X
, put

v_{\mathcal{L}}=\mathcal{L}^{\vee}-\mathcal{O}_{X}.

Then the following assertions hold.

(1)

If
X\in\Sm_{S}
, then for every
n\in\mathbf{Z}
there are canonical
representability isomorphisms

\CH^{n}(X/S)\simeq H^{2n,n}\bigl(X,\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{M},\lambda}\bigr)

and

\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}{}^{n}(X/S,\mathcal{L})\simeq H^{2n,n}\bigl(\Th_{X}(v_{\mathcal{L}}),\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{MW},\lambda}\bigr).

(2)

If
X
is separated, essentially of finite type over
S
, and
Zariski-locally a closed subscheme of a smooth
S
-scheme, then there are
canonical Borel-Moore representability isomorphisms

\CH_{n}(X/S)\simeq H^{\operatorname{BM}}_{2n,n}\bigl(X/S,\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{M},\lambda}\bigr)

and

\widetilde{\operatorname{CH}}{}_{n}(X/S,\mathcal{L})\simeq H^{\operatorname{BM}}_{2n,n}\bigl(\Th_{X}(v_{\mathcal{L}})/S,\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{MW},\lambda}\bigr).

(3)

These groups are the diagonal pieces of higher Rost-Schmid cohomology
and Borel-Moore homology theories represented by the objects

\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{M},\lambda}
and
\mathbf{H}_{\mathrm{MW},\lambda}
. In particular, they
come with localization long exact sequences and the homotopy coherent lci
pullbacks supplied by Theorem
B
.

(4)

For the standard Rost-Schmid motivic complexes of
Notation
6.3.7
, including the Milnor and
Milnor-Witt examples above, one has a canonical extraordinary base-change
equivalence

f^{!}\mathbf{H}_{S}(\mathcal{M})\simeq\mathbf{H}_{T}(\mathcal{M})

for every separated finite type morphism
f:T\to S
.

The first two assertions are Theorem
6.3.5
; the
extraordinary base-change is
Proposition
6.3.8
.

The same framework also applies to Quillen
K^{\prime}
-theory (or Thomason

G
-theory), and to the hermitian
K
-theory when a
dualizing complex is available. This is parallel to the role of
G
-theory
in Borel-Moore motivic homology and weight structures studied by Jin in

[
Jin16
]
. Of course, representability in
{\Der_{\mathbb{A}^{1}}}(S,R)
implies representability
in motivic stable homotopy category
\mathrm{SH}(S)
.

Further directions
.
The construction is deliberately formulated so that it can be used beyond
Rost-Schmid complexes. In particular, it is intended to serve as a geometric
engine for future constructions of higher fundamental classes, and hence of
higher trace and cotrace maps, both in motivic homotopy theory and in
étale torsion or
\ell
-adic settings. The next steps in the surrounding
program are the completion of the attack on the Ayoub conjecture and the
application of these ideas to the niveau filtration on motivic spectra, in
connection with the homotopy coniveau methods of Levine.
A central application of the construction developed in this paper is a corrected
formulation and proof of the universal moving lemma of

[
DFJ23
]
.

Organization of the paper
.
Section
1
develops the geometry of higher
deformation spaces. It recalls their construction, proves the normal
crossings description of their ordered boundary, introduces specialization
flags, and studies confluences and the behavior of panels under simplicial
operators.

Section
2
introduces weak Borel-Moore theories and
constructs the higher Gysin simplex for a flag of closed immersions. The
construction uses localization cubes for ordered normal crossings divisors and
the inflation package.

Section
3
proves the face and degeneracy
relations for these higher Gysin simplices. The proof is geometric: faces are
controlled by specialization flags and
\mathbb{A}^{1}
-invariance, while degeneracies
are controlled by confluences.

Section
4
proves the abstract rigidification
theorem and applies it to obtain a strict simplicial closed-immersion pullback
theory. It also isolates the shared rigidification principle used later in the
gluing arguments.

Section
5
glues the closed-immersion theory with the
already available smooth contravariance. The graph factorization of simplices
in
N(\Sm_{S})
reduces the construction to the closed-immersion case plus
smooth pullback, and the resulting raw simplices are rigidified to give the
contravariant functor on
N(\Sm_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}
.

Section
6
applies the general construction to
Rost-Schmid complexes of MW-homodules. It proves that these complexes form
weak Borel-Moore theories, constructs the motivic object
\mathbf{H}(\mathcal{M})
,
proves representability of cohomological and Borel-Moore Rost-Schmid groups,
specializes this to Chow and Chow-Witt groups, and proves exceptional
base-change and absolute purity for the standard examples.

Length and verification of the proof
.
The length of the paper reflects the nature of the construction. The
argument is highly sensitive to variance, shifts, twists, endpoint
conventions, and the ordering of faces and degeneracies. We have therefore
chosen to write the technical verifications explicitly, rather than suppressing
them under general coherence statements. This is especially important in
Sections
2
-
5
, where the
construction passes from higher deformation spaces to closed-immersion Gysin
simplices, then to simplicial identities, rigidification, and finally to the
prismatic gluing with smooth pullbacks.

Use of AI-assisted tools
.
During the preparation of this paper, the authors used large language model
tools as interactive assistants for drafting, expanding, and checking parts
of the manuscript. Their use was not autonomous. The mathematical strategy,
definitions, statements, and final proofs are the responsibility of the
authors.

More specifically, AI-assisted tools were used in the following ways: to
expand human-provided proof sketches into detailed drafts; to produce
referee-style reports on the readability and internal consistency of the
text; to search for possible inconsistencies in notation, variance, endpoint
conventions, shifts, twists, signs, and cross-references; and to help
reorganize lengthy technical arguments. In each case, the output was read,
checked, corrected, and, when necessary, rejected or substantially rewritten
by the authors.
The workflow typically consisted of a human mathematical proposal, an
AI-assisted expansion or consistency check, human proof-reading and
correction, further AI-assisted auditing, and final human revision. This
process was particularly useful for detecting local inconsistencies in a long
coherence-sensitive manuscript, but it did not replace mathematical judgement
or proof.

The authors are aware of the risks attached to such tools, in particular the
possibility of producing plausible but incorrect mathematical text. For this
reason, AI-assisted output was used only as part of an iterative process of
human verification. No AI system is an author of this paper, and the authors
take full responsibility for all mathematical claims, proofs, references, and
formulations.

Conventions and notation

Unless stated otherwise,
S
denotes a qcqs base scheme. All schemes are
implicitly
S
-schemes, and all fiber products are taken over
S
unless
explicitly indicated. By a vector bundle we mean a locally free
\mathcal{O}
-module
of finite rank. In the application to Rost-Schmid complexes,
S
is assumed
noetherian excellent and equipped with a dimension function; additional
hypotheses such as a pinning or a dualizing complex are stated explicitly when
they are used.

We write
\Delta
for the simplex category and
[n]=\{0<\cdots<n\}
. For a
simplicial set
K
,
K_{n}
denotes the set of
n
-simplices. For an
ordinary category
\mathcal{D}
,
N(\mathcal{D})
denotes its
nerve. An

\infty
-category is regarded as a simplicial set in the quasi-categorical
model. Thus a homotopy coherent functor from
N(\mathcal{D})
to an

\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
means a map of simplicial sets

N(\mathcal{D})\to\mathcal{C}
, equivalently an object of
\Fun(N(\mathcal{D}),\mathcal{C})
. Faces and degeneracies are denoted by
d_{i}
and
s_{i}
. By a space
we mean an
\infty
-groupoid. For an
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
, we write

\Map_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)
for the mapping space. We use
\simeq
for equivalences in
an
\infty
-category and
\cong
for isomorphisms in an ordinary category.

It is intentionally limited to the notation needed to navigate the construction
and the applications.

1.
Higher deformation spaces

1.1.
Operators on parameters

Fix a base scheme
S
. Throughout, “smooth
S
-scheme” means smooth, separated, and of finite presentation.
Let
\Sm_{S}
denote the category of smooth
S
-schemes, and let
\Sm^{imm}_{S}
be the category whose
objects are smooth
S
-schemes and whose morphisms are closed immersions.
(Any closed immersion between smooth
S
-schemes is automatically a regular immersion, see
[
Sta25
, Tag 067U]
.)

Notations 1.1.1
.

For
n\geq 0
, we write

[n]:=\{0,1,\dots,n\},

endowed with its standard ordering. Thus

[n]\setminus\{0\}=\{1,\dots,n\}.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})
be a flag of closed immersions between
smooth

S
-schemes, in other words, this is an
n
-simplex in the nerve of the category
\Sm^{imm}_{S}
(of smooth
S
-schemes with closed immersions).

We want to define an
n
-fold deformation space

p_{\sigma}:D(\sigma)\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}

as in the multi-centered deformation-space formalism (cf.
[
DM24
]
),
together with the following properties (which we isolate as lemmas, since they are the only
geometric facts used below).

Definition 1.1.2

(Parameter operations on affine space)
.

For
n\geq 0
set

\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}:=\Spec_{S}(\mathcal{O}_{S}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1}]).

•

(
Panels
) For each
0\leq k\leq n-1
, we have a regular immersion
\iota_{k}:\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
given by:

\iota_{k}^{*}(t_{i})=\begin{cases}t_{i}&\text{if }i<k,\\
0&\text{if }i=k,\\
t_{i-1}&\text{if }i>k.\end{cases}

•

(
Confluences
)
To avoid index confusion, we temporarily write
u_{0},\dots,u_{n-1}
for coordinates on
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
and

t_{0},\dots,t_{n}
for those on
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
. For each
0\leq k\leq n
, we have a map
\mu_{k}:\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
defined by parameter confluence:

\mu_{k}^{*}(u_{i})=\begin{cases}t_{i}&\text{if }i<k,\\
t_{k}t_{k+1}&\text{if }i=k<n,\\
t_{i+1}&\text{if }i>k.\end{cases}

When
k=n
, the map
\mu_{n}
is the projection forgetting
t_{n}
, i.e.
\mu_{n}^{*}(u_{i})=t_{i}
for all
0\leq i\leq n-1
.

Since the base scheme
S
is fixed throughout, we will often abbreviate
\mathbb{A}^{n}:=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
.

Remark 1.1.3
.

We do
not
claim that the family
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
with the operators
\iota_{k},\mu_{k}

forms a cosimplicial scheme:
\iota_{k}
is a panel inclusion (
t_{k}=0
) whereas
\mu_{k}

is a confluence (
t_{k}t_{k+1}
), and the usual identities
\sigma^{k}\delta^{k}=\operatorname{id}
fail.
What we use later are only the explicit pullback formulas for coordinate divisors
(Lemma
1.1.4
) and the induced canonical identifications.

Lemma 1.1.4

(Pullback of coordinate divisors under confluence)
.

Let
\mu_{k}:\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
be as above and write
u_{0},\dots,u_{n-1}
for the
coordinates on
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
and
t_{0},\dots,t_{n}
for those on
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
.

Then for
0\leq i\leq n-1
one has

\mu_{k}^{-1}(\{u_{i}=0\})=\begin{cases}\{t_{i}=0\}&i<k,\\
\{t_{k}=0\}\cup\{t_{k+1}=0\}&i=k<n,\\
\{t_{i+1}=0\}&i>k,\end{cases}

and for
k=n
one has
\mu_{n}^{-1}(\{u_{i}=0\})=\{t_{i}=0\}
.
In particular, as effective Cartier divisors,
\mu_{k}^{*}(\{u_{k}=0\})=V(t_{k}t_{k+1})=\{t_{k}=0\}+\{t_{k+1}=0\}
for
k<n
.

Proof.

This is immediate from the explicit formulas for
\mu_{k}^{*}(u_{i})
in Definition
1.1.2
.
∎

1.2.
Multi-deformation spaces and their strata

Let

\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})

be a flag of (necessarily
regular
) closed immersions between
smooth

S
-schemes (so
\sigma
is an
n
-simplex
of the nerve
N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})
).

It is
degenerate
if and only if one of the closed immersions

Z_{k-1}\hookrightarrow Z_{k}
is an identity (equivalently,
Z_{k-1}=Z_{k}
for some
1\leq k\leq n
).

The purpose of this subsection is to
define
the higher deformation space
D(\sigma)
and to prove the
geometric properties we will later use (normal crossings boundary, open stratum, deepest stratum, panelization).

1.2.1.
Multi-centered deformation spaces (after Dubouloz-Mayeux)

We briefly recall the formalism from
[
DM24
]
in the special case we need.

Definition 1.2.2

(Deformation datum and multi-centered deformation space
[
DM24
, Def. 2.1, Prop. 2.4]
)
.

Let
X
be a scheme and let
(I,\leq)
be a finite totally ordered set. Write
\mathrm{Clo}(X)
for the poset of closed subschemes of
X
(ordered by inclusion).
A
deformation datum
on
X
indexed by
I
consists of:

•

a decreasing family
(X_{i})_{i\in I}
of closed subschemes of
X

(i.e.
i\leq j\Rightarrow X_{j}\subset X_{i}
), and

•

a family
(D_{i})_{i\in I}
of locally principal closed subschemes of
X
.

For
i\in I
put

D_{i}^{\prime}\ :=\ \sum_{j\in I,\ j\geq i}D_{j}\qquad\in\mathrm{Clo}(X),

where the sum is the product of ideal sheaves (as in
[
DM24
, §2]
).
Then one defines the
multi-centered deformation space

D\Bigl(\bigl((D_{i})_{i\in I},(X_{i})_{i\in I}\bigr);\,X\Bigr):=\mathrm{Bl}\Bigl(\bigl(D_{i}^{\prime}/X_{i}\bigr)_{i\in I};\,X\Bigr)

as the multi-centered dilatation of
X
with multi-center
\{[X_{i},D_{i}^{\prime}]\}_{i\in I}

in the sense of
[
DM24
, Def. 2.1]
. It is characterized by the universal property

[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4]
: for any morphism
f:T\to X
such that each
f^{-1}(D_{i})
is a Cartier divisor on
T
,
there exists
at most one

X
-morphism
T\to D(\cdots)
, and it exists if and only if

f^{-1}(D_{i}^{\prime})\ \subset\ f^{-1}(X_{i})\quad\text{in}\quad\mathrm{Clo}(T)\qquad\text{for all }i\in I.

1.2.3.
Definition of
D(\sigma)
as an
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
-deformation

Set

\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}=\Spec_{S}\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{S}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1}]\bigr),\qquad X:=Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}.

For
0\leq k\leq n-1
let
C_{k}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
be the coordinate Cartier divisor
\{t_{k}=0\}
and set

\widetilde{D}_{k}:=Z_{n}\times_{S}C_{k}\ \subset\ X.

For
K\subset[n-1]
, set

C_{K}:=\bigcap_{k\in K}C_{k}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}.

For
i\in[n]\setminus\{0\}
, define

X_{i}:=Z_{n-i}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\subset X,\qquad D_{i}:=\widetilde{D}_{\,n-i}\subset X.

Thus
X_{1}\supset X_{2}\supset\cdots\supset X_{n}
is decreasing, and

D_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\geq i}D_{j}

is the pullback of the monomial divisor
\{t_{0}\cdots t_{n-i}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
.

Definition 1.2.4

(Higher deformation space attached to a flag)
.

The
higher deformation space
of

\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})

is the multi-centered deformation space

D(\sigma)\ :=\ D\Bigl(\bigl((D_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n},(X_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}\bigr);\ X\Bigr)

of Definition
1.2.2
. We denote by

\rho_{\sigma}:D(\sigma)\to X

its structure morphism, and we set

p_{\sigma}:=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}\circ\rho_{\sigma}:\ D(\sigma)\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S},\qquad q_{\sigma}:=\operatorname{pr}_{Z_{n}}\circ\rho_{\sigma}:\ D(\sigma)\to Z_{n}.

Equivalently, letting
\mathcal{I}_{i}\subset\mathcal{O}_{Z_{n}}
be the ideal sheaf of
Z_{i}\subset Z_{n}

for
0\leq i\leq n-1
, and still denoting by
\mathcal{I}_{i}
its pullback to

X=Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
, the scheme
D(\sigma)
is the affine
X
-scheme associated with the
quasi-coherent
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-subalgebra

\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}:=\sum_{a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in\mathbf{Z}}\mathcal{I}_{n-1}^{a_{n-1}}\mathcal{I}_{n-2}^{a_{n-2}-a_{n-1}}\cdots\mathcal{I}_{0}^{a_{0}-a_{1}}\,t_{0}^{-a_{0}}\cdots t_{n-1}^{-a_{n-1}}\;\subset\;\mathcal{O}_{X}[t_{0}^{\pm 1},\dots,t_{n-1}^{\pm 1}],

where, by convention,
\mathcal{J}^{m}=\mathcal{O}_{X}
for
m\leq 0
.
In other words,

D(\sigma)=\Spec_{X}(\mathcal{R}_{\sigma}).

This is the explicit multi-Rees-algebra model underlying the multi-dilatation formalism.

Remark 1.2.5
.

This scheme over
Z_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
could also be defined by the formula

\operatorname{Bl}^{(Z_{n}\times D_{t_{0}},Z_{n}\times D_{t_{0}t_{1}},\dots,Z_{n}\times D_{t_{0}t_{1}\cdots t_{n-1}})}_{(Z_{j}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S})_{0\leq j\leq n-1}}(Z_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S})

where we use the notation of
[
May23
, Section 9]
and
[
DMdS23
, Section 1]
.

Lemma 1.2.6
.

In the situation of Definition
1.2.4
, each
\widetilde{D}_{k}\subset X
is an effective Cartier divisor. Hence each
D_{i}
and each

D_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\geq i}D_{j}

is an effective Cartier divisor on
X
.

Moreover, for every morphism
T\to S
, if
\sigma_{T}
denotes the base-changed flag

(Z_{0}\times_{S}T\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\times_{S}T\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n}\times_{S}T)

and
D(\sigma_{T})
the multi-centered deformation space attached to the induced deformation datum on

X_{T}:=(Z_{n}\times_{S}T)\times_{T}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{T}\simeq X\times_{S}T,

then there is a canonical isomorphism

D(\sigma)\times_{S}T\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }D(\sigma_{T}).

Proof.

Each
C_{k}=\{t_{k}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
is an effective Cartier divisor, hence so is its pullback

\widetilde{D}_{k}=Z_{n}\times_{S}C_{k}\subset X
. Products of invertible ideal sheaves are invertible, so each

D_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\geq i}D_{j}
is again an effective Cartier divisor.

Let
T\to S
. The pullback of the deformation datum
(X_{i},D_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
along

X_{T}=X\times_{S}T\to X
is exactly the deformation datum defining
D(\sigma_{T})
. Since the
D_{i}
(equivalently the
D_{i}^{\prime}
) are Cartier, the universal property of multi-centered dilatations (cf.
[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4]
) yields a canonical isomorphism

D(\sigma)\times_{X}X_{T}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }D(\sigma_{T}).

Finally
D(\sigma)\times_{X}X_{T}\cong D(\sigma)\times_{S}T
, because
X_{T}=X\times_{S}T
.
∎

Remark 1.2.7

(Low-dimensional cases:
n=1
and
n=2
)
.

(1)

n=1
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1})
and write
t=t_{0}
for the coordinate on
\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
.
Then
D(\sigma)\to Z_{1}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
is the usual deformation to the normal cone of
Z_{0}
in
Z_{1}

(e.g.
[
Ful98
, Chap. 5]
), and it agrees with the affine-modification/dilatation description
(
[
DM24
, §6]
).
Over
U=\mathbb{G}_{m}=\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\setminus\{0\}
one has the canonical identification

D(\sigma)|_{U}\ \simeq\ Z_{1}\times_{S}U\qquad\text{(cf.\ Lemma\penalty\ \ref{lemma:Dsigma-generic}).}

The unique boundary divisor is
H_{0}=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(0)
, and restricting along
t=0
yields

H_{0}\ \simeq\ C_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}.

Since
Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}
is a regular immersion (objects are smooth over
S
), the normal cone is a vector bundle and

C_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\ \simeq\ N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}.

(2)

n=2
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow Z_{2})
and write
(t_{0},t_{1})
for the coordinates on
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
.
Then
D(\sigma)
is Rost’s double deformation space
D(Z_{2};Z_{1};Z_{0})
(
[
Ros96
, §10]
),
as recovered by the
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-deformation formalism of
[
DM24
, §6]
.
It is flat over
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
, and over
(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}\subset\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
one has

D(\sigma)|_{(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}}\ \simeq\ Z_{2}\times_{S}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}\qquad\text{(by iterating the $n=1$ generic-fiber identification).}

The boundary is the strict normal crossings divisor

H=H_{0}\cup H_{1},\qquad H_{k}=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(\{t_{k}=0\}),

and the strata
H_{0}
,
H_{1}
, and
H_{\{0,1\}}=H_{0}\cap H_{1}
admit canonical descriptions
in the smooth setting (see
[
DM24
, Thm. 6.3, Prop. 6.5]
).

Rost’s restriction identities.

To match Rost’s convention in
[
Ros96
, 10.0.1-10.0.5]
, it is convenient to relabel parameters by

(s,t):=(t_{1},t_{0}).

Assuming
Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow Z_{2}
are regular immersions (automatic in
\Sm_{S}
),
Rost proves canonical identifications of schemes over
Z_{2}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
:

(Rost 10.0.1)

\displaystyle D(\sigma)\big|_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{s}\times(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{t}\setminus\{0\})}

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ D(Z_{2};Z_{1})\times(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{t}\setminus\{0\}),

(Rost 10.0.2)

\displaystyle D(\sigma)\big|_{(\mathbb{A}^{1}_{s}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{t}}

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ (\mathbb{A}^{1}_{s}\setminus\{0\})\times D(Z_{2};Z_{0}),

(Rost 10.0.3)

\displaystyle D(\sigma)\big|_{\mathbb{A}^{1}_{s}\times\{0\}}

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ D\!\bigl(N_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}};\,N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\bigr),

(Rost 10.0.4)

\displaystyle D(\sigma)\big|_{\{0\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{t}}

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ D\!\bigl(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}};\,(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}})|_{Z_{0}}\bigr).

Moreover, the projection
D(\sigma)|_{(0,0)}\to Z_{2}
factors through
Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{2}
, and one has an identification
of schemes over
Z_{0}
:

(Rost 10.0.5)

D(\sigma)\big|_{(0,0)}=\ N\!\bigl(N_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}};\,N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\bigr)\ =\ N\!\bigl(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}};\,(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}})|_{Z_{0}}\bigr),

so in particular the deepest stratum
H_{\{0,1\}}
is a vector bundle over
Z_{0}
.
These identities agree with the stratum computations in
[
DM24
, §6.2.1]
and recover Rost’s explicit
description of the boundary and its strata
[
Ros96
, 10.0.3-10.0.5]
.

1.3.
Panels vs simplicial faces: specialization flags and panel restriction

The Rost-Dubouloz-Mayeux multi-deformation space
D(\sigma)\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}

will be shown to have a strict normal crossings boundary
H=\bigcup_{k=0}^{n-1}H_{k}
with

H_{k}=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(\{t_{k}=0\})
.
However,
restricting to a coordinate panel

\{t_{k}=0\}
does
not

recover the simplicial face
d_{k}\sigma
in general: already for
n=2
one
gets the deformation space of a
specialized (normal-cone) flag
.

Notations 1.3.1

(Normal sheaf and its total space)
.

If
X
is a scheme and
\mathcal{E}
is a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module of finite rank, we write

\mathbf{V}_{X}(\mathcal{E}):=\Spec_{X}\!\bigl(\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\bigr)

for its total space. When the base is clear, we simply write
\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{E})
.

If
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
is a regular immersion with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{I}
, we set

\mathcal{N}_{Z/X}:=(\mathcal{I}/\mathcal{I}^{2})^{\vee},\qquad N_{Z/X}:=\mathbf{V}_{Z}(\mathcal{N}_{Z/X}).

Thus
\mathcal{N}_{Z/X}
is the normal sheaf, whereas
N_{Z/X}
is its total space.

Lemma 1.3.2

(Normal sheaves along a regular flag)
.

Let
Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{j}\hookrightarrow Z_{j+1}
be composable regular immersions between smooth
S
-schemes.
Then there is a canonical short exact sequence of locally free
\mathcal{O}_{Z_{k}}
-modules

0\to\mathcal{N}_{Z_{k}/Z_{j}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{N}_{Z_{k}/Z_{j+1}}\longrightarrow(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{j}/Z_{j+1}})|_{Z_{k}}\to 0.

In particular, after applying
\mathbf{V}_{Z_{k}}(-)
, one gets a canonical closed immersion

N_{Z_{k}/Z_{j}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{k}/Z_{j+1}}.

Proof.

By
[
Sta25
, Tag 0638, Tag 063N]
, for composable immersions

Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{j}\hookrightarrow Z_{j+1},

with
Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{j}

H_{1}
-regular (hence regular in our setting), the conormal sequence is short exact:

0\to\mathcal{I}_{Z_{k}/Z_{j}}/\mathcal{I}_{Z_{k}/Z_{j}}^{2}\to\mathcal{I}_{Z_{k}/Z_{j+1}}/\mathcal{I}_{Z_{k}/Z_{j+1}}^{2}\to(\mathcal{I}_{Z_{j}/Z_{j+1}}/\mathcal{I}_{Z_{j}/Z_{j+1}}^{2})|_{Z_{k}}\to 0.

Dualizing gives the displayed short exact sequence of normal sheaves. Since
\mathbf{V}
sends a monomorphism
of locally free sheaves to a closed immersion of total spaces, the last assertion follows.
∎

Definition 1.3.3

(Specialized flag at a vertex)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})
be a
flag of regular closed immersions between smooth
S
-schemes, and fix

0\leq k\leq n-1
.

For
k+1\leq j\leq n
, write
N_{k,j}:=N_{Z_{k}/Z_{j}}
. Since the composite immersion
Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{j+1}
is regular,

N_{k,j+1}
is the total space of a vector bundle on
Z_{k}
, and there are canonical closed immersions

N_{k,j}\hookrightarrow N_{k,j+1}\qquad(j\geq k+1)

coming from functoriality of normal cones for composable regular immersions.

For
0\leq j\leq k
, write

N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{j}}:=Z_{j}\times_{Z_{k}}N_{k,k+1}.

Define the specialized flag
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n-1}
by

{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma):=\bigl(N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{k}}=N_{k,k+1}\hookrightarrow N_{k,k+2}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow N_{k,n}\bigr).

Equivalently, the first part of
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)
is obtained by restricting the bundle

N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+1}}
along the chain
Z_{0}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{k}
,
while the second part is obtained from the canonical inclusions

N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+1}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+2}}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{k}/Z_{n}}
.

Remark 1.3.4
.

If some step
Z_{i}\hookrightarrow Z_{i+1}
is the identity, then we have
\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}}=0
, so the corresponding terms in
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)
may be degenerate.

Remark 1.3.5

(The Dubouloz-Mayeux
V
-object in our convention)
.

Let
[Y,D]
be a mono-center in a scheme
X
, and put
Z:=Y\cap D
.
Under the regularity hypotheses of
[
DM24
, Def. 1.1]
, Dubouloz-Mayeux define a scheme

V\!\left(\frac{D}{Y,X}\right)

canonically isomorphic to

\operatorname{Bl}^{D}_{Y}(X)\times_{X}D.

Their notation
V(-)
uses the convention of
[
DM24
]
. With our convention

\mathbf{V}_{X}(\mathcal{E}):=\Spec_{X}\!\bigl(\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{E}^{\vee})\bigr),

this scheme is

V\!\left(\frac{D}{Y,X}\right)\cong\mathbf{V}_{Z}\!\left((\mathcal{C}_{Z/D}\otimes J_{Z}^{-1})^{\vee}\right),

where
\mathcal{C}_{Z/D}:=\mathcal{I}_{Z/D}/\mathcal{I}_{Z/D}^{2}
is the conormal sheaf of

Z\hookrightarrow D
, and
J_{Z}:=J|_{Z}
for the ideal
J\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
of
D\subset X
.

In particular, if
J_{Z}
is trivial, then

V\!\left(\frac{D}{Y,X}\right)\cong N_{Z/D}.

In the codimension-one stratum formula of
[
DM24
, Prop. 6.5]
, after fixing an index
s
,
one encounters the schemes

V_{i}:=V\!\left(\frac{D_{s}}{X_{s},X_{i}}\right)\qquad(0\leq i\leq s-1),

all naturally defined over
X_{s}\cap D_{s}
. In our smooth flag situation these identify with the
corresponding normal bundles.

Notations 1.3.6

(Boundary components)
.

For
0\leq k\leq n-1
, set

H_{k}(\sigma):=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(C_{k})=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(\{t_{k}=0\})\subset D(\sigma),

and for
K\subset[n-1]
set

H_{K}(\sigma):=\bigcap_{k\in K}H_{k}(\sigma),\qquad H_{\varnothing}(\sigma):=D(\sigma).

Lemma 1.3.7

(Panel restriction is a specialization)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}
and fix
0\leq k\leq n-1
.
Let
\iota_{k}:\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
be the coordinate panel embedding cutting out
C_{k}=\{t_{k}=0\}
.
Then there is a canonical isomorphism of
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
-schemes

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}:\ \iota_{k}^{*}D(\sigma)\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)).

Moreover, under
\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}
:

•

the scheme-theoretic inverse image
\iota_{k}^{*}H_{k}(\sigma)
is the whole space
\iota_{k}^{*}D(\sigma)
;

•

for
j\neq k
the remaining boundary components identify by the index shift

\iota_{k}^{*}H_{j}(\sigma)\ \cong\ \begin{cases}H_{j}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))&j<k,\\
H_{j-1}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))&j>k.\end{cases}

Equivalently, for every
K\subset\{0,\dots,n-1\}\setminus\{k\}
one has a canonical identification of strata

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}:\ H_{K\cup\{k\}}(\sigma)\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ H_{\phi_{k}(K)}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)),\qquad\phi_{k}(j)=\begin{cases}j&j<k,\\
j-1&j>k.\end{cases}

Proof.

Write

s:=n-k,\qquad X_{0}:=X=Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S},\qquad D_{s}=\widetilde{D}_{k}=Z_{n}\times_{S}C_{k}.

Since
\iota_{k}:\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{k}
, we have

\iota_{k}^{*}D(\sigma)=D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}C_{k}=D(\sigma)\times_{X}D_{s}.

By definition of the boundary components,

H_{k}(\sigma)=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(\{t_{k}=0\})=D(\sigma)\times_{X}D_{s},

so
\iota_{k}^{*}D(\sigma)
is the codimension-one stratum lying over the panel
C_{k}
.

We now apply
[
DM24
, Thm. 6.3 and Prop. 6.5]
to the singleton
\Sigma=\{s\}
.
This yields a canonical isomorphism

D(\sigma)\times_{X}D_{s}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }D(\sigma^{\mathrm{pan}}),

where
\sigma^{\mathrm{pan}}
is the codimension-one panelized deformation datum over the ambient scheme

V_{0}:=V\!\left(\frac{D_{s}}{X_{s},X_{0}}\right).

More precisely, for
0\leq i\leq s-1
set

V_{i}:=V\!\left(\frac{D_{s}}{X_{s},X_{i}}\right).

Then Proposition 6.5 of
[
DM24
]
gives the chain

\sigma^{\mathrm{pan}}=\Bigl((X_{n}\cap D_{s})\times_{X_{s}\cap D_{s}}V_{s-1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow(X_{s+1}\cap D_{s})\times_{X_{s}\cap D_{s}}V_{s-1}\hookrightarrow V_{s-1}\hookrightarrow V_{s-2}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow V_{0}\Bigr),

where the last arrows are the canonical closed immersions between the
V
-objects.
In other words, the “
V
-pieces” are precisely the terms

V_{s-1},V_{s-2},\dots,V_{0}=V\!\left(\frac{D_{s}}{X_{s},X_{s-1}}\right),V\!\left(\frac{D_{s}}{X_{s},X_{s-2}}\right),\dots,V\!\left(\frac{D_{s}}{X_{s},X_{0}}\right).

We now translate these terms into our notation.

Since

X_{i}=Z_{n-i}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S},\qquad X_{s}=Z_{k}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S},\qquad D_{s}=Z_{n}\times_{S}C_{k},

we have, for every
0\leq i\leq n
,

X_{i}\cap D_{s}=Z_{n-i}\times_{S}C_{k}.

In particular,

X_{s}\cap D_{s}=Z_{k}\times_{S}C_{k}.

The ideal
J\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
of
D_{s}\subset X
is generated by the global equation
t_{k}
.
Hence its restriction
J|_{X_{s}\cap D_{s}}
is canonically trivial. Therefore, by
Remark
1.3.5
,

V_{i}\cong\mathbf{V}\!\left(\mathcal{N}_{(X_{s}\cap D_{s})/(X_{i}\cap D_{s})}\right).

Since

X_{s}\cap D_{s}=Z_{k}\times_{S}C_{k},\qquad X_{i}\cap D_{s}=Z_{n-i}\times_{S}C_{k},

and normal sheaves commute with flat base change, we get

\mathcal{N}_{(X_{s}\cap D_{s})/(X_{i}\cap D_{s})}\cong\operatorname{pr}_{1}^{*}\mathcal{N}_{Z_{k}/Z_{n-i}},

hence

V_{i}\cong N_{Z_{k}/Z_{n-i}}\times_{S}C_{k}.

In particular,

V_{s-1}\cong N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+1}}\times_{S}C_{k},\qquad V_{s-2}\cong N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+2}}\times_{S}C_{k},\qquad\dots,\qquad V_{0}\cong N_{Z_{k}/Z_{n}}\times_{S}C_{k}.

Under the identification of
V_{s-1}
with
N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+1}}\times_{S}C_{k}
, the earlier terms become

(X_{i}\cap D_{s})\times_{X_{s}\cap D_{s}}V_{s-1}\cong(Z_{n-i}\times_{S}C_{k})\times_{(Z_{k}\times_{S}C_{k})}(N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+1}}\times_{S}C_{k})\cong N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+1}}|_{Z_{n-i}}\times_{S}C_{k}\qquad(i>s).

Therefore the whole chain
\sigma^{\mathrm{pan}}
identifies with

\Bigl(N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+1}}|_{Z_{0}}\times_{S}C_{k}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+1}}|_{Z_{k}}\times_{S}C_{k}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+2}}\times_{S}C_{k}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{k}/Z_{n}}\times_{S}C_{k}\Bigr).

Via
\iota_{k}:\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\xrightarrow{\sim}C_{k}
, this is exactly the specialized flag

{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)
of Definition
1.3.3
. Hence we obtain the claimed canonical
isomorphism

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}:\ \iota_{k}^{*}D(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)).

Finally, the description of boundary components is tautological on the base.
On the panel
C_{k}=\{t_{k}=0\}
, the remaining coordinate hyperplanes are given by

t_{j}=0
for
j<k
and by
t_{j}=0
for
j>k
, with the coordinate
t_{k}
omitted.
Hence under
\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}
one has

\iota_{k}^{*}H_{j}(\sigma)\cong\begin{cases}H_{j}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))&j<k,\\
H_{j-1}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))&j>k.\end{cases}

Intersecting these boundary components gives the equivalent stratum formula for

H_{K\cup\{k\}}(\sigma)
.
∎

Example 1.3.8

(Sanity check in Rost’s double deformation space (
n=2
))
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow Z_{2})
and write, in Rost’s notation,

X:=Z_{2},\qquad Y:=Z_{1},\qquad Z:=Z_{0},\qquad D(\sigma)=D(X;Y;Z)\xrightarrow{\,p_{\sigma}\,}\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}

with coordinates
(t_{0},t_{1})
as in the present paper. Assume for simplicity that the immersions are regular, so all
normal cones are vector bundles.

Panels and specialized flags.

By Lemma
1.3.7
, restriction of
D(\sigma)
to the coordinate panel
\{t_{k}=0\}

is canonically identified with the (single) deformation space attached to the specialized flag
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)

(Definition
1.3.3
). In the present case:

(1)

The panel
\{t_{0}=0\}
.

Here
k=0
and
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{0}(\sigma)=(N_{Z/Y}\hookrightarrow N_{Z/X})
, i.e.

{\operatorname{Sp}}_{0}(\sigma)=\bigl(N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}}\bigr).

Therefore

D(\sigma)\big|_{\{t_{0}=0\}}\ \simeq\ D\bigl(N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}}\bigr),

which is exactly Rost’s identification

D(X;Y;Z)\big|_{\mathbb{A}^{1}\times\{0\}}\ \simeq\ D\bigl(N_{Z/X};\,N_{Z/Y}\bigr)\qquad\text{(\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Rost96}{}{}, (10.0.3)]}).}

In particular, the special fiber over
(t_{0},t_{1})=(0,0)
computed along this panel is the normal bundle

N_{N_{Z/Y}/N_{Z/X}}
, i.e. a vector bundle over
N_{Z/Y}
.

Since the short exact sequence of normal sheaves

0\to\mathcal{N}_{Z/Y}\to\mathcal{N}_{Z/X}\to(\mathcal{N}_{Y/X})|_{Z}\to 0

identifies
\mathcal{N}_{Z/Y}
with a subbundle of
\mathcal{N}_{Z/X}
, one has canonically

N_{N_{Z/Y}/N_{Z/X}}\;\cong\;\mathbf{V}_{N_{Z/Y}}\!\bigl(\operatorname{pr}_{N_{Z/Y}}^{*}((\mathcal{N}_{Y/X})|_{Z})\bigr)\;\cong\;N_{Z/Y}\times_{Z}(N_{Y/X})|_{Z},

where
\operatorname{pr}_{N_{Z/Y}}:N_{Z/Y}\to Z
is the bundle projection.

(2)

The panel
\{t_{1}=0\}
.

Here
k=1
and
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{1}(\sigma)=(N_{Y/X}|_{Z}\hookrightarrow N_{Y/X})
, i.e.

{\operatorname{Sp}}_{1}(\sigma)=\bigl(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}}\big|_{Z_{0}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}}\bigr).

Thus

D(\sigma)\big|_{\{t_{1}=0\}}\ \simeq\ D\bigl(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}}|_{Z_{0}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}}\bigr),

agreeing with Rost’s second axis description

D(X;Y;Z)\big|_{\{0\}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}}\ \simeq\ D\bigl(N_{Y/X};\,N_{Y/X}\big|_{Z}\bigr)\qquad\text{(\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Rost96}{}{}, (10.0.4)]}).}

The origin
(0,0)
and Rost’s deepest fiber.

Combining the two descriptions above yields the expected identification of the fiber over
(0,0)
as a vector bundle
over
Z
:

D(\sigma)\big|_{\{(0,0)\}}\ \simeq\ N\bigl(N_{Z/X};\,N_{Z/Y}\bigr)\ \simeq\ N\bigl(N_{Y/X};\,N_{Y/X}|_{Z}\bigr)\qquad\text{(\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{Rost96}{}{}, (10.0.5)]}).}

This also matches the “deepest stratum is a vector bundle over
Z_{0}
” description in the
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-deformation formalism
(compare
[
DM24
, §6.2.1]
).

Why the panel is not
D(d_{1}\sigma)
.

Note that
d_{1}\sigma=(Z\hookrightarrow X)
, hence
D(d_{1}\sigma)
is the usual deformation space for
Z\subset X
,
with special fiber
N_{Z/X}
. By contrast, restricting
D(\sigma)
to (say)
\{t_{0}=0\}
produces a deformation space
for the
inclusion of bundles

N_{Z/Y}\subset N_{Z/X}
, whose special fiber is

N_{N_{Z/Y}/N_{Z/X}}\simeq N_{Z/Y}\times_{Z}(N_{Y/X})|_{Z}
. Thus, unless
N_{Z/Y}=0
(or
Y=X
), one should not expect an isomorphism

D(\sigma)|_{\{t_{0}=0\}}\simeq D(d_{1}\sigma)
: the intermediate embedding
Z\subset Y\subset X
is remembered on the panel.

Lemma 1.3.9

(Iterated panelization computes all strata)
.

Let
\sigma\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}
and let
K=\{k_{1}<\cdots<k_{r}\}\subset[n-1]:=\{0,\dots,n-1\}
be a subset of cardinality
r
.
Write

C_{K}:=\bigcap_{k\in K}\{t_{k}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S},\qquad\iota_{K}:\mathbb{A}^{n-r}_{S}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }C_{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}

for the canonical closed immersion (induced by the quotient

\mathcal{O}_{S}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1}]\twoheadrightarrow\mathcal{O}_{S}[t_{i}]_{i\notin K}
).
Then there is a canonical isomorphism of
\mathbb{A}^{n-r}_{S}
-schemes

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{K,\sigma}:\ \iota_{K}^{*}D(\sigma)\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{K}(\sigma)),

where
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{K}(\sigma):={\operatorname{Sp}}_{k_{r}}\cdots{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k_{1}}(\sigma)\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n-r}
denotes the iterated specialization along
K
.

Moreover, for every inclusion
K^{\prime}\subset K
the identifications
\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{K,\sigma}

are compatible with the restriction maps between strata induced by
C_{K}\hookrightarrow C_{K^{\prime}}
.

Proof.

By definition,

\iota_{K}^{*}D(\sigma)=D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}C_{K}=H_{K}(\sigma):=\bigcap_{k\in K}H_{k}(\sigma).

Step 1: iterated specialization.

Write
K=\{k_{1}<\cdots<k_{r}\}
and set
\sigma^{(0)}:=\sigma
.
Inductively, having defined
\sigma^{(j-1)}\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n-(j-1)}
, let

\bar{k}_{j}:=k_{j}-(j-1)\in\{0,\dots,n-j\}

(the position of
k_{j}
after removing
\{k_{1},\dots,k_{j-1}\}
), and set

\sigma^{(j)}:={\operatorname{Sp}}_{\bar{k}_{j}}\bigl(\sigma^{(j-1)}\bigr)\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n-j}.

Define
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{K}(\sigma):=\sigma^{(r)}
. This agrees with the shorthand

{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k_{r}}\cdots{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k_{1}}(\sigma)
with the standard index shift.

Step 2: existence of
\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{K,\sigma}
by induction on
r=|K|
.

For
r=0
the claim is tautological.
For
r=1
it is Lemma
1.3.7
.
Assume
r\geq 2
and write
K^{\prime}=K\setminus\{k_{r}\}
.
Inside
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
one has
C_{K}=C_{K^{\prime}}\cap\{t_{k_{r}}=0\}
, hence by associativity of fiber products

\iota_{K}^{*}D(\sigma)\ \cong\ \iota_{\bar{k}_{r}}^{*}\bigl(\iota_{K^{\prime}}^{*}D(\sigma)\bigr),

where
\iota_{\bar{k}_{r}}:\mathbb{A}^{n-r}_{S}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n-(r-1)}_{S}\simeq C_{K^{\prime}}
is the panel embedding
corresponding to the coordinate
t_{k_{r}}
on
C_{K^{\prime}}
.
By the induction hypothesis we have

\iota_{K^{\prime}}^{*}D(\sigma)\simeq D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{K^{\prime}}(\sigma))
, and applying
Lemma
1.3.7
to the simplex
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{K^{\prime}}(\sigma)
and the index
\bar{k}_{r}

yields

\iota_{\bar{k}_{r}}^{*}D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{K^{\prime}}(\sigma))\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{\bar{k}_{r}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{K^{\prime}}(\sigma)))=D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{K}(\sigma)).

Composing gives
\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{K,\sigma}
.

Step 3: compatibility for
K^{\prime}\subset K
.

For
K^{\prime}\subset K
the inclusion
C_{K}\hookrightarrow C_{K^{\prime}}
induces the restriction map

\operatorname{res}_{K,K^{\prime}}:\iota_{K}^{*}D(\sigma)\to\iota_{K^{\prime}}^{*}D(\sigma)
.
On the specialization side,
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{K}(\sigma)
is obtained from
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{K^{\prime}}(\sigma)
by further specializations
along
K\setminus K^{\prime}
(with the same shift rule), hence

D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{K}(\sigma))
is the corresponding iterated panel restriction of
D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{K^{\prime}}(\sigma))
.
Since
\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{K,\sigma}
is built by iterating (a) associativity of base change and
(b) the canonical panelization isomorphisms of Lemma
1.3.7
,
it is compatible with these restriction maps.
∎

Remark 1.3.10
.

We always interpret
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{K}(\sigma)
using the
increasing
ordering
K=\{k_{1}<\cdots<k_{r}\}
and the
index-shift convention of the proof. We do not assert here that other orderings give canonically identical flags
(as simplices of
N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})
); what is canonical for our purposes is the induced identification

\iota_{K}^{*}D(\sigma)\simeq D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{K}(\sigma))
coming from iterated base change.

Remark 1.3.11

(Warning: coordinate panels are not simplicial faces)
.

Even though
\iota_{k}:\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\simeq\{t_{k}=0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
is the usual
coordinate-face embedding, the induced restriction

\iota_{k}^{*}D(\sigma)

corresponds to the specialization
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)
, not to the simplicial face
d_{k}\sigma
.
Consequently, the assignment

\sigma\longmapsto D(\sigma)

is not a simplicial object in schemes.

The geometric replacement for the missing simplicial face is the comparison morphism

\Pi:D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow D(d_{k}\sigma)

of Lemma
1.4.16
, together with the deepest-stratum description of
Lemma
1.4.14
and the terminal-face identification of
Lemma
1.5.5
. These are the geometric inputs used later
to recover the simplicial face identities after applying the Gysin formalism.

Lemma 1.3.12

(Compatibility of panel identification with structure maps)
.

With notation as above, the panel identification

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{r,\sigma}:H_{r}(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\sim}D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{r}(\sigma))

is compatible with the structure maps to
Z_{n}
in the sense that the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{r}(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{r}(\sigma))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{r,\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces q_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{r,n}\circ p_{r,n}\circ q_{{\operatorname{Sp}}_{r}(\sigma)}}

commutes, where
p_{r,n}:N_{Z_{r}/Z_{n}}\to Z_{r}
is the vector bundle projection and
i_{r,n}:Z_{r}\hookrightarrow Z_{n}
is the closed immersion.

Proof.

In the proof of Lemma
1.3.7
, the isomorphism

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{r,\sigma}:H_{r}(\sigma)=D(\sigma)\times_{X}D_{n-r}\xrightarrow{\sim}D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{r}(\sigma))

was obtained by identifying the codimension-one stratum over

D_{n-r}=Z_{n}\times_{S}C_{r}

with the higher deformation space whose ambient scheme is

N_{Z_{r}/Z_{n}}\times_{S}C_{r}.

Under this identification, the restriction of the structural morphism

\rho_{\sigma}|_{H_{r}(\sigma)}:H_{r}(\sigma)\to Z_{n}\times_{S}C_{r}

is exactly the composite

D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{r}(\sigma))\xrightarrow{\ \rho_{{\operatorname{Sp}}_{r}(\sigma)}\ }N_{Z_{r}/Z_{n}}\times_{S}C_{r}\xrightarrow{\ (i_{r,n}\circ p_{r,n})\times\operatorname{id}_{C_{r}}\ }Z_{n}\times_{S}C_{r}.

Composing with the projection to
Z_{n}
yields

q_{\sigma}|_{H_{r}(\sigma)}=i_{r,n}\circ p_{r,n}\circ q_{{\operatorname{Sp}}_{r}(\sigma)}\circ\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{r,\sigma},

which is the claimed commutative diagram.
∎

Remark 1.3.13

(Dictionary of indices)
.

In this section we use three parallel index conventions; we record them once to avoid later reindexing overhead.

•

Simplicial/panel index.

For an
n
-simplex
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})
we label the
n
deformation parameters by

t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1}
on
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
.
The corresponding boundary components are

H_{k}(\sigma):=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(\{t_{k}=0\})\qquad(0\leq k\leq n-1),

and “the
k
-th panel” means restriction along
\iota_{k}:\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\simeq\{t_{k}=0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
.

•

Dubouloz-Mayeux deformation-datum index.

In the deformation datum
\bigl((X_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n},(D_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}\bigr)
we use indices

i=1,\dots,n
as in
[
DM24
]
, and our conventions are
reversed
relative to the coordinates:

X_{i}=Z_{n-i}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S},\qquad D_{i}=\widetilde{D}_{\,n-i}=Z_{n}\times_{S}\{t_{n-i}=0\}.

Equivalently, the correspondence between the two indices is

i=n-k\qquad\Longleftrightarrow\qquad k=n-i.

(Thus
k=0
corresponds to
i=n
, while
k=n-1
corresponds to
i=1
.)

•

Specialization index.

Restriction to the panel
\{t_{k}=0\}
corresponds to “specialization at the vertex
k
”:

\iota_{k}^{*}D(\sigma)\ \simeq\ D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)),

where
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)
is the specialized flag (Definition
1.3.3
).
More generally, for
K\subset[n-1]
, the stratum
H_{K}(\sigma)=\bigcap_{k\in K}H_{k}(\sigma)
corresponds
to iterated specialization along the indices in
K
(Lemma
1.3.9
).

1.4.
Boundary geometry: ordered SNC pairs, open stratum, deepest stratum

Definition 1.4.1

(Ordered SNC pair and the strata cube)
.

An
ordered strict normal crossings (SNC) pair over
S
of length
n
is a pair
(X,H_{\bullet})
where
X
is an
S
-scheme and

H_{\bullet}=(H_{0},\dots,H_{n-1})
is an ordered list of
effective Cartier divisors on
X
such that for every subset
K\subset\{0,\dots,n-1\}
the scheme-theoretic intersection in
X

H_{K}:=\bigcap_{i\in K}H_{i}\qquad(H_{\varnothing}:=X)

is either empty or smooth over
S
, and has codimension
|K|
in
X
.

We denote by

\operatorname{Loc}(X,H_{\bullet}):\mathcal{P}([n-1])^{\operatorname{op}}\longrightarrow\operatorname{Sch}_{S}

the
n
-cube of schemes
K\mapsto H_{K}
, where the maps are the natural closed immersions.

Recall from Notation
1.3.6
that, for the deformation space
D(\sigma)
, we define the Cartier divisors

H_{k}(\sigma):=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(C_{k})=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(\{t_{k}=0\})\subset D(\sigma)\quad(0\leq k\leq n-1).

Then
(D(\sigma),H_{\bullet})
forms an ordered SNC pair (see Lemma
1.4.3
). We set:

H(\sigma):=\bigcup_{k=0}^{n-1}H_{k}(\sigma),

H_{K}(\sigma):=\bigcap_{k\in K}H_{k}(\sigma)\ \quad(K\subset\{0,\dots,n-1\}).

Lemma 1.4.2

(Smoothness of the structural morphism)
.

Under the standing assumptions of this subsection, the morphism

p_{\sigma}:D(\sigma)\longrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}

is smooth.

Proof.

Each
Z_{i}
is smooth over
S
, hence flat over
S
, and each closed immersion

Z_{i}\hookrightarrow Z_{i+1}
is regular, hence
H_{1}
-regular. Therefore the

\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
-deformation datum attached to
\sigma
is exactly in the smooth

\mathbb{A}^{n}
-deformation setting of
[
DM24
, Thm. 4.1(ii)]
, and the conclusion is

[
DM24
, Prop. 6.1]
.
∎

Lemma 1.4.3

(Boundary divisors form an ordered SNC pair)
.

The family
H_{\bullet}=(H_{0},\dots,H_{n-1})
endows
D(\sigma)
with the structure
of an ordered SNC pair over
S
.

In particular, for every
K\subset\{0,\dots,n-1\}
the intersection
H_{K}
is smooth over
S
.

Proof.

For every subset
K\subset\{0,\dots,n-1\}
, let

C_{K}:=\bigcap_{k\in K}\{t_{k}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}.

Then
C_{K}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{n-|K|}_{S}
, so
C_{K}
is smooth over
S
, and

C_{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
is a regular immersion of codimension
|K|
,
cut out by the coordinate functions
(t_{k})_{k\in K}
.
Since
p_{\sigma}
is smooth (see Lemma
1.4.2
), for every
K
we have

H_{K}=D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}C_{K},

hence
H_{K}
is smooth over
S
. Moreover
H_{K}\hookrightarrow D(\sigma)

is the base change of
C_{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
, so it is again a regular
immersion of codimension
|K|
; in particular, each
H_{k}
is an effective
Cartier divisor. Thus
(D(\sigma),H_{\bullet})
is an ordered SNC pair in the
sense of Definition
1.4.1
.

∎

Lemma 1.4.4

(Generic stratum)
.

Let
U:=(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
be the complement of the coordinate hyperplanes.
Then there is a canonical identification over
U

D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}U\ \simeq\ Z_{n}\times_{S}U.

Equivalently,
D(\sigma)\setminus H\simeq Z_{n}\times_{S}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}
canonically. This identification is functorial
for pullback of flags along morphisms
S^{\prime}\to S
.

Proof.

Over
U
all coordinate divisors
\{t_{k}=0\}
are empty, hence all
D_{i}
and
D_{i}^{\prime}
restrict to the empty subscheme of

X|_{U}=Z_{n}\times U
. In the universal property
[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4]
, the condition

f^{-1}(D_{i}^{\prime})\subset f^{-1}(X_{i})
becomes vacuous over
U
, so
D(\sigma)|_{U}
represents the same functor as
X|_{U}
.
By uniqueness of representing objects,
D(\sigma)|_{U}\simeq X|_{U}\simeq Z_{n}\times U
.

Functoriality for base change
S^{\prime}\to S
follows because the construction of the datum
(X_{i},D_{i})
commutes with base
change and the universal property is stable under base change (cf.
[
DM24
, Notation 2.6]
).
∎

Lemma 1.4.5

(One-parameter slice of
D(\sigma)
)
.

Let
\sigma\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}
and fix
0\leq k\leq n-1
. Let

W_{k}\ :=\ \{(t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1})\in\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\mid t_{j}\in\mathbb{G}_{m}\ \text{for all }j\neq k\}\ \simeq\ \mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n-1},

and write
j_{k}:W_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
for the open immersion. Set

D(\sigma)^{(k)}\ :=\ D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}W_{k}\qquad\text{and}\qquad D(\sigma)^{(k)}_{0}\ :=\ D(\sigma)^{(k)}\times_{W_{k}}\{t_{k}=0\}.

Then there is a canonical isomorphism of pairs over
S

(D(\sigma)^{(k)},\ D(\sigma)^{(k)}_{0})\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ \bigl(D(Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{n}),\ N_{Z_{k}/Z_{n}}\bigr)\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n-1},

where
D(Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
is the usual deformation-to-the-normal-cone space
(cf. Remark
1.2.7
(1)).
This isomorphism is compatible with the identification of open complements

D(\sigma)^{(k)}\setminus D(\sigma)^{(k)}_{0}\ =\ D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}\ \simeq\ Z_{n}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}

(from Lemma
1.4.4
).

Proof.

Write
X:=Z_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
and recall that
D(\sigma)
is the multi-dilatation

D((D_{i}),(X_{i});X)
, and let
(D_{i}^{(k)},X_{i}^{(k)};X^{(k)})

be its pullback along
\operatorname{id}_{Z_{n}}\times j_{k}
, so
X^{(k)}=Z_{n}\times W_{k}
.
By definition,
D(\sigma)^{(k)}=D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}W_{k}
is canonically identified with

D((D_{i}^{(k)}),(X_{i}^{(k)});X^{(k)})
.

Over
W_{k}
, each monomial divisor
D_{i}^{\prime}
(defined from
t_{0}\cdots t_{n-i}
) becomes either empty or equal to the
single Cartier divisor
(t_{k}=0)
: explicitly, since
t_{j}
is invertible on
W_{k}
for
j\neq k
,

D_{i}^{\prime}|_{W_{k}}\ =\begin{cases}(t_{k}=0)&\text{if }i\leq n-k,\\
\varnothing&\text{if }i>n-k.\end{cases}

(We regard the empty closed subscheme as an effective Cartier divisor, with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{T}
.)

Thus, for a morphism
f:T\to X^{(k)}=Z_{n}\times W_{k}
, the multi-dilatation conditions

f^{-1}(D_{i}^{\prime}|_{W_{k}})\subseteq f^{-1}(X_{i}^{(k)})
are:

•

vacuous for
i>n-k
;

•

for
i\leq n-k
, the single condition that
f^{-1}(t_{k}=0)
maps into
Z_{n-i}
.

Since
Z_{k}=Z_{n-(n-k)}\subseteq Z_{n-i}
for all
i\leq n-k
, these are equivalent to the single strongest condition

f^{-1}(t_{k}=0)\ \subseteq\ f^{-1}(Z_{k}\times W_{k}).

By the universal property of multi-dilatations (as in
[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4]
), this shows that

D(\sigma)^{(k)}
represents the same functor as the
one-step
dilatation for the closed immersion

Z_{k}\times W_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{n}\times W_{k}
with respect to the divisor
(t_{k}=0)
.
But that one-step dilatation is exactly the base change of the usual deformation space

D(Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\to\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
along
W_{k}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n-1}
, i.e.

D(Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n-1}
, with special fiber
N_{Z_{k}/Z_{n}}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n-1}
.
This yields the claimed canonical isomorphism of pairs, and compatibility with the open complements is immediate.
∎

Lemma 1.4.6

(Deepest stratum: projection to
Z_{0}
and vector bundle structure)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})
be a flag of regular closed
immersions between smooth
S
-schemes and let

p_{\sigma}:D(\sigma)\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
and
q_{\sigma}:D(\sigma)\to Z_{n}
be as in Definition
1.2.4
.
Recall that we denote by

H_{[n-1]}\;:=\;\bigcap_{k=0}^{n-1}H_{k}\;=\;p_{\sigma}^{-1}(\{t_{0}=\cdots=t_{n-1}=0\})\subset D(\sigma)

the deepest stratum.

(1)

The restriction
(q_{\sigma})_{|H_{[n-1]}}:H_{[n-1]}\to Z_{n}
factors canonically through the closed immersion

Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{n}
. We write the induced morphism as

\pi_{\sigma}:\ H_{[n-1]}\longrightarrow Z_{0}.

(2)

The morphism
\pi_{\sigma}
is the projection of (the total space of) a vector bundle over
Z_{0}
, canonically determined by
\sigma
:
there exists a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{Z_{0}}
-module
\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}
and a canonical isomorphism of
Z_{0}
-schemes

H_{[n-1]}\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ \mathbf{V}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}):=\Spec_{Z_{0}}\bigl(\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathcal{O}_{Z_{0}}}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}^{\vee})\bigr),

under which
\pi_{\sigma}
identifies with the structure morphism
\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma})\to Z_{0}
.
In particular,
\pi_{\sigma}
has a canonical zero section.

All constructions commute with base change in
S
(pullback of flags along
S^{\prime}\to S
).

Proof.

The point is that the deepest stratum is obtained by successive panelizations; in the regular case every intermediate
V
-object is a vector bundle, so the final stratum is a vector bundle over the smallest center
Z_{0}
.

Keep the notation of Definition
1.2.4
:

X:=Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S},\qquad X_{i}:=Z_{n-i}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\ (1\leq i\leq n),\qquad D_{i}:=Z_{n}\times_{S}\{t_{n-i}=0\}\subset X.

We have
\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}=Z_{n}\times_{S}\{t_{0}=\cdots=t_{n-1}=0\}\subset X
.
Since
\rho_{\sigma}:D(\sigma)\to X
satisfies
p_{\sigma}=\operatorname{pr}_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}\circ\rho_{\sigma}
, we have

H_{[n-1]}=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(\{0\})=\rho_{\sigma}^{-1}\big(\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}\big)=D(\sigma)\times_{X}\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}

as closed subschemes of
D(\sigma)
.

(1) Canonical factorization through
Z_{0}
.

By
[
DM24
, Prop. 6.1]
, in the smooth/regular setting the deformation space
D(\sigma)
is smooth over
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
.
By
[
DM24
, Thm. 6.3]
, the stratum
H_{[n-1]}
belongs to the class for which the canonical stratum isomorphism
of
[
DM24
, Thm. 5.3]
applies (Equation (5.2) loc. cit.).
Specializing that canonical description to the full subset
\{1,\dots,n\}
identifies the structural map

H_{[n-1]}\to X
as factoring through the smallest center pulled back to
\big(\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}\big)
, namely

X_{n}\times_{X}\big(\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}\big)\;=\;(Z_{0}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S})\times_{(Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S})}(Z_{n}\times_{S}\{0\})\;\simeq\;Z_{0}.

Composing with
\operatorname{pr}_{Z_{n}}:X\to Z_{n}
gives the claimed factorization

(q_{\sigma})_{|H_{[n-1]}}:H_{[n-1]}\to Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{n}
, and we denote the first arrow by
\pi_{\sigma}
.

(2) Vector bundle structure on
H_{[n-1]}\to Z_{0}
.

We only need the existence of the vector bundle here; an explicit local normal-form description
will be given in Proposition
1.4.9
below.
By
[
DM24
, Thm. 6.3]
, Equation (5.2) of
[
DM24
, Thm. 5.3]
applies to the full subset

S=\{1,\dots,n\}
.
The resulting description of the deepest stratum is obtained by iterating the codimension-one
panelization formula
[
DM24
, Prop. 6.5]
. At each step, the new term is a Dubouloz-Mayeux

V
-object
V(D/Y,X)\cong\mathbf{V}\!\bigl((C_{Z/D}\otimes J_{Z}^{-1})^{\vee}\bigr)
as in
Remark
1.3.5
.
In our smooth situation all immersions are regular and all
D_{i}
are Cartier divisors, so each such

V
-object is the total space of a vector bundle over the current base.
Iterating this construction down to the full stratum shows that
H_{[n-1]}
is canonically the total
space of a vector bundle over

X_{n}\times_{X}\Bigl(\bigcap_{i=1}^{n}D_{i}\Bigr)\simeq Z_{0}.

We denote the corresponding locally free
\mathcal{O}_{Z_{0}}
-module by
\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}
.
Thus there is a canonical isomorphism

H_{[n-1]}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma})

over
Z_{0}
, and
\pi_{\sigma}
identifies with the vector-bundle projection.

Base change functoriality.

All ingredients commute with base change in
S
: the deformation datum does, the formation of the multi-centered
deformation space is compatible with base change by the universal property
[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4, Cor. 2.5]
,
and the stratum/panelization isomorphisms in
[
DM24
, Thm. 5.3, Thm. 6.3, Prop. 6.5]
are canonical.
Hence
\pi_{\sigma}
and the identification
H_{[n-1]}\simeq\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma})
are functorial for pullback of flags.
∎

Lemma 1.4.7

(Adapted block equations)
.

Let

\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}

be a flag of regular closed immersions between smooth
S
-schemes, and put

r_{i}:=\rk(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}})\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

Fix a point
z\in Z_{0}
. Then, after shrinking
Z_{n}
Zariski-locally around
z
, there exist
a Zariski-open neighbourhood
U\subset Z_{n}
of
z
and tuples

\mathbf{x}_{i}=(x_{i,1},\dots,x_{i,r_{i}})\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{O}_{U})^{r_{i}}\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1)

such that, writing

U_{j}:=U\times_{Z_{n}}Z_{j}\subset U,

one has

U_{j}=V(\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathbf{x}_{j+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})\qquad(0\leq j\leq n),

with the convention
U_{n}=U
, and such that the images of
\mathbf{x}_{i}
in

\mathfrak{I}_{U_{i}/U_{i+1}}/\mathfrak{I}_{U_{i}/U_{i+1}}^{2}

form a basis. Moreover, for every
0\leq j\leq n-1
, the concatenated tuple

(\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathbf{x}_{j+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})

is a regular sequence generating the ideal sheaf of
U_{j}
in
U
.

Proof.

We argue by descending induction on
i
.

For
i=n-1
, since
Z_{n-1}\hookrightarrow Z_{n}
is a regular immersion of codimension

r_{n-1}
, after shrinking around
z
there exist functions

\mathbf{x}_{n-1}=(x_{n-1,1},\dots,x_{n-1,r_{n-1}})

on an open neighbourhood
U^{(n-1)}\subset Z_{n}
of
z
cutting out

Z_{n-1}\cap U^{(n-1)}
, and whose classes form a basis of the conormal sheaf.

Suppose that, for some
i<n-1
, on an open neighbourhood
U^{(i+1)}\subset Z_{n}
of
z

we have already constructed blocks
\mathbf{x}_{j}
for all
j>i
such that

U^{(i+1)}_{j}=V(\mathbf{x}_{j},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})\qquad(j\geq i+1).

Since
U^{(i+1)}_{i}\hookrightarrow U^{(i+1)}_{i+1}
is a regular immersion of codimension

r_{i}
, after shrinking
U^{(i+1)}_{i+1}
around
z
there exist functions

\bar{\mathbf{x}}_{i}=(\bar{x}_{i,1},\dots,\bar{x}_{i,r_{i}})\in\Gamma(U^{(i+1)}_{i+1},\mathcal{O}_{U^{(i+1)}_{i+1}})^{r_{i}}

cutting out
U^{(i+1)}_{i}
inside
U^{(i+1)}_{i+1}
, and whose classes form a basis of

\mathfrak{I}_{U^{(i+1)}_{i}/U^{(i+1)}_{i+1}}/\mathfrak{I}_{U^{(i+1)}_{i}/U^{(i+1)}_{i+1}}^{2}
.
After shrinking
U^{(i+1)}
further, we may lift the
\bar{x}_{i,a}
to functions

x_{i,a}\in\Gamma(U^{(i)},\mathcal{O}_{U^{(i)}})

on an open neighbourhood
U^{(i)}\subset U^{(i+1)}
of
z
.
Then the ideal of
U^{(i)}_{i}
in
U^{(i)}
is generated by the ideal of

U^{(i)}_{i+1}
together with
\mathbf{x}_{i}
, hence

U^{(i)}_{i}=V(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1}).

This finishes the induction. Replacing
U
by the final open neighbourhood, we obtain the
claimed system of equations.

For the last assertion, fix
j
. The immersion
U_{j}\hookrightarrow U
is regular, and its
ideal is generated by the
\sum_{\ell=j}^{n-1}r_{\ell}
components of

(\mathbf{x}_{j},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})
. Since this number equals the codimension of

U_{j}
in
U
, these generators form a regular sequence.
∎

Lemma 1.4.8

(The explicit model has Cartier coordinate hyperplanes)
.

Let
U\subset Z_{n}
and
\mathbf{x}_{i}
be as in Lemma
1.4.7
, and put

T_{i}:=t_{0}t_{1}\cdots t_{i}\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

Define

A:=\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1},\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}]\Big/(\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n-1},

and let

Y:=\Spec_{U}(A)\longrightarrow U\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}.

Then, for every
0\leq k\leq n-1
, the pullback of
V(t_{k})\subset U\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
to
Y

is an effective Cartier divisor. Equivalently, the image of
t_{k}
in
A
is a non-zero-divisor.

Proof.

Fix
k
. Write

B:=\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1},\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}],\qquad J:=(\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n-1},

so that
A=B/J
.

We will show that the images of the generators of
J
in
B/(t_{k})
form a regular sequence.
This implies that

t_{k},\ (\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n-1}

is a regular sequence in
B
, hence
t_{k}
is a non-zero-divisor on
A
.

Modulo
t_{k}
, the relations
\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}
become simply
\mathbf{x}_{i}
for
all
i\geq k
, because
T_{i}
is divisible by
t_{k}
. By
Lemma
1.4.7
, the concatenated tuple

(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{x}_{k+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})

is a regular sequence on
U
. Hence, after quotienting
B/(t_{k})
by these blocks, we obtain
a polynomial ring over
U_{k}
:

R_{k}:=\mathcal{O}_{U_{k}}[t_{0},\dots,\widehat{t_{k}},\dots,t_{n-1},\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{k-1},\mathbf{u}_{k},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}].

For
0\leq i\leq k-1
, define inductively

R_{i}:=R_{i+1}/(\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}),

where
\mathbf{x}_{i}
denotes the pullback of the block
\mathbf{x}_{i}
to
U_{k}
.
We claim, by descending induction on
i
, that
T_{i}
is a non-zero-divisor on
R_{i}
.

For
i=k-1
, the element
T_{k-1}=t_{0}\cdots t_{k-1}
is a non-zero-divisor on the polynomial
ring
R_{k}
. Modulo
T_{k-1}
, the block
\mathbf{x}_{k-1}-T_{k-1}\mathbf{u}_{k-1}
reduces to

\mathbf{x}_{k-1}
, and
\mathbf{x}_{k-1}
is a regular sequence on
U_{k}
because

U_{k-1}\hookrightarrow U_{k}
is a regular immersion. Therefore
T_{k-1}
remains a
non-zero-divisor after quotienting by
\mathbf{x}_{k-1}-T_{k-1}\mathbf{u}_{k-1}
, i.e. on

R_{k-1}
.

Now assume
0\leq i<k-1
and that
T_{i+1}
is a non-zero-divisor on
R_{i+1}
.
Since

T_{i+1}=T_{i}\,t_{i+1},

it follows that
T_{i}
is also a non-zero-divisor on
R_{i+1}
. Hence, componentwise,
the block
\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}
is a regular sequence on
R_{i+1}
, because each
equation is linear in a fresh variable with non-zero-divisor coefficient
T_{i}
.

Moreover, modulo
T_{i}
, every previously imposed relation

\mathbf{x}_{j}-T_{j}\mathbf{u}_{j}
with
j>i
reduces to
\mathbf{x}_{j}
, since each
T_{j}
is
divisible by
T_{i}
. Thus

R_{i+1}/T_{i}R_{i+1}

is a polynomial algebra over
\mathcal{O}_{U_{i+1}}
. In that quotient, the block
\mathbf{x}_{i}

is a regular sequence because
U_{i}\hookrightarrow U_{i+1}
is a regular immersion and the
images of
\mathbf{x}_{i}
form a basis of its conormal sheaf. Therefore

T_{i}
remains a non-zero-divisor after quotienting by

\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}
, i.e. on
R_{i}
.

This proves the induction. In particular, the image of
J
in
B/(t_{k})
is generated by a
regular sequence. Consequently
t_{k}
is a non-zero-divisor on
A=B/J
, as required.
∎

Proposition 1.4.9

(Zariski-local adapted block coordinates and their transition law)
.

Let

\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}

be a flag of regular closed immersions between smooth
S
-schemes, and set

r_{i}:=\rk(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}})\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

Then for every point
