z\in Z_{0}
there exists a Zariski-open neighbourhood

U\subset Z_{n}
of
z
and functions

\mathbf{x}_{i}=(x_{i,1},\dots,x_{i,r_{i}})\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{O}_{U})^{r_{i}}\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1)

such that, writing

U_{j}:=U\times_{Z_{n}}Z_{j}\subset U,

one has

U_{j}=V(\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathbf{x}_{j+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})\qquad(0\leq j\leq n),

and the images of
\mathbf{x}_{i}
in the conormal sheaf

\mathfrak{I}_{U_{i}/U_{i+1}}/\mathfrak{I}_{U_{i}/U_{i+1}}^{2}

form a basis.

Let

T_{i}:=t_{0}t_{1}\cdots t_{i}\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

Then, for every such adapted system:

(1)

there is a canonical isomorphism

D(\sigma)|_{U}\;\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\;\Spec_{U}\!\Bigl(\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1},\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}]\Big/(\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n-1}\Bigr),

where
\mathbf{u}_{i}=(u_{i,1},\dots,u_{i,r_{i}})
;

(2)

after restricting to the deepest stratum
H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)
, one gets

H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)|_{U_{0}}\;\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\;\Spec_{U_{0}}\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{U_{0}}[\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}]\bigr)\;\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\;\mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}}|_{U_{0}}\Bigr),

where the block
\mathbf{u}_{i}
is the fiber coordinate on the summand

\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}}|_{U_{0}}
;

(3)

if
\mathbf{y}_{i}=(y_{i,1},\dots,y_{i,r_{i}})
is another adapted system on a Zariski-open

V\subset Z_{n}
, then after shrinking
W:=U\cap V
around

W_{0}:=W\times_{Z_{n}}Z_{0}

there exist matrices

A_{i}\in\operatorname{GL}_{r_{i}}(\Gamma(W,\mathcal{O}_{W})),\qquad B_{ij}\in M_{r_{i}\times r_{j}}(\Gamma(W,\mathcal{O}_{W}))\quad(i<j),

such that

\mathbf{y}_{i}=A_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}+\sum_{j>i}B_{ij}\mathbf{x}_{j}\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

Writing
\mathbf{v}_{i}:=\mathbf{y}_{i}/T_{i}
, one has on
D(\sigma)|_{W}

\mathbf{v}_{i}=A_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}+\sum_{j>i}B_{ij}(t_{i+1}\cdots t_{j})\mathbf{u}_{j},

and hence on
H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)|_{W_{0}}

\mathbf{v}_{i}=(A_{i}|_{W_{0}})\mathbf{u}_{i}.

In particular, the transition function on the
i
-th block is exactly the transition
function of the vector bundle
\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}}|_{W_{0}}
.

Proof.

The existence of
U
and of the adapted blocks
\mathbf{x}_{i}
is
Lemma
1.4.7
.

The role of the adapted block coordinates is to reduce the higher deformation
space to an explicit monomial local model. Once this model is written down, both
the description of the deepest stratum and the transition law are obtained by
unwinding the same coordinates.

For (
1
), write

X_{U}:=U\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S},\qquad I_{j}:=(\mathbf{x}_{j},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})\subset\mathcal{O}_{U}

for the ideal sheaf of
U_{j}\subset U
. By Definition
1.2.4
,
the algebra of
D(\sigma)|_{U}
is the
\mathcal{O}_{X_{U}}
-subalgebra

\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,U}=\sum_{a_{0},\dots,a_{n-1}\in\mathbf{Z}}I_{n-1}^{a_{n-1}}I_{n-2}^{a_{n-2}-a_{n-1}}\cdots I_{0}^{a_{0}-a_{1}}\,t_{0}^{-a_{0}}\cdots t_{n-1}^{-a_{n-1}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0}^{\pm 1},\dots,t_{n-1}^{\pm 1}].

Set
a_{n}:=0
and
b_{i}:=a_{i}-a_{i+1}
. Then

a_{i}=b_{i}+b_{i+1}+\cdots+b_{n-1},\qquad t_{0}^{-a_{0}}\cdots t_{n-1}^{-a_{n-1}}=T_{0}^{-b_{0}}\cdots T_{n-1}^{-b_{n-1}},

so

\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,U}=\sum_{b_{0},\dots,b_{n-1}\in\mathbf{Z}}I_{0}^{b_{0}}\cdots I_{n-1}^{b_{n-1}}\,T_{0}^{-b_{0}}\cdots T_{n-1}^{-b_{n-1}}.

Since
I_{j}^{m}=\mathcal{O}_{U}
for
m\leq 0
, the terms with some
b_{i}<0
are already generated
over
\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1}]
by the terms with all
b_{i}\geq 0
. Hence

\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,U}=\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1}]\Bigl[\frac{I_{0}}{T_{0}},\dots,\frac{I_{n-1}}{T_{n-1}}\Bigr].

Now
I_{i}=(\mathbf{x}_{i},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})
, and for every
j\geq i
and every component

x_{j,a}
of
\mathbf{x}_{j}
one has

\frac{x_{j,a}}{T_{i}}=(t_{i+1}\cdots t_{j})\frac{x_{j,a}}{T_{j}}.

Therefore
\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,U}
is generated over
\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1}]
by the
elements

u_{i,a}:=\frac{x_{i,a}}{T_{i}}\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1,\ 1\leq a\leq r_{i}).

Consider the
X_{U}
-scheme

Y:=\Spec_{U}\!\Bigl(\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1},\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}]\Big/(\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n-1}\Bigr).

Because the relations
\mathbf{x}_{i}=T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}
hold in
\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,U}
, there is
a canonical
X_{U}
-morphism

D(\sigma)|_{U}\longrightarrow Y.

Let
\mathcal{C}_{U}
be the full subcategory of
X_{U}
-schemes
f:T\to X_{U}
such that
each pullback
f^{-1}(t_{k}=0)
is an effective Cartier divisor.
We claim that
Y
represents on
\mathcal{C}_{U}
the same functor as
D(\sigma)|_{U}
.

So let
f:T\to X_{U}
be an object of
\mathcal{C}_{U}
. Write

s_{k}:=f^{\sharp}(t_{k})\in\Gamma(T,\mathcal{O}_{T}),\qquad S_{i}:=s_{0}s_{1}\cdots s_{i}.

Because each
V(s_{k})=f^{-1}(t_{k}=0)
is an effective Cartier divisor, each
s_{k}
is
locally a non-zero-divisor, hence so is
S_{i}
, and

V(S_{i})=f^{-1}(V(T_{i}))

is again an effective Cartier divisor.

To give a lift
T\to Y
over
X_{U}
is equivalent to giving sections

v_{i,a}\in\Gamma(T,\mathcal{O}_{T})\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1,\ 1\leq a\leq r_{i})

such that

f^{\sharp}(x_{i,a})=S_{i}\,v_{i,a}\qquad\text{for all }i,a.

Such
v_{i,a}
, if they exist, are unique, and they exist if and only if

f^{\sharp}(x_{i,a})\in(S_{i})\qquad\text{for all }i,a.

On the other hand, by Definition
1.2.2
(reindexed by
i\mapsto n-i
),
a lift
T\to D(\sigma)|_{U}
exists if and only if

f^{-1}(V(T_{i}))\subset f^{-1}(U_{i})\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

Since

U_{i}=V(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1}),

this is equivalent to

f^{\sharp}(x_{j,a})\in(S_{i})\qquad\text{for all }j\geq i,\ 1\leq a\leq r_{j}.

These conditions are equivalent to the previous ones: taking
j=i
gives

f^{\sharp}(x_{i,a})\in(S_{i})
, and conversely, if

f^{\sharp}(x_{j,a})=S_{j}v_{j,a}
, then

f^{\sharp}(x_{j,a})=S_{i}\,(s_{i+1}\cdots s_{j})v_{j,a}\in(S_{i})\qquad(j\geq i).

Thus
Y
and
D(\sigma)|_{U}
represent the same functor on
\mathcal{C}_{U}
, provided

Y
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{U}
. This is exactly
Lemma
1.4.8
. Therefore

D(\sigma)|_{U}\;\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\;\Spec_{U}\!\Bigl(\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1},\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}]\Big/(\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n-1}\Bigr).

Statement (
2
) is obtained by setting

t_{0}=\cdots=t_{n-1}=0
. Since
U_{0}\subset U
is already defined by

\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1}
, the fiber is

\Spec_{U_{0}}\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{U_{0}}[\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}]\bigr).

By construction, the block
\mathbf{u}_{i}
is dual to the chosen basis of the conormal sheaf

\mathfrak{I}_{U_{i}/U_{i+1}}/\mathfrak{I}_{U_{i}/U_{i+1}}^{2}
, hence is the fiber coordinate on

\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}}|_{U_{0}}
. This gives the claimed vector bundle identification.

For (
3
), let
W:=U\cap V
and write

W_{i}:=W\times_{Z_{n}}Z_{i}.

Since
\mathbf{y}_{i}
cuts out
W_{i}
, each
\mathbf{y}_{i}
lies in the ideal

(\mathbf{x}_{i},\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})\subset\Gamma(W,\mathcal{O}_{W}),

so there exist matrices
A_{i}
and
B_{ij}
such that

\mathbf{y}_{i}=A_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}+\sum_{j>i}B_{ij}\mathbf{x}_{j}.

Reducing modulo
(\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})
, the blocks
\mathbf{x}_{i}
and

\mathbf{y}_{i}
induce two bases of

\mathfrak{I}_{W_{i}/W_{i+1}}/\mathfrak{I}_{W_{i}/W_{i+1}}^{2}
. Hence
A_{i}|_{W_{i}}
is invertible.
After shrinking
W
around
W_{0}
, we may assume

A_{i}\in\operatorname{GL}_{r_{i}}(\Gamma(W,\mathcal{O}_{W}))\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

By (
1
), on
W
we have two presentations

A_{\mathbf{x}}:=\mathcal{O}_{W}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1},\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}]\Big/(\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n-1},

A_{\mathbf{y}}:=\mathcal{O}_{W}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1},\mathbf{v}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{v}_{n-1}]\Big/(\mathbf{y}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{v}_{i})_{0\leq i\leq n-1}.

The assignment

\mathbf{v}_{i}\longmapsto A_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}+\sum_{j>i}B_{ij}(t_{i+1}\cdots t_{j})\mathbf{u}_{j}

defines an
\mathcal{O}_{W}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1}]
-algebra morphism

A_{\mathbf{y}}\longrightarrow A_{\mathbf{x}},

because in
A_{\mathbf{x}}
one has

\mathbf{y}_{i}-T_{i}\Bigl(A_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}+\sum_{j>i}B_{ij}(t_{i+1}\cdots t_{j})\mathbf{u}_{j}\Bigr)=\mathbf{y}_{i}-A_{i}\mathbf{x}_{i}-\sum_{j>i}B_{ij}\mathbf{x}_{j}=0.

Via the canonical identifications

\Spec_{W}(A_{\mathbf{x}})\xrightarrow{\sim}D(\sigma)|_{W}\xleftarrow{\sim}\Spec_{W}(A_{\mathbf{y}}),

this is the transition isomorphism between the two coordinate presentations. Therefore, on

D(\sigma)|_{W}
,

\mathbf{v}_{i}=A_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}+\sum_{j>i}B_{ij}(t_{i+1}\cdots t_{j})\mathbf{u}_{j}.

Restricting to the deepest stratum kills all
t_{\ell}
, hence every mixed term with
j>i

vanishes and

\mathbf{v}_{i}=(A_{i}|_{W_{0}})\mathbf{u}_{i}.

This is exactly the transition law of the bundle

\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}}|_{W_{0}}
.
∎

Corollary 1.4.10

(Rank of the deepest vector bundle)
.

With notation as in Lemma
1.4.6
, one has

\rk(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma})=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\rk(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}}).

Proof.

By Proposition
1.4.9
(
2
),
Zariski-locally on
Z_{0}
one has

H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\simeq\mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}}|_{U_{0}}\Bigr).

Hence

\rk(\mathcal{E}_{\sigma})=\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}\rk(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}}).\qed

Corollary 1.4.11

(Canonical global direct-sum description of the deepest stratum)
.

Let

\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}

be a flag of regular closed immersions between smooth
S
-schemes, and let

H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\subset D(\sigma)

be its deepest stratum.
Then there is a canonical isomorphism of
Z_{0}
-schemes

H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\;\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\;\mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}})|_{Z_{0}}\Bigr).

Equivalently, the vector bundle
\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}
of Lemma
1.4.6
(2)
admits a canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}\;\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\;\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}})|_{Z_{0}}.

Proof.

Choose a Zariski-open cover
(U_{\alpha})_{\alpha}
of a neighbourhood of
Z_{0}
in
Z_{n}

by adapted charts as in Proposition
1.4.9
, and write

U_{\alpha,0}:=U_{\alpha}\times_{Z_{n}}Z_{0}
.
For each
\alpha
, the chosen adapted coordinates determine an isomorphism

\phi_{\alpha}:H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)|_{U_{\alpha,0}}\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}})|_{U_{\alpha,0}}\Bigr).

On an overlap
W_{0}:=U_{\alpha,0}\cap U_{\beta,0}
, let

\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\alpha)}
and
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\beta)}
be the corresponding block coordinates.
By Proposition
1.4.9
(
3
),
there exist matrices
A_{i}\in\operatorname{GL}_{r_{i}}(\Gamma(W_{0},\mathcal{O}_{W_{0}}))
such that

\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\beta)}=(A_{i}|_{W_{0}})\,\mathbf{u}_{i}^{(\alpha)}\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

Thus
\phi_{\beta}\circ\phi_{\alpha}^{-1}
is the vector bundle automorphism induced by the
block-diagonal matrix
\bigoplus_{i}A_{i}
, which is exactly the transition automorphism of

\bigoplus_{i}(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}})|_{Z_{0}}
.
Hence the
\phi_{\alpha}
glue to a global isomorphism

H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}})|_{Z_{0}}\Bigr).

Comparing with Lemma
1.4.6
(2) yields the canonical isomorphism

\mathcal{E}_{\sigma}\simeq\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}})|_{Z_{0}}.

∎

Remark 1.4.12

(What Corollary
1.4.11
does
not
say)
.

For
n=2
, Corollary
1.4.11
does
not
identify the deepest stratum with

\,N_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}}
. Rather, Rost’s description gives

H_{\{0,1\}}(\sigma)\simeq N_{\,N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}/N_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}}}.

If
F\subset E
is a subbundle of vector bundles on a scheme
X
, then the normal bundle of the closed immersion

\mathbf{V}_{X}(F)\hookrightarrow\mathbf{V}_{X}(E)
is canonically isomorphic, as an
X
-scheme, to

\mathbf{V}_{X}\!\bigl(F\oplus(E/F)\bigr).

Applying this with
F=\mathcal{N}_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}
and
E=\mathcal{N}_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}}
yields

H_{\{0,1\}}(\sigma)\simeq\mathbf{V}_{Z_{0}}\!\Bigl(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\oplus(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}})|_{Z_{0}}\Bigr).

This is fully compatible with the nonsplit exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{N}_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\to\mathcal{N}_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}}\to(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}})|_{Z_{0}}\to 0;

Corollary
1.4.11
does
not
assert that this sequence splits.

Lemma 1.4.13

(Global block description of panel restriction on the deepest stratum)
.

Let

\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n},\qquad 1\leq k\leq n-1,

and set

\mathcal{N}_{i}:=(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}})|_{Z_{0}},\qquad N_{i}:=\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{N}_{i}),\qquad\pi_{k}:N_{k}\to Z_{0}.

Then the global isomorphism on deepest strata

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}\big|_{H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)}:H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))

of Lemma
1.3.7
identifies

H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))
canonically with the total space of the vector bundle

\pi_{k}^{*}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{i\neq k}\mathcal{N}_{i}\Bigr)

over
N_{k}
.
More precisely, under the canonical identifications

H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\simeq\mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{N}_{i}\Bigr),\qquad H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\simeq\mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\pi_{k}^{*}(\bigoplus_{i\neq k}\mathcal{N}_{i})\Bigr),

the inverse

\theta_{k,\sigma}:H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)

is given by insertion of the tautological
N_{k}
-coordinate in the
k
-th block:

(e_{k},(u_{i})_{i\neq k})\longmapsto(u_{0},\dots,u_{k-1},e_{k},u_{k+1},\dots,u_{n-1}).

In particular the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{k}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{k,\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\sigma}}

commutes.

Proof.

The existence of

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}\big|_{H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)}

is global by Lemma
1.3.7
.
It remains to identify it.

This is local on
Z_{0}
for the Zariski topology.
Choose an adapted chart
U\subset Z_{n}
as in
Proposition
1.4.9
, with coordinates

\mathbf{x}_{i}
and corresponding deepest-stratum coordinates
\mathbf{u}_{i}
.
Restricting to the panel
t_{k}=0
and applying the panel identification

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}:H_{k}(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\sim}D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)),

one obtains on
U_{0}:=U\times_{Z_{n}}Z_{0}
the explicit presentation

H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))|_{U_{0}}\;\simeq\;\Spec_{U_{0}}\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{U_{0}}[\mathbf{e}_{k},\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{k}},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}]\bigr),

where
\mathbf{e}_{k}
is the fiber coordinate on
\mathcal{N}_{k}|_{U_{0}}
.
In these coordinates,
\theta_{k,\sigma}
is exactly

(\mathbf{e}_{k},\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\widehat{\mathbf{u}_{k}},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1})\longmapsto(\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{k-1},\mathbf{e}_{k},\mathbf{u}_{k+1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}).

On overlaps, Proposition
1.4.9
(
3
)
shows that each block transforms independently by the transition functions of the corresponding
normal bundle. Hence these local descriptions glue globally.
The commutativity of the square is immediate from the formula.
∎

Lemma 1.4.14

(Global block description of the deepest comparison morphism)
.

Let

\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n},\qquad 1\leq k\leq n-1,

and set

\mathcal{N}_{i}:=(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{i}/Z_{i+1}})|_{Z_{0}},\qquad\mathcal{N}_{k-1,k+1}:=(\mathcal{N}_{Z_{k-1}/Z_{k+1}}).

Let

\widetilde{\Pi}_{\operatorname{deep}}:=\Pi_{\operatorname{deep}}\circ\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}\big|_{H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)}:H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\longrightarrow H_{{[n-2]}}(d_{k}\sigma).

Under the canonical identifications

H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\simeq\mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{N}_{i}\Bigr)

and

H_{{[n-2]}}(d_{k}\sigma)\simeq\mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{i<k-1}\mathcal{N}_{i}\oplus\mathcal{N}_{k-1,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i>k}\mathcal{N}_{i}\Bigr),

the morphism
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\operatorname{deep}}
is induced by the bundle map which is:

(1)

the identity on the summands
\mathcal{N}_{i}
for
i<k-1
and for
i>k
;

(2)

on the skipped stage, the canonical composite

\mathcal{N}_{k-1}\oplus\mathcal{N}_{k}\xrightarrow{\ \operatorname{pr}_{1}\ }\mathcal{N}_{k-1}\xrightarrow{\ \iota\ }\mathcal{N}_{k-1,k+1}|_{Z_{0}},

where
\iota
is the natural inclusion coming from the short exact conormal/normal sequence
for

Z_{k-1}\hookrightarrow Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{k+1}.

Consequently, if

s_{k}:\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{N}_{k})\longrightarrow H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\simeq\mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{N}_{i}\Bigr)

denotes the inclusion of the
k
-th summand as the zero section in all other blocks, and

s_{d}:Z_{0}\longrightarrow H_{{[n-2]}}(d_{k}\sigma)

denotes the zero section, then

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{V}(\mathcal{N}_{k})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{{[n-2]}}(d_{k}\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{k}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\Pi}_{\operatorname{deep}}}

is Cartesian.

Proof.

This is local on
Z_{0}
for the Zariski topology.
Choose an adapted chart
U\subset Z_{n}
as in
Proposition
1.4.9
, with local coordinates

\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1}

and deepest-stratum coordinates

\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}.

For the face
d_{k}\sigma
, use the adapted system whose blocks are

\mathbf{x}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{k-2},(\mathbf{x}_{k-1},\mathbf{x}_{k}),\mathbf{x}_{k+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1}.

This yields a
local
identification

\mathcal{N}_{k-1,k+1}|_{U_{0}}\simeq\mathcal{N}_{k-1}|_{U_{0}}\oplus\mathcal{N}_{k}|_{U_{0}},

used only to compute the local expression of the morphism below.

In these coordinates, the local computation of the morphism

\Pi_{\operatorname{deep}}:H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\to H_{{[n-2]}}(d_{k}\sigma)

gives, after transport by

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}\big|_{H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)},

the formula

(\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1})\longmapsto\bigl(\mathbf{u}_{0},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{k-2},(\mathbf{u}_{k-1},0),\mathbf{u}_{k+1},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}\bigr).

This is exactly the displayed bundle morphism.

On overlaps, Proposition
1.4.9
(
3
)
shows that the local formulas are compatible with the transition functions of the bundles

\mathcal{N}_{i}
and
\mathcal{N}_{k-1,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}
; hence they glue globally.

The Cartesianity statement is immediate from the explicit formula: the preimage of the
zero section in the target is obtained by imposing

\mathbf{u}_{i}=0\quad(i\neq k),

which is exactly the image of the summand
N_{k}
inside

\bigoplus_{i=0}^{n-1}\mathcal{N}_{i}.

∎

Lemma 1.4.15

(Functoriality of deepest strata under panel restriction)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}
be a regular flag and let

1\leq k\leq n-1
. Set
N_{k,k+1}:=N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+1}}
and write
\pi_{0}:N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}\to Z_{0}
for the bundle projection.

Let
\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}:H_{k}(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\sim}D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))
be the panel identification of
Lemma
1.3.7
. Then
\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}
restricts to an
isomorphism on deepest strata

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}\big|_{H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)}:\ H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)),

and, denoting its inverse by

\theta_{k,\sigma}:\ H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }H_{[n-1]}(\sigma),

the deepest-stratum projections of Lemma
1.4.6
fit into a commutative diagram of
S
-schemes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{0}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{k,\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\sigma}}

Proof.

The restriction

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{k,\sigma}\big|_{H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)}:H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))

is the case
K={[n-1]}\setminus\{k\}
of
Lemma
1.3.7
.
The compatibility of the projections is exactly the content of
Lemma
1.4.13
.
∎

Lemma 1.4.16

(Normal-bundle replacement: global comparison morphism, compatible with all strata)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}
and let
1\leq k\leq n-1
.
Write

N_{k,k+1}:=N_{Z_{k}/Z_{k+1}},\qquad N_{k,n}:=N_{Z_{k}/Z_{n}},

with projection
p:N_{k,n}\to Z_{k}
and inclusion
i_{k,n}:Z_{k}\hookrightarrow Z_{n}
.

(1) Global comparison morphism.

There exists a canonical morphism

(1.4.16.a)

\Pi=\Pi_{k,\sigma}:\ D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow D(d_{k}\sigma)

over
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
characterized by the commutative diagram

\textstyle{D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}}
\textstyle{D(d_{k}\sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\rho_{d}}
\textstyle{N_{k,n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{(i_{k,n}\circ p)\times\operatorname{id}}
\textstyle{Z_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S},}

where
\rho_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}
and
\rho_{d}
are the structure morphisms of the higher deformation spaces.

(2) Compatibility with all boundary strata.

For every subset
K\subset{[n-2]}
, the morphism
\Pi
induces by restriction a canonical morphism

\Pi_{K}:\ H_{K}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow H_{K}(d_{k}\sigma),

and these restrictions are compatible with the inclusions of strata: for every
K\subset{[n-2]}
and every

i\in{[n-2]}\setminus K
, the square

\textstyle{H_{K\cup\{i\}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{K\cup\{i\}}}
\textstyle{H_{K\cup\{i\}}(d_{k}\sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{K}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{K}}
\textstyle{H_{K}(d_{k}\sigma)}

commutes, where the vertical maps are the closed immersions of iterated intersections.

(3) Base change description of the strata maps.

For every subset
K\subset{[n-2]}
, write

C_{K}:=\bigcap_{i\in K}\{t_{i}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S},\qquad\iota_{K}:\mathbb{A}^{n-1-|K|}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }C_{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}.

Then
H_{K}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))=D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}C_{K}
and similarly for
d_{k}\sigma
.
Since
\Pi
is a morphism over
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
, it satisfies

\Pi^{-1}\bigl(H_{K}(d_{k}\sigma)\bigr)=H_{K}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)),

and the induced map

\Pi_{K}:\ H_{K}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow H_{K}(d_{k}\sigma)

is
exactly
the base change of
\Pi
along
\iota_{K}
:

\Pi_{K}\;=\;\Pi\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}C_{K}.

Equivalently, for every inclusion
K\subset K\cup\{i\}
the square

\textstyle{H_{K\cup\{i\}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{K\cup\{i\}}}
\textstyle{H_{K\cup\{i\}}(d_{k}\sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{K}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{K}}
\textstyle{H_{K}(d_{k}\sigma)}

is cartesian, and the maps are compatible with all iterated intersections.

(4) Open stratum.

Under the canonical identifications of open strata (Lemma
1.4.4
),
the restriction of
\Pi
to the open locus is

\Pi|_{U}=f_{U}:=(i_{k,n}\circ p)\times\operatorname{id}_{(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n-1}}:U_{{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)}\simeq N_{k,n}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n-1}\to U_{d_{k}\sigma}\simeq Z_{n}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n-1}.

(5) Deepest stratum.

On the deepest stratum, the restriction
\Pi_{{[n-2]}}
is the base change of
\Pi
along

0\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
, and its geometry is described globally by
Lemma
1.4.14
.

In particular, the restriction
\Pi_{[n-2]}
defines a canonical morphism

\Pi_{\operatorname{deep}}:=\Pi_{{[n-2]}}:\ H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow H_{{[n-2]}}(d_{k}\sigma).

Writing
s_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}:N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}\to H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))
and

s_{d}:Z_{0}\to H_{{[n-2]}}(d_{k}\sigma)
for the zero sections, there is a Cartesian square

\textstyle{N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{s_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}}
\textstyle{Z_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{d}}
\textstyle{H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{\operatorname{deep}}}
\textstyle{H_{{[n-2]}}(d_{k}\sigma),}

equivalently: for every
z\in Z_{0}
, if
0\in H_{{[n-2]}}(d_{k}\sigma)_{z}
denotes the zero vector, then

\Pi_{\operatorname{deep},z}^{-1}(0)\subset H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))_{z}\quad\text{is the image of the zero section }(N_{k,k+1})_{z}\hookrightarrow H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))_{z}.

Proof.

let

\rho_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}:D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\to N_{k,n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S},\qquad\rho_{d}:D(d_{k}\sigma)\to Z_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}

be the structure morphisms. Define

f:=\bigl((i_{k,n}\circ p)\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}\bigr):N_{k,n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\longrightarrow Z_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}.

(1) Construction of
\Pi
.

We apply the universal property of
D(d_{k}\sigma)

(
[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4]
) to the composite

h:=f\circ\rho_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}:D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow Z_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}.

Write the vertices of
d_{k}\sigma
as

W^{\prime}_{j}=\begin{cases}Z_{j}&(0\leq j\leq k-1),\\
Z_{j+1}&(k\leq j\leq n-1).\end{cases}

For
1\leq r\leq n-1
, set

D_{r}:=Z_{n}\times\{t_{n-1-r}=0\}\subset Z_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S},\qquad X_{r}:=W^{\prime}_{n-1-r}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\subset Z_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}.

Then
D(d_{k}\sigma)
represents morphisms
u:T\to Z_{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
such that
each
u^{-1}(D_{r})
is Cartier and

u^{-1}(D_{r}^{\prime})\subset u^{-1}(X_{r})\qquad(1\leq r\leq n-1).

For the Cartier condition, one has

h^{-1}(D_{r})=\rho_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}^{-1}\bigl(N_{k,n}\times\{t_{n-1-r}=0\}\bigr)=H_{n-1-r}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)),

which is an effective Cartier divisor by Lemma
1.4.3
.

For the incidence condition, write the vertices of
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)
as

W_{j}=\begin{cases}N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{j}}&(0\leq j\leq k),\\
N_{Z_{k}/Z_{j+1}}&(k+1\leq j\leq n-1).\end{cases}

Since
D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))
is the higher deformation space attached to this flag,
its defining incidence condition gives

h^{-1}(D_{r}^{\prime})\subset\rho_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}^{-1}(W_{n-1-r}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}).

It therefore suffices to show

W_{j}\subset(i_{k,n}\circ p)^{-1}(W^{\prime}_{j})\subset N_{k,n}\qquad(0\leq j\leq n-2).

If
j<k
, then
W^{\prime}_{j}=Z_{j}
, so

(i_{k,n}\circ p)^{-1}(W^{\prime}_{j})=p^{-1}(Z_{j})=N_{k,n}|_{Z_{j}},

and

W_{j}=N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{j}}\hookrightarrow N_{k,n}|_{Z_{j}}

by Lemma
1.3.2
.

If
j=k
, then
W^{\prime}_{k}=Z_{k+1}
, and since
i_{k,n}\circ p
factors through

Z_{k}\subset Z_{k+1}
, one has

(i_{k,n}\circ p)^{-1}(W^{\prime}_{k})=N_{k,n}.

Moreover

W_{k}=N_{k,k+1}\hookrightarrow N_{k,n}

again by Lemma
1.3.2
.

If
j>k
, then
W^{\prime}_{j}=Z_{j+1}
, again containing
Z_{k}
, hence

(i_{k,n}\circ p)^{-1}(W^{\prime}_{j})=N_{k,n},

and

W_{j}=N_{Z_{k}/Z_{j+1}}\hookrightarrow N_{k,n}

by Lemma
1.3.2
.

Thus the universal property yields a unique morphism

\Pi:D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow D(d_{k}\sigma)

such that

\rho_{d}\circ\Pi=f\circ\rho_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}.

Composing with the projection to
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
shows that
\Pi
is a morphism over
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
.
This proves (1).

(2) and (3).

For each
i\in[n-2]
, let

C_{i}:=\{t_{i}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}.

Because
\Pi
is a morphism over
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
, one has

\Pi^{-1}\bigl(D(d_{k}\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}C_{i}\bigr)=D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}C_{i}.

By definition,

H_{i}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))=D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}C_{i},\qquad H_{i}(d_{k}\sigma)=D(d_{k}\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}C_{i}.

Hence for every subset
K\subset[n-2]
, writing

C_{K}:=\bigcap_{i\in K}C_{i}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S},

one gets

H_{K}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))=D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}C_{K},\qquad H_{K}(d_{k}\sigma)=D(d_{k}\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}C_{K},

and therefore

\Pi^{-1}\bigl(H_{K}(d_{k}\sigma)\bigr)=H_{K}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)).

The induced morphism

\Pi_{K}:H_{K}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow H_{K}(d_{k}\sigma)

is exactly the base change of
\Pi
along
C_{K}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
.
In particular, for every inclusion
K\subset K\cup\{i\}
, the square

\textstyle{H_{K\cup\{i\}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{K\cup\{i\}}}
\textstyle{H_{K\cup\{i\}}(d_{k}\sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{H_{K}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{K}}
\textstyle{H_{K}(d_{k}\sigma)}

is Cartesian.

Finally, the ordered coordinates
(t_{0},\dots,t_{n-2})
identify

C_{K}
with
\mathbb{A}^{n-1-|K|}
, yielding the displayed form

\Pi_{K}=\Pi\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}C_{K}.

(4) Open stratum.

Let

U:=(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n-1}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}.

By Lemma
1.4.4
,

D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}U\simeq N_{k,n}\times U,\qquad D(d_{k}\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}U\simeq Z_{n}\times U.

Under these identifications, the relation

\rho_{d}\circ\Pi=f\circ\rho_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}

becomes

\Pi|_{U}=((i_{k,n}\circ p)\times\operatorname{id}_{U}),

which is the required formula.

(5) Deepest stratum.

The deepest stratum is the fiber over
0\in\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
, hence

\Pi_{\operatorname{deep}}=\Pi_{[n-2]}=\Pi\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}\{0\}.

To identify its geometry, combine Lemma
1.4.15

with Lemma
1.4.14
. The first gives an isomorphism

\theta_{k,\sigma}:H_{[n-2]}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\xrightarrow{\sim}H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)

compatible with the projections to
N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}
and
Z_{0}
, and its explicit
local formula shows that

\theta_{k,\sigma}\circ s_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}=s_{k},

where
s_{k}:N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}\to H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)
is the inclusion of the
k
-th
summand used in Lemma
1.4.14
. Under this identification,

\Pi_{\operatorname{deep}}
becomes the morphism
\widetilde{\Pi}_{\operatorname{deep}}
of
Lemma
1.4.14
. Transporting the Cartesian square from that lemma
back along
\theta_{k,\sigma}
yields

\textstyle{N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\pi_{0}}
\scriptstyle{s_{{\operatorname{Sp}}}}
\textstyle{Z_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{s_{d}}
\textstyle{H_{[n-2]}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\Pi_{\operatorname{deep}}}
\textstyle{H_{[n-2]}(d_{k}\sigma).}

This is exactly the required statement.
∎

Remark 1.4.17

(Intrinsic form of
\Pi_{\operatorname{deep}}
)
.

Lemma
1.4.14
gives a stronger description than
Lemma
1.4.16
(2).
Under the canonical direct-sum identifications of the deepest strata, the morphism

\Pi_{\operatorname{deep}}:H_{{[n-2]}}({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow H_{{[n-2]}}(d_{k}\sigma)

is the identity on the summands corresponding to indices different from
k-1,k
, and on the skipped stage it is
the composite

N_{Z_{k-1}/Z_{k}}|_{Z_{0}}\oplus N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}\longrightarrow N_{Z_{k-1}/Z_{k}}|_{Z_{0}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{k-1}/Z_{k+1}}|_{Z_{0}}.

In particular,
\Pi_{\operatorname{deep}}
is fiberwise linear over
\pi_{0}:N_{k,k+1}|_{Z_{0}}\to Z_{0}
.

Remark 1.4.18

(Why the global comparison morphism
\Pi
is the right one)
.

The morphism

\Pi:D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow D(d_{k}\sigma)

is the canonical comparison map between the two global deformation spaces attached to
panel restriction and simplicial face thanks to the universal property of the deformation space (see
[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4]
).

On the open stratum,
\Pi
is simply

(i_{k,n}\circ p)\times\operatorname{id}

by Lemma
1.4.16
(4).
On the deepest stratum, its behavior is governed by
Lemma
1.4.14
.

One could instead consider the base-changed morphism

D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma))\longrightarrow D(d_{k}\sigma)\times_{Z_{n}}Z_{k},

but this auxiliary map is generally poorly behaved: its fiber dimension typically jumps between the generic
and special fibers, so it is not the natural object for the later intersection-theoretic constructions.
The point of
\Pi
is precisely that it is the canonical global morphism compatible with the simplicial geometry
of the deformation spaces.

Remark 1.4.19

(Sanity check of Lemmas
1.4.4
and
1.4.6
in the Rost case
n=2
)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow Z_{2})
be a flag of regular closed immersions between smooth
S
-schemes,
and write
p_{\sigma}:D(\sigma)\to\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
and
q_{\sigma}:D(\sigma)\to Z_{2}
.
Denote the boundary components by
H_{0}=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(t_{0}=0)
and
H_{1}=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(t_{1}=0)
, and the deepest stratum by

H_{\{0,1\}}=H_{0}\cap H_{1}=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(0,0)
.

(1) Generic locus.

Lemma
1.4.4
specializes to the canonical identification

D(\sigma)\big|_{(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}}\ \simeq\ Z_{2}\times_{S}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}.

This is the expected “nothing happens away from the coordinate axes” statement, and it matches Rost’s
functorial identifications of the restrictions to
\mathbb{A}^{1}\times(\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\})
and

(\mathbb{A}^{1}\setminus\{0\})\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
in
[
Ros96
, 10.0.1-10.0.2]
(see also the
\mathbb{A}^{2}
-deformation formalism of

[
DM24
, §6]
).

(2) Axes and codimension-one strata.

Still in the regular/smooth setting, Rost identifies the restriction of
D(\sigma)
to the coordinate axes by

[
Ros96
, 10.0.3-10.0.4]
; in the notation of Section
1
this can be read as

D(\sigma)\big|_{t_{0}=0}\ \simeq\ D\!\bigl(N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}}\bigr),\qquad D(\sigma)\big|_{t_{1}=0}\ \simeq\ D\!\bigl(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}}\big|_{Z_{0}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}}\bigr),

compatible with the stratum computations in the smooth case in
[
DM24
, Thm. 6.3, Prop. 6.5]
.
In particular, the components
H_{0}
and
H_{1}
are precisely the (simple) deformation hypersurfaces along these axes.

(3) The deepest stratum.

Lemma
1.4.6
(1) recovers Rost’s assertion that the projection

D(\sigma)\big|_{(0,0)}\to Z_{2}
factors through
Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{2}

[
Ros96
, 10.0.5]
.
Moreover Corollary
1.4.11
says that
H_{\{0,1\}}\to Z_{0}
is canonically a vector bundle
of rank
\rk(N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}})+\rk(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}})
, globally split as

H_{\{0,1\}}\ \simeq\ \mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\bigl(N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\bigr)_{|Z_{0}}\ \oplus\ \bigl(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}}\bigr)_{|Z_{0}}\Bigr).

This agrees with Rost’s identification of the fiber at
(0,0)
with the corresponding iterated-normal description

[
Ros96
, 10.0.5]
(and with the canonical “deepest stratum” description provided by the
V
-construction in

[
DM24
, §6]
).

1.5.
Functorialities: confluences/degeneracies and the initial face

We collect the precise geometric compatibilities satisfied by the assignment
\sigma\mapsto D(\sigma)
.
We have strict compatibilities for degeneracies and for coordinate
panel restrictions
t_{k}=0
(which correspond to specialization flags
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{k}(\sigma)
).
These operations should not be confused with the simplicial faces
d_{k}
of the nerve.

Lemma 1.5.1

(Degeneracies are given by parameter confluence)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}
, and let

0\leq k\leq n
. Write
u_{0},\dots,u_{n-1}
for the coordinates on
\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
and

t_{0},\dots,t_{n}
for those on
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
, and set

f_{k}:=\operatorname{id}_{Z_{n}}\times\mu_{k}:\ Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}\longrightarrow Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}.

Then there is a canonical isomorphism of
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
-schemes

\vartheta_{k,\sigma}:\ D(s_{k}\sigma)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }f_{k}^{*}D(\sigma).

Under
\vartheta_{k,\sigma}
, the boundary components behave as follows.

•

If
0\leq k\leq n-1
, then

H_{j}(s_{k}\sigma)\ \cong\ \begin{cases}f_{k}^{*}H_{j}(\sigma)&j<k,\\[2.0pt]
p_{s_{k}\sigma}^{-1}(\{t_{k}=0\})&j=k,\\[2.0pt]
p_{s_{k}\sigma}^{-1}(\{t_{k+1}=0\})&j=k+1,\\[2.0pt]
f_{k}^{*}H_{j-1}(\sigma)&j\geq k+2.\end{cases}

Equivalently,

f_{k}^{*}H_{k}(\sigma)=H_{k}(s_{k}\sigma)+H_{k+1}(s_{k}\sigma)

as effective Cartier divisors, with underlying closed subset

\{t_{k}=0\}\cup\{t_{k+1}=0\}.

•

If
k=n
, then

H_{j}(s_{n}\sigma)\cong f_{n}^{*}H_{j}(\sigma)\qquad(0\leq j\leq n-1),\qquad H_{n}(s_{n}\sigma)=p_{s_{n}\sigma}^{-1}(\{t_{n}=0\}).

Proof.

Set

X:=Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S},\qquad X^{\prime}:=Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}.

For
\sigma
, let

X_{i}:=Z_{n-i}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\subset X,\qquad D_{i}:=Z_{n}\times_{S}\{u_{n-i}=0\}\subset X\qquad(1\leq i\leq n),

and write
D_{i}^{\prime}:=\sum_{r\geq i}D_{r}
.

For
s_{k}\sigma
, write
\widetilde{X}_{j},\widetilde{D}_{j},\widetilde{D}_{j}^{\prime}
for the analogous
deformation data on
X^{\prime}
.

Assume first that
k<n
. Since
s_{k}\sigma
repeats the vertex
Z_{k}
, one has for

1\leq i\leq n

f_{k}^{-1}(X_{i})=Z_{n-i}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}=\begin{cases}\widetilde{X}_{i}&i\leq n-k,\\
\widetilde{X}_{i+1}&i\geq n-k+1.\end{cases}

Moreover
D_{i}^{\prime}
is pulled back from the divisor

\{u_{0}\cdots u_{n-i}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}.

Using

\mu_{k}^{*}(u_{j})=t_{j}\quad(j<k),\qquad\mu_{k}^{*}(u_{k})=t_{k}t_{k+1},\qquad\mu_{k}^{*}(u_{j})=t_{j+1}\quad(j>k),

we get

f_{k}^{-1}(D_{i}^{\prime})=\begin{cases}\operatorname{div}(t_{0}\cdots t_{n+1-i})=\widetilde{D}_{i}^{\prime}&i\leq n-k,\\[2.0pt]
\operatorname{div}(t_{0}\cdots t_{n-i})=\widetilde{D}_{i+1}^{\prime}&i\geq n-k+1.\end{cases}

Thus the pullback datum
\bigl(f_{k}^{-1}(X_{i}),f_{k}^{-1}(D_{i}^{\prime})\bigr)_{1\leq i\leq n}
is obtained
from the datum defining
D(s_{k}\sigma)
by omitting the single index
n+1-k
.
This omitted incidence condition is redundant, because

\widetilde{X}_{n-k}=\widetilde{X}_{n+1-k}=Z_{k}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}

and

\widetilde{D}^{\prime}_{n-k}\supseteq\widetilde{D}^{\prime}_{n+1-k}.

Hence the condition at
n-k
already implies the one at
n+1-k
. Therefore

D(s_{k}\sigma)
and
f_{k}^{*}D(\sigma)
represent the same subfunctor of

\mathrm{Hom}(-,X^{\prime})
, so the universal property
[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4]
yields a
canonical isomorphism

\vartheta_{k,\sigma}:D(s_{k}\sigma)\xrightarrow{\sim}f_{k}^{*}D(\sigma).

If
k=n
, then
\mu_{n}
is the projection forgetting
t_{n}
. The simplex
s_{n}\sigma

repeats the final vertex
Z_{n}
, so the extra center is
\widetilde{X}_{1}=X^{\prime}
, and its
incidence condition is automatic. All remaining centers and summed divisors are simply the
pullbacks of those for
\sigma
, so the same universal property again gives a canonical
isomorphism
\vartheta_{n,\sigma}
.

Finally, the boundary statement is immediate from the definition

H_{j}(\tau)=p_{\tau}^{-1}(\{t_{j}=0\})

and Lemma
1.1.4
. For
k<n
, the divisor

\mu_{k}^{-1}(\{u_{k}=0\})
is
\{t_{k}=0\}\cup\{t_{k+1}=0\}
, while all other coordinate
hyperplanes pull back to a single coordinate hyperplane; this gives the displayed formulas
and the equality

f_{k}^{*}H_{k}(\sigma)=H_{k}(s_{k}\sigma)+H_{k+1}(s_{k}\sigma).

For
k=n
, the first
n
boundary components are unchanged under pullback, and

\{t_{n}=0\}
gives the extra component.
∎

Lemma 1.5.2

(Degeneracy effect on the open stratum)
.

Let
\sigma\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}
and
0\leq k\leq n
. With notation as in
Lemma
1.5.1
, write
U_{\tau}:=D(\tau)\setminus H(\tau)
.

(1)

If
0\leq k\leq n-1
, then under the canonical isomorphism

\vartheta_{k,\sigma}:D({s_{k}\sigma})\xrightarrow{\sim}(\operatorname{id}_{Z_{n}}\times\mu_{k})^{*}D(\sigma)

one has a canonical identification

U_{s_{k}\sigma}\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ (\operatorname{id}_{Z_{n}}\times\mu_{k})^{*}U_{\sigma}.

(2)

If
k=n
, then
\vartheta_{n,\sigma}
identifies
U_{s_{n}\sigma}
with

(\operatorname{id}_{Z_{n}}\times\mu_{n})^{*}U_{\sigma}\ \cap\ \{t_{n}\neq 0\},

hence canonically
U_{s_{n}\sigma}\simeq U_{\sigma}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Proof.

By Lemma
1.5.1
, the isomorphism
\vartheta_{k,\sigma}
identifies the boundary
divisor as

H(s_{k}\sigma)=\begin{cases}(\operatorname{id}\times\mu_{k})^{*}H(\sigma)&(0\leq k\leq n-1),\\
(\operatorname{id}\times\mu_{n})^{*}H(\sigma)\ \cup\ \{t_{n}=0\}&(k=n).\end{cases}

Taking complements inside
D(s_{k}\sigma)
and transporting along
\vartheta_{k,\sigma}
yields

U_{s_{k}\sigma}=\begin{cases}(\operatorname{id}\times\mu_{k})^{*}(D(\sigma)\setminus H(\sigma))=(\operatorname{id}\times\mu_{k})^{*}U_{\sigma}&(0\leq k\leq n-1),\\[2.0pt]
(\operatorname{id}\times\mu_{n})^{*}U_{\sigma}\cap\{t_{n}\neq 0\}&(k=n).\end{cases}

For
k=n
,
\mu_{n}
is the projection
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}\to\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
forgetting
t_{n}
, so

(\operatorname{id}\times\mu_{n})^{*}U_{\sigma}\simeq U_{\sigma}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
, and intersecting with
\{t_{n}\neq 0\}
gives

U_{s_{n}\sigma}\simeq U_{\sigma}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
canonically.
∎

Remark 1.5.3
.

For
0\leq k\leq n-1
, one has
\mu_{k}^{-1}((\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n})=(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n+1}
, hence
Lemma
1.4.4
gives
U_{s_{k}\sigma}\simeq Z_{n}\times_{S}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n+1}
.
For
k=n
,
\mu_{n}^{-1}((\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n})=(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}
(since
\mu_{n}
forgets
t_{n}
),
and Lemma
1.5.2
(2) gives

U_{s_{n}\sigma}\simeq U_{\sigma}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}\simeq Z_{n}\times_{S}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n+1}
.
Thus
U_{s_{k}\sigma}\simeq Z_{n}\times_{S}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n+1}
for all
0\leq k\leq n
.

Lemma 1.5.4

(Product formula for the initial face)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})
be a flag with
n\geq 1
.
Let
\Omega_{0}:=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\setminus\{t_{0}=0\}\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
be the open chart where the first coordinate is invertible.

(1)

There is a canonical isomorphism of schemes over
\Omega_{0}
:

\kappa_{\sigma}:\ D(\sigma)|_{\Omega_{0}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ D(d_{0}\sigma)\times_{S}\mathbb{G}_{m}.

(2)

This isomorphism induces an isomorphism of
ordered SNC pairs
:

\kappa_{\sigma}:\bigl(D(\sigma)|_{\Omega_{0}},\;H_{\bullet}(\sigma)|_{\Omega_{0}}\bigr)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\bigl(D(d_{0}\sigma)\times_{S}\mathbb{G}_{m},\;H_{\bullet}(d_{0}\sigma)\times_{S}\mathbb{G}_{m}\bigr).

Specifically,
H_{0}(\sigma)|_{\Omega_{0}}=\varnothing
, and for
k\geq 1
, the divisor
H_{k}(\sigma)|_{\Omega_{0}}
maps to
H_{k-1}(d_{0}\sigma)\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

Proof.

Set
X:=Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
and recall the deformation datum for
\sigma
:

X_{i}=Z_{n-i}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\subset X,\qquad D_{i}=Z_{n}\times_{S}\{t_{n-i}=0\}\subset X\qquad(1\leq i\leq n),

and
D_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{j\geq i}D_{j}
, i.e.
D_{i}^{\prime}
is pulled back from the monomial divisor

\{t_{0}t_{1}\cdots t_{n-i}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
.

Restrict to
\Omega_{0}
(so
t_{0}
is invertible). Then
D_{n}=Z_{n}\times\{t_{0}=0\}
becomes empty, hence
D_{n}^{\prime}=\varnothing
.
For
1\leq i\leq n-1
we have

D_{i}^{\prime}|_{\Omega_{0}}\ \text{ pulled back from }\ \{t_{1}\cdots t_{n-i}=0\}\subset\Omega_{0}.

In particular, the incidence condition for the index
i=n
in the universal property

[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4]
is vacuous on
\Omega_{0}
, so
D(\sigma)|_{\Omega_{0}}
is the multi-centered dilatation attached to the

truncated
datum
(X_{i}|_{\Omega_{0}},D_{i}^{\prime}|_{\Omega_{0}})_{1\leq i\leq n-1}
.

Now consider
\sigma^{\prime}:=d_{0}\sigma=(Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})
, which is an
(n-1)
-simplex.
Its deformation datum on
X^{\prime}:=Z_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
is

X_{i}^{\prime}=Z_{n-i}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S},\qquad(D_{i}^{\prime})^{\prime}\ \text{pulled back from }\ \{u_{0}u_{1}\cdots u_{(n-1)-i}=0\}\qquad(1\leq i\leq n-1).

Under the projection
\operatorname{pr}:\Omega_{0}\to\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
with
u_{j}=t_{j+1}
, we get

(\operatorname{id}_{Z_{n}}\times\operatorname{pr})^{-1}(X_{i}^{\prime})=X_{i}|_{\Omega_{0}},\qquad(\operatorname{id}_{Z_{n}}\times\operatorname{pr})^{-1}\bigl((D_{i}^{\prime})^{\prime}\bigr)=D_{i}^{\prime}|_{\Omega_{0}}\qquad(1\leq i\leq n-1).

Hence the truncated datum for
\sigma
on
\Omega_{0}
is exactly the pullback of the datum for
\sigma^{\prime}
along
\operatorname{pr}
.
By the base-change property/universal property of multi-centered dilatations
[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4]
,
this yields a canonical identification

D(\sigma)|_{\Omega_{0}}\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ (\operatorname{id}_{Z_{n}}\times\operatorname{pr})^{*}D(d_{0}\sigma)=\operatorname{pr}^{*}D(d_{0}\sigma),

and since
\Omega_{0}\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
, this is the same as
D(d_{0}\sigma)\times_{S}\mathbb{G}_{m}
.

For the boundary: on
\Omega_{0}
the divisor
H_{0}(\sigma)=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(t_{0}=0)
is empty, and for
k\geq 1
the equation

t_{k}=0
corresponds to
u_{k-1}=0
on the
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
-factor, so
H_{k}(\sigma)|_{\Omega_{0}}
identifies with

H_{k-1}(d_{0}\sigma)\times_{S}\mathbb{G}_{m}
. This is compatible with the ordered SNC lists, hence gives the claimed isomorphism of
ordered SNC pairs.
∎

Lemma 1.5.5

(Terminal face via the last panel and the zero section)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})
be a flag with
n\geq 1
.
Let

N_{n-1,n}:=N_{Z_{n-1}/Z_{n}}

be the normal bundle (a vector bundle on
Z_{n-1}
) with zero section
s:Z_{n-1}\hookrightarrow N_{n-1,n}
.
Then there is a canonical isomorphism of
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
-schemes

(s\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}})^{*}\,D\bigl({\operatorname{Sp}}_{n-1}(\sigma)\bigr)\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ D(d_{n}\sigma).

Consequently, composing with the last-panel identification

\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{n-1,\sigma}:\iota_{n-1}^{*}D(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\sim}D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{n-1}(\sigma))

from Lemma
1.3.7
, one obtains a canonical isomorphism

(s\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}})^{*}\iota_{n-1}^{*}D(\sigma)\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ D(d_{n}\sigma).

Proof.

For
k=n-1
, the specialized flag is

{\operatorname{Sp}}_{n-1}(\sigma)=\bigl(N_{n-1,n}|_{Z_{0}}\hookrightarrow N_{n-1,n}|_{Z_{1}}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow N_{n-1,n}|_{Z_{n-2}}\hookrightarrow N_{n-1,n}\bigr)\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n-1},

where
N_{n-1,n}|_{Z_{j}}=Z_{j}\times_{Z_{n-1}}N_{n-1,n}
.
Base change along the zero section
s:Z_{n-1}\hookrightarrow N_{n-1,n}
yields canonical identifications

Z_{n-1}\times_{N_{n-1,n}}(N_{n-1,n}|_{Z_{j}})\;=\;Z_{n-1}\times_{N_{n-1,n}}(Z_{j}\times_{Z_{n-1}}N_{n-1,n})\;\cong\;Z_{j}\qquad(0\leq j\leq n-1),

hence the pulled-back flag
s^{*}{\operatorname{Sp}}_{n-1}(\sigma)
is exactly the truncated flag

d_{n}\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n-1}).

Now compare deformation data.
By Definition
1.2.4
,
D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{n-1}(\sigma))
is the multi-centered deformation space
constructed from the ambient scheme
N_{n-1,n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
, with centers

(N_{n-1,n}|_{Z_{n-1-i}})\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\subset(N_{n-1,n})\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}\qquad(1\leq i\leq n-1),

and divisors pulled back from the coordinate hyperplanes on
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
exactly as for
D(-)
.
Pulling this deformation datum back along
s\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}
identifies it with the deformation datum
defining
D(d_{n}\sigma)
(same index set, same monomial divisors on the base, and the centers become

Z_{(n-1)-i}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
).

Because
s\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}}
is the identity on the
\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
-factor, the pulled-back divisors
s^{*}D_{i}
remain effective Cartier divisors on
Z_{n-1}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
.

Moreover, a morphism
f:T\to Z_{n-1}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
satisfies the pulled-back incidence
conditions (for the
d_{n}\sigma
-datum) if and only if
(s\times\operatorname{id})\circ f
satisfies the original
incidence conditions (for the
{\operatorname{Sp}}_{n-1}(\sigma)
-datum).
By the universal property
[
DM24
, Prop. 2.4]
applied to both dilatations, this bijection
on
T
-points yields a canonical isomorphism

(s\times\operatorname{id}_{\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}})^{*}\,D({\operatorname{Sp}}_{n-1}(\sigma))\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ D(d_{n}\sigma).

Composing with
\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{n-1,\sigma}
gives the second displayed isomorphism.
∎

Lemma 1.5.6

(Compatibilities for iterated panels and confluences)
.

Let
\sigma\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}
.

(1)

(Panels: associativity).

For
0\leq k<j\leq n-1
, the two iterated restrictions to

C_{k}\cap C_{j}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
identify canonically:

\iota_{j-1}^{*}\iota_{k}^{*}D(\sigma)\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ \iota_{k}^{*}\iota_{j}^{*}D(\sigma)\qquad\text{over }\mathbb{A}^{n-2}_{S}.

Equivalently both sides identify canonically with the single base change
along
\iota_{\{k,j\}}:\mathbb{A}^{n-2}_{S}\simeq\{t_{k}=t_{j}=0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
.

Under the identification
\iota_{\{k,j\}}:\mathbb{A}^{n-2}_{S}\simeq\{t_{k}=t_{j}=0\}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
of
Lemma
1.3.9
, this matches the canonical identification between the two iterated
specializations (with the standard index shift).

(2)

(Confluence + panels: base change).

Fix
0\leq\ell\leq n
, and let

\vartheta_{\ell,\sigma}:D(s_{\ell}\sigma)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }(\operatorname{id}_{Z_{n}}\times\mu_{\ell})^{*}D(\sigma)

be as in Lemma
1.5.1
.
For each panel
C_{k}=\{t_{k}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
(
0\leq k\leq n
), restricting along
C_{k}
yields a canonical isomorphism

\iota_{k}^{*}D(s_{\ell}\sigma)\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ (\operatorname{id}_{Z_{n}}\times(\mu_{\ell}|_{C_{k}}))^{*}D(\sigma),

where
\iota_{k}:\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}\simeq C_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
is the
k
-th panel embedding of
\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
.
Moreover
\mu_{\ell}
pulls back coordinate divisors as in Lemma
1.1.4
; in particular, for
\ell\leq n-1
,

\mu_{\ell}^{-1}(\{u_{\ell}=0\})=C_{\ell}\ \cup\ C_{\ell+1}.

(3)

(Critical panels factor through the boundary).

Assume
\ell\leq n-1
. Then the restrictions
\mu_{\ell}|_{C_{\ell}}
and
\mu_{\ell}|_{C_{\ell+1}}

factor through the panel
\{u_{\ell}=0\}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{n-1}_{S}
, and each forgets one coordinate on the source.

Proof.

(1) Write
C_{k}=\{t_{k}=0\}\subset\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
. Then

\iota_{k}^{*}D(\sigma)=D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}C_{k}.

Hence both iterated restrictions are (canonically) the triple fiber product

D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}C_{k}\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}C_{j}\ \cong\ D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}(C_{k}\cap C_{j})=D(\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}}C_{\{k,j\}},

and the desired identification is the associativity isomorphism for fiber products.
Transporting along
\Theta^{\mathrm{sp}}_{r,\sigma}
identifies both sides with the same panelization of
\sigma

(at indices
\{k,j\}
with the standard shift), so the statement about iterated specializations follows.

(2) Restrict
\vartheta_{\ell,\sigma}
along the closed immersion
C_{k}\hookrightarrow\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}
and use base change:

D(s_{\ell}\sigma)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}}C_{k}\ \cong\ \bigl((\operatorname{id}\times\mu_{\ell})^{*}D(\sigma)\bigr)\times_{\mathbb{A}^{n+1}_{S}}C_{k}\ \cong\ (\operatorname{id}\times(\mu_{\ell}|_{C_{k}}))^{*}D(\sigma).

The pullback of coordinate divisors is exactly Lemma
1.1.4
.

(3) On coordinate rings,
\mu_{\ell}^{\#}:\mathcal{O}_{S}[u_{0},\dots,u_{n-1}]\to\mathcal{O}_{S}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n}]
satisfies

u_{\ell}\mapsto t_{\ell}t_{\ell+1}
. On
C_{\ell}
we have
t_{\ell}=0
, hence
u_{\ell}
maps to
0
, so

\mu_{\ell}|_{C_{\ell}}
factors through the quotient by
(u_{\ell})
, i.e. through the panel
\{u_{\ell}=0\}
.
Moreover, on
C_{\ell}
the formula becomes

u_{i}\mapsto t_{i}\ (i<\ell),\qquad u_{\ell}\mapsto 0,\qquad u_{i}\mapsto t_{i+1}\ (i>\ell),

which does not involve
t_{\ell+1}
; equivalently, after identifying
C_{\ell}\simeq\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
by omitting
t_{\ell}
,
the map forgets the coordinate corresponding to
t_{\ell+1}
.
The case of
C_{\ell+1}
is analogous (now
t_{\ell+1}=0
and the map is independent of
t_{\ell}
).
Finally, pulling back
D(\sigma)
along these factorizations lands in
H_{\ell}(\sigma)=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(u_{\ell}=0)
by definition.
∎

Remark 1.5.7
.

Consequently, after identifying
D(s_{\ell}\sigma)\simeq(\operatorname{id}\times\mu_{\ell})^{*}D(\sigma)
, the restriction of

D(s_{\ell}\sigma)
to either critical panel
C_{\ell}
or
C_{\ell+1}
maps canonically into the boundary divisor

H_{\ell}(\sigma)=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(\{u_{\ell}=0\})
(rather than into all of
D(\sigma)
). This is the geometric input
used in Section
2
when analyzing the “
d_{\ell}s_{\ell}
-type” relations via localization boundary maps.

Remark 1.5.8

(Sanity check for
n=2
: recovering Rost’s formulas)
.

Let
\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow Z_{2})
and write
D:=D(\sigma)
with structure map

p_{\sigma}:D\to\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
and coordinates
(t_{0},t_{1})
on
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
(so
H_{k}=p_{\sigma}^{-1}(t_{k}=0)
).

(1) Generic fiber.

Lemma
1.4.4
gives a canonical identification

D|_{(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}}\ \simeq\ Z_{2}\times_{S}(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{2}.

This is the expected “nothing happens away from the boundary” statement and matches the corresponding
generic identification for Rost’s double deformation space (cf.
[
Ros96
, (10.0.1)-(10.0.2)]
, on the locus
where the relevant parameter(s) are invertible).

(2) The two coordinate panels (axes) and Rost’s specializations.

By Example
1.3.8
(equivalently, by the panel restriction/specialization formalism),
each axis of
\mathbb{A}^{2}_{S}
identifies
D
with an
ordinary
deformation space:

\displaystyle D|_{\{t_{0}=0\}}

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ D\!\bigl({\operatorname{Sp}}_{0}(\sigma)\bigr)\;=\;D\!\bigl(N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}}\bigr),

\displaystyle D|_{\{t_{1}=0\}}

\displaystyle\ \simeq\ D\!\bigl({\operatorname{Sp}}_{1}(\sigma)\bigr)\;=\;D\!\bigl(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}}|_{Z_{0}}\hookrightarrow N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}}\bigr).

These are precisely the two axis identifications in Rost’s notation (compare
[
Ros96
, (10.0.3)-(10.0.4)]
).

(3) The origin (deepest stratum) and the “normal-of-normal” bundle.

The deepest stratum

H_{\{0,1\}}\;=\;p_{\sigma}^{-1}(t_{0}=t_{1}=0)

is, by Lemma
1.4.6
, canonically a vector bundle over
Z_{0}
of rank

\rk(N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}})+\rk(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}})
, with a canonical zero section.
Zariski-locally on
Z_{0}
it identifies with

H_{\{0,1\}}\ \simeq\ \mathbf{V}\!\Bigl(\,(N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}})|_{Z_{0}}\ \oplus\ (N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}})|_{Z_{0}}\Bigr),

and globally it is governed by the usual exact sequence on
Z_{0}
,

0\to N_{Z_{0}/Z_{1}}\to N_{Z_{0}/Z_{2}}\to(N_{Z_{1}/Z_{2}})|_{Z_{0}}\to 0
.
This matches Rost’s identification of the fiber over
(0,0)
with a “normal-of-normal” bundle over
Z_{0}
,
namely
[
Ros96
, (10.0.5)]
.

(4) Open chart where one parameter is invertible: product decomposition.

On the chart
\Omega_{0}=\{t_{0}\neq 0\}\simeq\mathbb{G}_{m}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
, Lemma
1.5.4
yields

D|_{\Omega_{0}}\ \simeq\ D(d_{0}\sigma)\times_{S}\mathbb{G}_{m},\qquad d_{0}\sigma=(Z_{1}\hookrightarrow Z_{2}),

and this is an isomorphism of ordered SNC pairs:

H_{0}|_{\Omega_{0}}=\varnothing
and
H_{1}|_{\Omega_{0}}
corresponds to
H_{0}(d_{0}\sigma)\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
.
Geometrically: once
t_{0}
is invertible, the deepest center
Z_{0}
imposes no incidence condition, so only the
(single) deformation for
Z_{1}\subset Z_{2}
remains, together with the free
\mathbb{G}_{m}
-parameter.
This is the expected “forget the deepest center” behavior and is the analogue of Rost’s restriction
to the locus where the parameter corresponding to
Z_{0}
is invertible (compare
[
Ros96
, (10.0.1)]
;
see also
[
DM24
, §6.2.1]
for the same viewpoint via multi-centered deformations).

(5) Degeneracies as confluences (two-panel phenomenon).

For
n=2
, Lemma
1.5.1
says that the degeneracy
s_{\ell}\sigma
corresponds to
pulling back
D(\sigma)
along the confluence map
\mu_{\ell}
which replaces one parameter by a product of two.
Consequently, the pullback of the corresponding boundary divisor splits as a union of two adjacent coordinate
hyperplanes, exactly as recorded abstractly in Lemma
1.5.6
(2)-(3).
This is the geometric source of the “two critical panels mapping into one boundary component” phenomenon used later
when translating simplicial identities into localization boundary computations.

1.6.
Tor-independent base change for deformation to the normal cone

We now isolate the geometric input needed to glue the smooth and immersion parts in Section
5
.

Lemma 1.6.1

(Tor-independent base change for deformation to the normal cone)
.

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a
regular
closed immersion with ideal sheaf
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
.
Let
g:X^{\prime}\to X
be any morphism and form
Z^{\prime}:=Z\times_{X}X^{\prime}
with induced immersion
i^{\prime}:Z^{\prime}\hookrightarrow X^{\prime}
and ideal sheaf
\mathcal{J}_{Z^{\prime}}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}
.
Assume the square is Tor-independent, i.e.
\Tor^{\mathcal{O}_{X}}_{r}(\mathcal{O}_{Z},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})=0
for all
r>0
.
Then the natural map
\mathcal{J}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{J}_{Z^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism; we denote the common ideal sheaf by
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
.

Moreover, the canonical map of
\mathbf{Z}
-graded
\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}
-algebras

g^{*}\mathcal{R}_{X}(\mathcal{J})\ \longrightarrow\ \mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})

is an isomorphism, hence there is a canonical isomorphism over
X^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}

D(Z^{\prime}/X^{\prime})\ \xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\ D(Z/X)\times_{X}X^{\prime}.

Moreover, under this identification the open strata identify as
U^{\prime}\simeq X^{\prime}\times\mathbb{G}_{m}
and
U\simeq X\times\mathbb{G}_{m}

compatibly with pullback, and the special fibers identify as vector bundles

N_{Z^{\prime}/X^{\prime}}\simeq N_{Z/X}\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}
.

Here
\mathcal{R}_{X}(\mathcal{J})
denotes the Rees algebra

\mathcal{R}_{X}(\mathcal{J}):=\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}\mathcal{J}^{n}t^{-n}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}[t,t^{-1}],

so that
D(Z/X)=\Spec_{X\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}}\mathcal{R}_{X}(\mathcal{J})
.

Proof.

Write
\mathcal{J}\subset\mathcal{O}_{X}
for the ideal sheaf of
Z
.
Tensoring the exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{J}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z}\to 0

with
\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}
and using

\Tor^{\mathcal{O}_{X}}_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})=0

gives an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{J}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime}}\to 0.

Hence the natural map
\mathcal{J}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{J}_{Z^{\prime}}
is an isomorphism. We denote the
common ideal sheaf by
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
.

We must show that for all
n\geq 0
the canonical map

g^{*}(\mathcal{J}^{n})=\mathcal{J}^{n}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\longrightarrow(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n}

is an isomorphism; then
g^{*}\mathcal{R}_{X}(\mathcal{J})\simeq\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
, and taking

\Spec
gives the desired base change for
D(Z/X)
.

Step 1: Tor-independence for locally free
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
-modules.

Since the square is Tor-independent, we have
\mathcal{O}_{Z}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime}}
.
If
\mathcal{E}
is a locally free
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
-module, viewed as an
\mathcal{O}_{X}
-module via
i_{*}
, then

\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\simeq\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Z}}^{\mathbf{L}}\bigl(\mathcal{O}_{Z}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}^{\mathbf{L}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\bigr)\simeq\mathcal{E}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Z}}\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime}}

because
\mathcal{E}
is flat over
\mathcal{O}_{Z}
. In particular
\Tor_{r}^{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{E},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})=0
for
r>0
.

Step 2: conormal bundle and quasi-regularity of the pullback ideal.

Because
i
is regular,
\mathcal{J}/\mathcal{J}^{2}
is locally free on
Z
.
Tor-independence gives the canonical identification

(\mathcal{J}/\mathcal{J}^{2})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Z}}\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime}}\ \simeq\ \mathcal{J}^{\prime}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{2}.

In particular,
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{2}
is locally free on
Z^{\prime}
.

We next show that
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is quasi-regular. This is local on
X^{\prime}
.
Choose an affine open
U=\Spec(A)\subset X
on which
\mathcal{J}|_{U}
is generated by a regular
sequence
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}\in A
, and set

U^{\prime}:=U\times_{X}X^{\prime}=\Spec(B).

Since
f_{1},\dots,f_{r}
is a regular sequence, the Koszul complex

K_{A}(f_{\bullet}):=K_{A}(f_{1},\dots,f_{r})

is a finite free resolution of
A/(f_{1},\dots,f_{r})
. Hence

H_{p}\!\bigl(K_{B}(f_{\bullet}\otimes 1)\bigr)\cong\Tor_{p}^{A}(A/(f_{1},\dots,f_{r}),B)\qquad(p\geq 0),

where
K_{B}(f_{\bullet}\otimes 1)=K_{A}(f_{\bullet})\otimes_{A}B
.
By Tor-independence the right-hand side vanishes for
p>0
, so

f_{1}\otimes 1,\dots,f_{r}\otimes 1
is a Koszul-regular sequence in
B
generating

\mathcal{J}^{\prime}|_{U^{\prime}}
. Therefore
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
is a Koszul-regular ideal sheaf on
X^{\prime}
, hence
quasi-regular by
[
Sta25
, Tag 063E]
.

Since
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{2}
is locally free, the quasi-regular criterion

[
Sta25
, Tag 063H]
yields canonical isomorphisms

\operatorname{Sym}^{n}_{\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{2})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n+1}\qquad(n\geq 0).

Similarly, since
\mathcal{J}
is regular, it is also quasi-regular, so

\operatorname{Sym}^{n}_{\mathcal{O}_{Z}}(\mathcal{J}/\mathcal{J}^{2})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{J}^{n}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1}\qquad(n\geq 0).

Because symmetric powers commute with base change for locally free modules, we obtain
canonical isomorphisms

(\mathcal{J}^{n}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{Z}}\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime}}\;\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\;(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n+1}\qquad(n\geq 0).

Step 3: control of
\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n}
by induction.

We claim by induction on
n\geq 1
that:

(1)

\Tor_{1}^{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})=0
, and

(2)

the canonical map

(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n}

is an isomorphism.

For
n=1
, (2) is tautological:

\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{Z}\qquad\text{and hence}\qquad(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\ \simeq\ \mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime}}\ =\ \mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}/\mathcal{J}^{\prime}.

Condition (1) for
n=1
is exactly the Tor-independence hypothesis

\Tor^{\mathcal{O}_{X}}_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{Z},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})=0.

Assume the claim holds for
n
. Consider the short exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{J}^{n}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n}\to 0.

Tensoring with
\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}
remains exact because:

(1)

\Tor_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})=0
by induction, and

(2)

\Tor_{1}(\mathcal{J}^{n}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})=0
by Step 1, since

\mathcal{J}^{n}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1}
is locally free on
Z
.

Thus we obtain a short exact sequence

0\to(\mathcal{J}^{n}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\to(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\to(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n})\otimes\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\to 0.

Using Step 2 and the induction hypothesis, this identifies with

0\to(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n+1}\to\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n+1}\to\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n}\to 0,

hence

(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1})\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\simeq\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n+1}.

Moreover, applying the long exact Tor sequence to

0\to\mathcal{J}^{n}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n}\to 0

and using again Step 1 together with the induction hypothesis gives

\Tor_{1}^{\mathcal{O}_{X}}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n+1},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})=0.

This proves the induction.

Step 4: base change for
\mathcal{J}^{n}
.

From the exact sequence
0\to\mathcal{J}^{n}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}\to\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n}\to 0
and
\Tor_{1}(\mathcal{O}_{X}/\mathcal{J}^{n},\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}})=0
,
tensoring with
\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}
yields an exact sequence

0\to\mathcal{J}^{n}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\to\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n}\to 0,

so
\mathcal{J}^{n}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{X}}\mathcal{O}_{X^{\prime}}\simeq(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n}
. Equivalently,
g^{*}(\mathcal{J}^{n})\simeq(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n}
for all
n\geq 0
.

Step 5: Rees algebra and geometric consequences.

Thus
g^{*}\mathcal{R}_{X}(\mathcal{J})\simeq\mathcal{R}_{X^{\prime}}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})
as graded algebras, so

D(Z^{\prime}/X^{\prime})\simeq D(Z/X)\times_{X}X^{\prime}
over
X^{\prime}\times\mathbb{A}^{1}_{S}
.
The identifications on the open stratum (
t\neq 0
) follow by construction.
For the special fiber (
t=0
), Step 2 gives

\bigoplus_{n\geq 0}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{n+1}\;\simeq\;\operatorname{Sym}_{\mathcal{O}_{Z^{\prime}}}(\mathcal{J}^{\prime}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{2}),

so the special fiber is the vector bundle

\mathbf{V}\!\bigl((\mathcal{J}^{\prime}/(\mathcal{J}^{\prime})^{2})^{\vee}\bigr)\;\simeq\;\mathbf{V}\!\bigl((\mathcal{J}/\mathcal{J}^{2})^{\vee}\bigr)\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}=N_{Z/X}\times_{Z}Z^{\prime}.

∎

Lemma 1.6.2

(Flagwise Tor-independent base change for higher deformation spaces)
.

Let

\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}

be a flag of regular closed immersions between smooth
S
-schemes, and write
X:=Z_{n}
.
Let
g:Y\to X
be an
S
-morphism, and for
0\leq i\leq n
set

Y_{i}:=Y\times_{X}Z_{i}.

Assume that for every
0\leq i\leq n-1
, the cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{i+1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{i+1}}

is Tor-independent, i.e.

\Tor_{r}^{\mathcal{O}_{Z_{i+1}}}(\mathcal{O}_{Z_{i}},\mathcal{O}_{Y_{i+1}})=0\qquad(r>0).

Then each
Y_{i}\hookrightarrow Y_{i+1}
is a regular closed immersion. Hence

\sigma_{Y}:=(Y_{0}\hookrightarrow Y_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Y_{n}=Y)

is again a flag of regular closed immersions. Let
D(\sigma_{Y})
denote the higher deformation
space attached to
\sigma_{Y}
by the same construction as in
Definition
1.2.4
.

Then there is a canonical isomorphism of schemes over
Y\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}

D(\sigma_{Y})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }D(\sigma)\times_{X}Y.

It identifies the ordered boundary divisors
H_{k}
and therefore all iterated strata

H_{K}=\bigcap_{k\in K}H_{k}

scheme-theoretically.

If
g
is flat (in particular, if
g
is smooth), then the Tor-independence hypothesis is automatic.
For
n=1
, this recovers Tor-independent base change for the usual deformation to the normal cone.

Proof.

The argument is local along the flag. After choosing coordinates adapted to the
original flag and pulling them back along
g
, the higher deformation algebra
becomes an explicit monomial Rees algebra, and the claimed base-change
isomorphism is exactly the statement that these monomials are preserved under
the Tor-independent pullback.

Write
\mathbb{A}^{n}:=\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
, and for
0\leq i\leq n-1
put

T_{i}:=t_{0}t_{1}\cdots t_{i}.

We first prove the claim locally on
Y
, then glue.

Step 1: local adapted coordinates on the original flag.

Fix a point
y\in Y
, and let
x:=g(y)\in X=Z_{n}
.
Let
m\in\{0,\dots,n\}
be the smallest index such that
x\in Z_{m}

(with the convention
m=n
if
x\notin Z_{n-1}
).
After shrinking
X
around
x
, we may choose a Zariski-open neighbourhood

U\subset X
such that

U\cap Z_{i}=\varnothing\qquad(i<m).

By the same descending-induction argument as in
Proposition
1.4.9
, after shrinking
U
further if necessary,
there exist functions

\mathbf{x}_{i}=(x_{i,1},\dots,x_{i,r_{i}})\in\Gamma(U,\mathcal{O}_{U})^{r_{i}}\qquad(m\leq i\leq n-1)

such that, writing

U_{j}:=U\times_{X}Z_{j}\subset U\qquad(m\leq j\leq n),

one has

U_{j}=V(\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathbf{x}_{j+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})\qquad(m\leq j\leq n),

and the images of
\mathbf{x}_{i}
in the conormal sheaf

\mathfrak{I}_{U_{i}/U_{i+1}}/\mathfrak{I}_{U_{i}/U_{i+1}}^{2}

form a basis. In particular, for each
m\leq i\leq n-1
, the sequence
\mathbf{x}_{i}

is a regular sequence in
\mathcal{O}_{U_{i+1}}
generating the ideal of
U_{i}\subset U_{i+1}
.

Set

U_{Y}:=Y\times_{X}U,\qquad U_{j,Y}:=Y_{j}\times_{X}U=U_{Y}\times_{U}U_{j}.

Step 2: the pulled-back blocks are again adapted.

Fix
i
with
m\leq i\leq n-1
.
Since
U_{i}\hookrightarrow U_{i+1}
is cut out by the regular sequence
\mathbf{x}_{i}
,
the Koszul complex
K_{U_{i+1}}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
is a locally free resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}

as an
\mathcal{O}_{U_{i+1}}
-module.
Pull it back along
U_{i+1,Y}\to U_{i+1}
. The resulting complex is exactly the Koszul complex

K_{U_{i+1,Y}}(g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{i})

on the pulled-back sequence
g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{i}
.
Because the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i,Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i+1,Y}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{i+1}}

is Tor-independent, we have

H_{q}\!\bigl(K_{U_{i+1,Y}}(g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{i})\bigr)\cong\Tor_{q}^{\mathcal{O}_{U_{i+1}}}(\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}},\mathcal{O}_{U_{i+1,Y}})=0\qquad(q>0),

and

H_{0}\!\bigl(K_{U_{i+1,Y}}(g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{i})\bigr)\cong\mathcal{O}_{U_{i}}\otimes_{\mathcal{O}_{U_{i+1}}}\mathcal{O}_{U_{i+1,Y}}\cong\mathcal{O}_{U_{i,Y}}.

Hence
K_{U_{i+1,Y}}(g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{i})
is a resolution of
\mathcal{O}_{U_{i,Y}}
, so

g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{i}
is a regular sequence in
\mathcal{O}_{U_{i+1,Y}}
generating the ideal
of
U_{i,Y}\subset U_{i+1,Y}
.

Since the ideal is generated by a regular sequence of length
r_{i}
, its classes form a basis of

\mathcal{I}_{U_{i,Y}/U_{i+1,Y}}/\mathcal{I}_{U_{i,Y}/U_{i+1,Y}}^{2}
.
Hence the pulled-back blocks
g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{i}
are adapted coordinates.

In particular, each
U_{i,Y}\hookrightarrow U_{i+1,Y}
is a regular closed immersion of codimension
r_{i}
.

By descending induction on
j
, it follows that

U_{j,Y}=V(g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{j},g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{j+1},\dots,g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{n-1})\qquad(m\leq j\leq n).

Thus the pulled-back blocks
g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{i}
are adapted coordinates for the pulled-back flag

U_{m,Y}\hookrightarrow U_{m+1,Y}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow U_{n,Y}=U_{Y}.

This proves in particular that each
Y_{i}\hookrightarrow Y_{i+1}
is regular.

Step 3: corrected local presentation of the two deformation spaces.

For
0\leq j\leq m-1
, the closed subscheme
U_{j}\subset U
is empty, so its ideal
sheaf is
\mathcal{O}_{U}
. Equivalently, in the explicit multi-Rees algebra of
Definition
1.2.4
we have

I_{j}=\mathcal{O}_{U}\qquad(0\leq j\leq m-1),

while for
m\leq j\leq n-1
,

I_{j}=(\mathbf{x}_{j},\mathbf{x}_{j+1},\dots,\mathbf{x}_{n-1})\subset\mathcal{O}_{U}.

Set

T_{i}:=t_{0}t_{1}\cdots t_{i}\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

Then the explicit formula of Definition
1.2.4
gives

\mathcal{R}_{\sigma,U}=\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1}]\Bigl[T_{0}^{-1},\dots,T_{m-1}^{-1},\frac{\mathbf{x}_{m}}{T_{m}},\dots,\frac{\mathbf{x}_{n-1}}{T_{n-1}}\Bigr].

Indeed, for
j\geq i\geq m
one has

\frac{x_{j,a}}{T_{i}}=(t_{i+1}\cdots t_{j})\frac{x_{j,a}}{T_{j}},

so the generators
\mathbf{x}_{j}/T_{i}
with
j>i
are already generated by the blocks

\mathbf{x}_{j}/T_{j}
.

Introducing variables
v_{0},\dots,v_{m-1}
and

\mathbf{u}_{m},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}
, we obtain a presentation

D(\sigma)|_{U}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\Spec_{U}\!\Bigl(\mathcal{O}_{U}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1},v_{0},\dots,v_{m-1},\mathbf{u}_{m},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}]\Big/(T_{i}v_{i}-1)_{0\leq i\leq m-1},(\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i})_{m\leq i\leq n-1}\Bigr).

Applying the same computation to the pulled-back flag on
U_{Y}
, using the adapted
blocks
g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{i}
, yields

D(\sigma_{Y})|_{U_{Y}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\Spec_{U_{Y}}\!\Bigl(\mathcal{O}_{U_{Y}}[t_{0},\dots,t_{n-1},v_{0},\dots,v_{m-1},\mathbf{u}_{m},\dots,\mathbf{u}_{n-1}]\Big/(T_{i}v_{i}-1)_{0\leq i\leq m-1},(g^{*}\mathbf{x}_{i}-T_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i})_{m\leq i\leq n-1}\Bigr).

This algebra is exactly the pullback of the preceding one from
U
to
U_{Y}
.
Therefore

D(\sigma_{Y})|_{U_{Y}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }D(\sigma)|_{U}\times_{U}U_{Y}.

Step 4: gluing.

When
y
varies, the opens
U_{Y}
form a Zariski cover of
Y
.
On overlaps, the local isomorphisms above agree because they are obtained from the canonical
local presentations of the two deformation spaces by ordinary base change and restriction.
Hence they glue to a global canonical isomorphism

D(\sigma_{Y})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }D(\sigma)\times_{X}Y

over
Y\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}
.

Step 5: boundary divisors and strata.

Both sides are schemes over
Y\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}
, and for each
k\in[n-1]
the boundary divisor

H_{k}
is defined scheme-theoretically by the equation
t_{k}=0
.
Since the isomorphism is over
Y\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}
, it identifies each
H_{k}
, hence also every
iterated stratum

H_{K}=\bigcap_{k\in K}H_{k}

scheme-theoretically.

Finally, if
g
is flat, then every
Y_{i+1}\to Z_{i+1}
is flat, so each square above is
automatically Tor-independent. For
n=1
, the statement is exactly the ordinary
Tor-independent base change for the deformation to the normal cone.
∎

2.
Higher Gysin simplices for closed immersions

2.1.
Simplices, mapping spaces, and whiskering

We work over the fixed base scheme
S

and we consider a pointed
\infty
-category
\mathcal{C}
admitting finite limits.
We work with the Joyal model: thus
\mathcal{C}
is a quasicategory
(i.e. a simplicial set satisfying the inner horn-filling condition), and an

\infty
-functor
F:\mathcal{C}\to\mathcal{D}
means a morphism of simplicial sets.

2.1.1
.

Let us describe the global strategy following in Sections
2
-
4
.
We will work with a weak Borel-Moore theory (see Definition
2.2.12
), which to a smooth
S
-scheme
X
and an integer
n\in\mathbf{Z}
, associates an object
\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)\in\mathcal{C}
.
The aim of the whole section is to build a higher coherent contravariant functoriality out of this data associated with closed-immersions. We proceed in three stages.

In the current section, we associate to every

\sigma\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}

the
higher Gysin simplex
:

\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{simp}}(\sigma)\in\Map_{\mathcal{C}}\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{m}(Z_{n}),\mathcal{G}_{m}(Z_{0})\bigr)_{n-1}.

In Section
3
, we prove that these simplices satisfy the required face and degeneracy identities, canonically up to contractible choice. In Section
4
, we rigidify those contractible choices and obtain a strict simplicial map

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}:N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{C}.

Notations 2.1.2
.

Let

\operatorname{sSet}:=\Fun(\Delta^{\operatorname{op}},\operatorname{Set}).

For
n\geq 0
, the boundary of
\Delta^{n}
is the subobject

\partial\Delta^{n}:=\bigcup_{k=0}^{n}\delta^{k}(\Delta^{n-1})\subset\Delta^{n}.

For vertices (objects)
x,y\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
, we write
\Map_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)
for the mapping
space, defined as the usual Kan complex associated to
\mathcal{C}
. In particular,
\Map_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)
is a Kan complex and
its
0
-simplices are the edges
x\to y
in
\mathcal{C}
.
(See
[
Lur09b
, §1.2.2]
.)
Precisely, we take
\Map_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)
to be the
right mapping space

\Map^{R}_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)
of
[
Lur09b
, §1.2.2]
, so that pre- and post-composition induce
morphisms of simplicial sets functorially in both variables.

Convention 2.1.3

(Structural data vs. contractible-choice data)
.

We systematically distinguish two kinds of simplices.

(1) Structural simplices.

By
structural
data we mean simplices that are part of the chosen input.
In this paper, structural simplices include:

•

the functoriality homotopies and higher coherences built into the weak Borel-Moore theories,

•

the chosen inverse data for vector bundle projections,

•

the localization fiber sequences and their compatibility data,

•

and any simplices obtained from these by simplicial functoriality, whiskering, or composition.

Thus, structural simplices are part of the formal package and are regarded as fixed once the theory is fixed.

(2) Homotopies and higher coherences in mapping spaces.

A
homotopy
between parallel morphisms
f,g:x\to y
in
\mathcal{C}
means a

1
-simplex in the Kan complex
\Map_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)
with endpoints
f
and
g
.
More generally, an
n
-simplex in
\Map_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)
encodes an
n
-parameter family
of morphisms and higher coherences among lower-dimensional data.

(3) Contractible-choice data (“canonical up to contractible choice”).

Sometimes we use the fact that a certain extension or filler space is contractible.
Formally, if
i:A\hookrightarrow B
is a trivial cofibration of simplicial sets and
K
is a Kan complex,
then restriction induces a trivial Kan fibration

\Map_{\operatorname{sSet}}(B,K)\longrightarrow\Map_{\operatorname{sSet}}(A,K),

so the space of fillers of a given boundary datum is contractible.
Whenever we appeal to such a fact, we may choose a filler once and for all, and any later construction
depending on this choice is understood only up to the resulting contractible indeterminacy.

(4) Terminology.

We say
structural
when the simplex is part of the chosen input data, and

canonical up to contractible choice
when it is obtained only from a contractible filler space as in (3).

Definition 2.1.4

(Simplices with fixed endpoints)
.

Let
x,y\in\mathcal{C}_{0}
. For
n\geq 0
, an
n
-simplex from
x
to
y
(or
n
-arrow
)
means an element of
\Map_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)_{n}
, i.e. a simplicial map

\Delta^{n}\to\Map_{\mathcal{C}}(x,y)
.
Thus:

•

0
-simplices are morphisms
x\to y
,

•

1
-simplices are homotopies between morphisms,

•

higher simplices encode higher coherences.

Definition 2.1.5

(Whiskering)
.

Let
a:x^{\prime}\to x
and
b:y\to y^{\prime}
be morphisms in
\mathcal{C}
.
Functoriality of mapping spaces in each variable induces simplicial maps
