, apply Proposition
4.2.6
(3) to the
monomorphism

S^{\partial}_{n,n}\hookrightarrow S_{n,n}

and to

p_{\sigma,n}:E_{\sigma,n}\to S_{n,n}.

Using Lemma
4.2.18
(1), we obtain a contractible Kan
complex of extensions of
b_{\sigma,n}(u)
from
S^{\partial}_{n,n}
to
S_{n,n}
.
Choose one, and denote it by

t_{\sigma,n}\in\operatorname{Sec}(p_{\sigma,n})_{0}.

Assume inductively that
t_{\sigma,N}\in\operatorname{Sec}(p_{\sigma,N})_{0}
has been chosen and extends

b_{\sigma,N}(u)
.
By the last paragraph of the proof of
Lemma
4.2.18
, there is a canonical identification

E_{\sigma,N+1}\big|_{S_{n,N}}=E_{\sigma,N}.

Hence
t_{\sigma,N}
may be viewed as a section of
p_{\sigma,N+1}
over
S_{n,N}
.

Inside
S_{n,N+1}
, form the simplicial subset

T_{n,N+1}:=S_{n,N}\cup_{S^{\partial}_{n,N}}S^{\partial}_{n,N+1}.

The two sections

t_{\sigma,N}\ \text{on }S_{n,N},\qquad b_{\sigma,N+1}(u)\ \text{on }S^{\partial}_{n,N+1},

agree on the overlap
S^{\partial}_{n,N}
by construction, so they glue to a section

\widetilde{t}_{\sigma,N+1}:T_{n,N+1}\to E_{\sigma,N+1}.

Apply Proposition
4.2.6
(3) again to the monomorphism

T_{n,N+1}\hookrightarrow S_{n,N+1}

and to
p_{\sigma,N+1}
. Since
p_{\sigma,N+1}
satisfies the boundary lifting hypothesis by
Lemma
4.2.18
(1), the space of extensions of

\widetilde{t}_{\sigma,N+1}
to a section of
p_{\sigma,N+1}
is a contractible Kan complex.
Choose one, and denote it by

t_{\sigma,N+1}\in\operatorname{Sec}(p_{\sigma,N+1})_{0}.

Then
t_{\sigma,N+1}
restricts to
t_{\sigma,N}
on
S_{n,N}
and extends

b_{\sigma,N+1}(u)
on
S^{\partial}_{n,N+1}
.

By induction, we obtain a compatible family

(t_{\sigma,N})_{N\geq n}\in\varprojlim_{N\geq n}\operatorname{Sec}(p_{\sigma,N})_{0}=\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m}(\sigma)_{0}.

Write

x_{\sigma}:=(t_{\sigma,N})_{N\geq n}\in\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m}(\sigma)_{0}.

Step 3: build the filler in
E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}
.

Define

\widetilde{u}:=(\sigma,\eta)\in(E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}})_{n},\qquad\eta_{\alpha}:=\alpha^{*}(x_{\sigma})\in\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m}(\sigma\circ\alpha)_{0}

for every
\alpha:[r]\to[n]
.
Because the pullback maps on
\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m}(-)
are strictly functorial, the family

\eta=\{\eta_{\alpha}\}_{\alpha}
satisfies

\beta^{*}(\eta_{\alpha})=\eta_{\alpha\circ\beta}

for all
\beta:[s]\to[r]
. Hence
(\sigma,\eta)
is an
n
-simplex of
E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}
.

By construction,
p_{m}(\widetilde{u})=\sigma
.
If
\alpha:[r]\to[n]
is non-surjective, then for every
N\geq\max\{n,r\}
, the section

t_{\sigma,N}
restricts on
S^{\partial}_{n,N}
to
b_{\sigma,N}(u)
. Therefore the

N
-th truncation of
\alpha^{*}(x_{\sigma})
is exactly
u_{\alpha,\leq N}
, and hence

\alpha^{*}(x_{\sigma})=u_{\alpha}\qquad\text{in }\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m}(\sigma\circ\alpha)_{0}.

This shows that
\widetilde{u}
restricts to
u
on
\partial\Delta^{n}
.

Thus
\widetilde{u}
is the required lift.
∎

Proposition 4.2.24

(Existence of
1
-skeleton and global coherent sections)
.

Let

i:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\hookrightarrow N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}

be the inclusion, and form the pullback

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m,\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m,\sk_{1}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}

Then:

(1)

The simplicial set of sections

\operatorname{Sec}(p_{m,\sk_{1}})

is a contractible Kan complex.

(2)

In particular, there exists a simplicial map

s_{1,m}:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\longrightarrow E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}

such that

p_{m}\circ s_{1,m}=i.

(3)

For every such choice of
s_{1,m}
, let

\operatorname{Sec}_{s_{1,m}}(p_{m})

denote the simplicial set of lifts in the commutative square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces s_{1,m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces p_{m}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\operatorname{id}}

Then

\operatorname{Sec}_{s_{1,m}}(p_{m})

is a contractible Kan complex.

(4)

In particular, there exists a simplicial section

s_{m}:N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\longrightarrow E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}

such that

p_{m}\circ s_{m}=\operatorname{id}_{N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad s_{m}|_{\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})}=s_{1,m}.

Proof.

Since
p_{m}
has the right lifting property with respect to all boundary inclusions by
Proposition
4.2.23
, the same is true for
p_{m,\sk_{1}}
by base change.
Thus Proposition
4.2.6
(4) implies that

\operatorname{Sec}(p_{m,\sk_{1}})

is a contractible Kan complex. This proves (1) and hence (2).

Now fix
s_{1,m}
as in (2). Applying
Proposition
4.2.6
(3) to the monomorphism

i:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\hookrightarrow N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}},

the map

s_{1,m}:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\to E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}},

and

\operatorname{id}_{N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}}:N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\to N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}},

shows that

\operatorname{Sec}_{s_{1,m}}(p_{m})

is a contractible Kan complex. This proves (3), and any vertex gives the section
s_{m}

of (4).
∎

4.3.
Extracting coherent operator comparisons

Construction 4.3.1

(Coherent operator comparisons from the global coherent section)
.

Choose once and for all a vertex

\mathbf{s}_{m}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{s_{1,m}}(p_{m})_{0}

as in Proposition
4.2.24
, and let

s_{m}:N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\longrightarrow E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}

be the corresponding simplicial section.

Let

\sigma:\Delta^{n}\to N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}

and let

\alpha:[r]\to[n]

be a simplicial operator.
Write

s_{m}(\sigma)=(\sigma,\eta^{\sigma})\in(E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}})_{n},

and let

x_{\sigma}:=\eta^{\sigma}_{\operatorname{id}_{[n]}}\in\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m}(\sigma)_{0}

be the component indexed by
\operatorname{id}_{[n]}:[n]\to[n]
.
For
N\geq\max\{n,r\}
, let

t_{\sigma,N}\in\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m,\leq N}(\sigma)_{0}=\operatorname{Sec}(p_{\sigma,N})_{0}

be the image of
x_{\sigma}
under the canonical projection

\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m}(\sigma)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m,\leq N}(\sigma).

If
\alpha=\operatorname{id}_{[n]}
, define

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\operatorname{id},m}:=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)}.

Assume now that
\alpha\neq\operatorname{id}
. For
N\geq\max\{n,r\}
, evaluate
t_{\sigma,N}
on the
nondegenerate
1
-simplex

\lambda_{\alpha}:=\bigl(\operatorname{id}_{[n]}\xleftarrow{\alpha}\alpha\bigr)\in(S_{n,N})_{1}.

Applying Lemma
4.2.16
(b) yields an equivalence

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\alpha,m}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma\circ\alpha)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)

in
\Fun(\Delta^{r},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
.

For composable operators

[\ell]\xrightarrow{\beta}[r]\xrightarrow{\alpha}[n],

evaluating
t_{\sigma,N}
on the
2
-simplex

\lambda_{\alpha,\beta}:=\bigl(\operatorname{id}_{[n]}\xleftarrow{\alpha}\alpha\xleftarrow{\beta}\alpha\circ\beta\bigr)\in(S_{n,N})_{2}

and applying Lemma
4.2.16
(c) yields a chosen homotopy

\beta^{*}(c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\alpha,m})\circ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma\circ\alpha,\beta,m}\ \simeq\ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\alpha\circ\beta,m}.

(If one of
\alpha
,
\beta
, or
\alpha\circ\beta
is an identity, we take the
evident unit homotopy instead.)

These constructions are independent of
N
by compatibility of the truncation maps.
Indeed, if
N^{\prime}\geq N\geq\max\{n,r\}
, then the restriction map

\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m,\leq N^{\prime}}(\sigma)\longrightarrow\operatorname{Choices}^{\operatorname{coh}}_{m,\leq N}(\sigma)

sends
t_{\sigma,N^{\prime}}
to
t_{\sigma,N}
, and the simplices

\lambda_{\alpha}=\bigl(\operatorname{id}_{[n]}\xleftarrow{\alpha}\alpha\bigr),\qquad\lambda_{\alpha,\beta}=\bigl(\operatorname{id}_{[n]}\xleftarrow{\alpha}\alpha\xleftarrow{\beta}\alpha\circ\beta\bigr)

already belong to
S_{n,N}\subset S_{n,N^{\prime}}
.
Hence evaluating
t_{\sigma,N^{\prime}}
on these simplices restricts exactly to the evaluations of

t_{\sigma,N}
, both for the comparison edge and for the chosen composition homotopy.

Lemma 4.3.2

(Coherent operator comparisons)
.

The comparisons of
Construction
4.3.1

satisfy

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\operatorname{id},m}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)},

and the chosen homotopies for composable operators are compatible with triple compositions.

Proof.

This follows by evaluating the simplicial section

s_{m}:N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\to E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}

on the simplices

(\operatorname{id}_{[n]})\in(S_{n,N})_{0},\qquad(\operatorname{id}_{[n]}\xleftarrow{\alpha}\alpha)\in(S_{n,N})_{1},\qquad(\operatorname{id}_{[n]}\xleftarrow{\alpha}\alpha\xleftarrow{\beta}\alpha\circ\beta)\in(S_{n,N})_{2},

and their
3
-simplex analogues. The simplicial identities for
s_{m}
give exactly the required
identity, composition, and triple-composition compatibilities.
∎

4.4.
Purely simplicial rigidification

Notations 4.4.1

(Abstract rigidification data)
.

Let
K
be a simplicial set and let
\mathcal{C}
be a quasicategory.
For every simplex

\tau:\Delta^{r}\to K,

assume given a simplex

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)\in\Fun(\Delta^{r},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}_{0}.

For every simplicial operator

\alpha:[s]\to[r],

assume given an equivalence

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau\circ\alpha)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)

in
\Fun(\Delta^{s},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
, such that

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\operatorname{id},m}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)},

and such that these equivalences are equipped with chosen homotopies expressing
compatibility with composition, themselves compatible with triple compositions.

Normalization in dimension
1
.

For every
1
-simplex

\tau:\Delta^{1}\to K

and every proper injective

\alpha:[0]\hookrightarrow[1],

we require that

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau\circ\alpha)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)

is the identity under the tautological identification

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau\circ\alpha)=\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)

in
\Fun(\Delta^{0},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}=\mathcal{C}^{\simeq}
.

Remark 4.4.2

(Comparison-symbol conventions)
.

To avoid confusion, we use:

•

\varepsilon_{i,m}^{\sigma}
and
\upsilon_{j,m}^{\sigma}
for the face and degeneracy
comparisons coming from Sections
3
;

•

\epsilon^{(1)}_{\tau}
for the initial comparison data on the normalized
1
-skeleton;

•

\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}
for the comparison equivalences produced during rigidification;

•

\epsilon_{X,m}
for the Koszul automorphism from the inflation package.

Lemma 4.4.3

(Restriction to the boundary is a categorical fibration)
.

Let
\mathcal{C}
be a quasicategory and
n\geq 1
. Then restriction along

\partial\Delta^{n}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}
induces a categorical fibration

\rho_{n}:\Fun(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})\longrightarrow\Fun(\partial\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C}).

In particular, the induced map on maximal Kan complexes

\rho_{n}^{\simeq}:\Fun(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}\longrightarrow\Fun(\partial\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}

is a Kan fibration of simplicial sets.

Proof.

This is
[
Lur24
,
01F3
and
01F4
]
.

∎

Lemma 4.4.4

(Skeletal attachment by nondegenerate simplices)
.

Let
L
be a simplicial set and let
n\geq 1
. Write
L_{n}^{\mathrm{nd}}
for the set of
nondegenerate
n
-simplices of
L
. Then there is a pushout square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{\sigma\in L_{n}^{\mathrm{nd}}}\partial\Delta^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sk_{n-1}(L)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\displaystyle\coprod_{\sigma\in L_{n}^{\mathrm{nd}}}\Delta^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sk_{n}(L).}

Proof.

This is the standard description of the skeletal filtration of a simplicial set:

\sk_{n}(L)
is obtained from
\sk_{n-1}(L)
by attaching one copy of
\Delta^{n}
for each
nondegenerate
n
-simplex, along its boundary.
∎

Lemma 4.4.5

(Contractible space of composites in a Kan complex)
.

Let
X
be a Kan complex, and let

x\xrightarrow{u}y\xrightarrow{v}z

be composable edges in
X
. Then the simplicial set of
2
-simplices

\theta\in X_{2}

with

d_{2}(\theta)=u,\qquad d_{0}(\theta)=v

is a contractible Kan complex.

Proof.

This is folklore (see
[
Lur24
,
01PL
]
) but here’s a short proof. The simplicial set of the statement is the fiber of the restriction map

\Map_{\operatorname{sSet}}(\Delta^{2},X)\longrightarrow\Map_{\operatorname{sSet}}(\Lambda^{2}_{1},X)

over the horn determined by
u
and
v
. Since
\Lambda^{2}_{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{2}
is inner
anodyne and
X
is Kan, this restriction map is a trivial Kan fibration. Hence the fiber is a
contractible Kan complex.
∎

Lemma 4.4.6

(Boundary comparison for an attached simplex)
.

Let
n\geq 2
, let

\sigma:\Delta^{n}\to K

be a nondegenerate simplex, and let

G:\partial\Delta^{n}\to\mathcal{C}

be a simplicial map.

Assume that for every proper injective map

\alpha:[d]\hookrightarrow[n]\qquad(d<n),

we are given an equivalence

{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}}_{\alpha}:G|_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma\circ\alpha)

in
\Fun(\Delta^{d},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
, and that for every pair of proper injectives

[\ell]\xrightarrow{\beta}[d]\xrightarrow{\alpha}[n]

we are given a chosen homotopy

\beta^{*}(\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\alpha})\ \simeq\ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma\circ\alpha,\beta,m}\circ\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\alpha\circ\beta}

in
\Fun(\Delta^{\ell},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
, compatible with triple compositions.

Then there exist:

(1)

an equivalence

e_{\sigma}:G\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\rho_{n}\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)\bigr)

in
\Fun(\partial\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
,

(2)

and for every proper injective

\alpha:[d]\hookrightarrow[n],

a chosen homotopy

h_{\alpha}:e_{\sigma}|_{\alpha}\ \simeq\ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\alpha,m}\circ\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\alpha}

in
\Fun(\Delta^{d},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
,

and these chosen homotopies are compatible with further proper injectives.

Proof.

The key point is that the previously constructed comparison data on the proper
faces of
\sigma
should assemble into a single comparison map on the whole
boundary

\partial\Delta^{n}.

Conceptually, nothing new happens here: one fills the boundary skeleton by
skeleton in the Kan complex of equivalences. The only non-formal input is that
at each stage the already chosen lower-dimensional data determine the boundary
of the next simplex compatibly; the rest is a routine filling argument.

Set

X:=\Fun(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq},

which is a Kan complex.

For every proper injective

\alpha:[d]\hookrightarrow[n],

choose once and for all a composite

y_{\alpha}:G|_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)

of

G|_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\ {\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}}_{\alpha}\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma\circ\alpha)\xrightarrow{\ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\alpha,m}\ }\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma),

for some chosen composite of the consecutive edges.

The space of such composites is contractible by
Lemma
4.4.5
; we fix one choice, and all later choices made
from these composite spaces are unique up to contractible choice.

Equivalently,
y_{\alpha}
is a
d
-simplex of

X
.

By the assumed compatibility of the
{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}}_{\alpha}
, the coherence of the
comparison maps
c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{-,-,m}
under composition and triple compositions, and
Lemma
4.4.5
, for every

[\ell]\xrightarrow{\beta}[d]\xrightarrow{\alpha}[n]

with
\alpha,\beta
proper injective, choose once and for all a homotopy

\beta^{*}(y_{\alpha})\simeq y_{\alpha\circ\beta}

in
\Fun(\Delta^{\ell},X)^{\simeq}
, compatible with triple compositions.

We now construct by induction on
d=0,\dots,n-1
:

(1)

a simplicial map

e_{\sigma}^{(d)}:\sk_{d}(\partial\Delta^{n})\to X,

(2)

and for every nondegenerate simplex

\alpha:[r]\hookrightarrow[n]\qquad(r\leq d),

an equivalence

h_{\alpha}:e_{\sigma}^{(d)}|_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }y_{\alpha}

in
\Fun(\Delta^{r},X)^{\simeq}
,

such that these
h_{\alpha}
are compatible under restriction to proper faces.

Base step
d=0
.

Define
e_{\sigma}^{(0)}
on each vertex
\alpha:[0]\hookrightarrow[n]
by

e_{\sigma}^{(0)}(\alpha):=y_{\alpha}\in X_{0}.

Then
h_{\alpha}:=\operatorname{id}_{y_{\alpha}}
.

Inductive step.

Assume
d\geq 1
and that
e_{\sigma}^{(d-1)}
and the
h_{\alpha}
have been constructed.

Let

\alpha:[d]\hookrightarrow[n]

be a nondegenerate
d
-simplex of
\partial\Delta^{n}
. Its boundary

\partial\alpha:\partial\Delta^{d}\to\sk_{d-1}(\partial\Delta^{n})

has already been mapped to
X
, giving

b_{\alpha}:=e_{\sigma}^{(d-1)}|_{\partial\alpha}:\partial\Delta^{d}\to X.

For each face map
\delta^{i}:[d-1]\hookrightarrow[d]
, the inductive datum gives an equivalence

h_{\alpha\circ\delta^{i}}:e_{\sigma}^{(d-1)}|_{\alpha\circ\delta^{i}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }y_{\alpha\circ\delta^{i}}.

Using the chosen homotopies

\delta^{i,*}(y_{\alpha})\simeq y_{\alpha\circ\delta^{i}},

the already constructed facewise equivalences

h_{\alpha\circ\delta^{i}}:e_{\sigma}^{(d-1)}|_{\alpha\circ\delta^{i}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }y_{\alpha\circ\delta^{i}}

define a coherent boundary datum on the faces of
\partial\Delta^{d}
with values in the Kan complex

\Fun(\Delta^{1},X)^{\simeq}.

By gluing these facewise data over the skeletal filtration of
\partial\Delta^{d}
, we obtain an equivalence

\bar{h}_{\alpha}:b_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\rho_{d}(y_{\alpha})

in
\Fun(\partial\Delta^{d},X)^{\simeq}
, compatible with restriction to proper faces.

By Lemma
4.4.3
, restriction

\rho_{d}^{\simeq}:\Fun(\Delta^{d},X)^{\simeq}\longrightarrow\Fun(\partial\Delta^{d},X)^{\simeq}

is a Kan fibration. Consider the lifting problem

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{1}_{0}=\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\Delta^{d},X)^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\partial\Delta^{d},X)^{\simeq}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces y_{\alpha}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{d}^{\simeq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\bar{h}_{\alpha}}

Since
\rho_{d}^{\simeq}
is a Kan fibration, there exists a lift

h_{\alpha}:\Delta^{1}\to\Fun(\Delta^{d},X)^{\simeq}.

Let

x_{\alpha}:=h_{\alpha}(0)\in\Fun(\Delta^{d},X)^{\simeq}_{0}.

Then
x_{\alpha}
is a
d
-simplex of
X
, and
h_{\alpha}
is an equivalence

h_{\alpha}:x_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }y_{\alpha}

in
\Fun(\Delta^{d},X)^{\simeq}
satisfying

\rho_{d}(x_{\alpha})=b_{\alpha}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\rho_{d}(h_{\alpha})=\bar{h}_{\alpha}.

Doing this for all nondegenerate
d
-simplices
\alpha
, Lemma
4.4.4

gives a unique extension

e_{\sigma}^{(d)}:\sk_{d}(\partial\Delta^{n})\to X

of
e_{\sigma}^{(d-1)}
sending each such
\alpha
to
x_{\alpha}
.

This completes the induction.

At
d=n-1
, we obtain a simplicial map

e_{\sigma}:\partial\Delta^{n}\to X.

By adjunction, this is exactly an equivalence

e_{\sigma}:G\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\rho_{n}\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)\bigr)

in
\Fun(\partial\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
.

Finally, for every proper injective
\alpha:[d]\hookrightarrow[n]
, the previously constructed
equivalence

h_{\alpha}:e_{\sigma}|_{\alpha}\xrightarrow{\sim}y_{\alpha}

is precisely the required chosen homotopy

e_{\sigma}|_{\alpha}\ \simeq\ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\alpha,m}\circ{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}}_{\alpha}.

The compatibility with further proper injectives is built into the induction.
∎

Lemma 4.4.7

(Propagation of comparison data to a degenerate top-dimensional simplex)
.

Let
n\geq 1
, and let

\widetilde{F}:\sk_{n}(K)\to\mathcal{C}

be a simplicial map.

Assume that for every simplex

\mu\in K_{r}\qquad(r<n),

we are given an equivalence

{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu}}:\widetilde{F}(\mu)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(\mu)

in
\Fun(\Delta^{r},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
, and that for every proper injective

\alpha:[d]\hookrightarrow[r]\qquad(r<n),

the two morphisms

\alpha^{*}({\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,\alpha,m}\circ\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu\circ\alpha}

from

\widetilde{F}(\mu\circ\alpha)=\alpha^{*}\widetilde{F}(\mu)

to

\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\mu)

are connected by a chosen homotopy, compatible with further proper injectives.

Let

\tau=\mu\circ s\in K_{n}

be degenerate, where

s:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[r]\qquad(r<n)

is surjective.

Define

{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}}:=\bigl(c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,s,m}\bigr)^{-1}\circ s^{*}({\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu}}).

Then for every proper injective

\alpha:[d]\hookrightarrow[n],

the two morphisms

\alpha^{*}({\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}\circ{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau\circ\alpha}}

from

\widetilde{F}(\tau\circ\alpha)=\alpha^{*}\widetilde{F}(\tau)

to

\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)

are connected by a chosen homotopy in
\Fun(\Delta^{d},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
, compatible with
further proper injectives.

Proof.

The core point is that the comparison data for a degenerate simplex

\tau=\mu\circ s

should be obtained by transporting the comparison data for
\mu
along the
degeneracy operator
s
. For a proper injective

\alpha:[d]\hookrightarrow[n],

the only real idea is to factor

s\circ\alpha=\bar{\alpha}\circ s_{\alpha}

as a surjection followed by an injection, and then compare the two resulting
ways of transporting comparison data using the coherence of the operator
comparisons
c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{-,-,m}
. Once this factorization is in place, the rest
is a straightforward bookkeeping of the chosen homotopies, together with the
inductive hypothesis when
s_{\alpha}\neq\operatorname{id}
.

We argue by induction on
n
.

If
n=1
, then
\tau
is of the form

\tau=\mu\circ s\qquad\text{with}\qquad\mu\in K_{0}

and
s:[1]\twoheadrightarrow[0]
. The only proper injectives

\alpha:[0]\hookrightarrow[1]

satisfy
s\circ\alpha=\operatorname{id}_{[0]}
, so the claim follows immediately from the definition of

{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}}
and the identity compatibility

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,\operatorname{id},m}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}_{m}(\mu)}.

Assume now
n\geq 2
, and let

\alpha:[d]\hookrightarrow[n]

be a proper injective. Factor the composite

s\circ\alpha:[d]\to[r]

in
\Delta
as

[d]\xrightarrow{\ s_{\alpha}\ }[d^{\prime}]\xrightarrow{\ \bar{\alpha}\ }[r],

where
s_{\alpha}
is surjective and
\bar{\alpha}
is injective. Then

s\circ\alpha=\bar{\alpha}\circ s_{\alpha}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\tau\circ\alpha=(\mu\circ\bar{\alpha})\circ s_{\alpha}.

By coherence of the operator comparisons under composition, we have chosen homotopies

\alpha^{*}(c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,s,m})\circ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}\ \simeq\ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,s\circ\alpha,m}

and

s_{\alpha}^{*}(c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,\bar{\alpha},m})\circ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu\circ\bar{\alpha},s_{\alpha},m}\ \simeq\ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,\bar{\alpha}\circ s_{\alpha},m}=c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,s\circ\alpha,m}.

Hence, after inverting the displayed equivalences and whiskering as needed, we obtain a chosen
homotopy

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}\circ\bigl(c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu\circ\bar{\alpha},s_{\alpha},m}\bigr)^{-1}\ \simeq\ \alpha^{*}\bigl(c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,s,m}\bigr)^{-1}\circ s_{\alpha}^{*}(c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,\bar{\alpha},m}).

Since
\bar{\alpha}
is injective and
d^{\prime}<n
, the assumed compatibility for
\mu
yields a
chosen homotopy

\bar{\alpha}^{*}({\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu}})\ \simeq\ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,\bar{\alpha},m}\circ\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu\circ\bar{\alpha}}.

Pulling back along
s_{\alpha}
, we get

\alpha^{*}s^{*}({\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu}})=s_{\alpha}^{*}\bar{\alpha}^{*}({\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu}})\ \simeq\ s_{\alpha}^{*}(c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,\bar{\alpha},m})\circ s_{\alpha}^{*}(\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu\circ\bar{\alpha}}).

If
s_{\alpha}=\operatorname{id}
, then

\tau\circ\alpha=\mu\circ\bar{\alpha}

and combining
(1)
,
(2)
, and the definition

{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}}=\bigl(c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu,s,m}\bigr)^{-1}\circ s^{*}({\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu}})

gives the required homotopy

\alpha^{*}({\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}})\ \simeq\ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}\circ{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau\circ\alpha}}.

If
s_{\alpha}\neq\operatorname{id}
, then
d<n
, so
\tau\circ\alpha
has strictly smaller dimension than

\tau
. Applying the present lemma inductively to the lower-dimensional degenerate simplex

\tau\circ\alpha=(\mu\circ\bar{\alpha})\circ s_{\alpha},

we obtain a chosen homotopy

{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau\circ\alpha}}\ \simeq\ \bigl(c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\mu\circ\bar{\alpha},s_{\alpha},m}\bigr)^{-1}\circ s_{\alpha}^{*}(\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\mu\circ\bar{\alpha}}).

Combining
(1)
,
(2)
,
(3)
, and the definition of
{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}}
, we again obtain

\alpha^{*}({\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}})\ \simeq\ c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}\circ{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau\circ\alpha}}.

The compatibility with further proper injectives follows from the inductive compatibility already
known in lower dimensions, together with the assumed coherence of the operator comparisons

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{-,-,m}
under composition and triple compositions.

∎

Input now available for rigidification.

At this stage we have produced, for every simplex
\tau
of the ambient simplicial set,
a distinguished simplex

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)\in\Fun(\Delta^{r},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}_{0},

together with coherent comparison equivalences

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau\circ\alpha)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)

for all simplicial operators
\alpha
, compatible with identities, composition, and triple
compositions. The remaining task is purely simplicial: starting from a prescribed strict

1
-skeleton, extend it to a simplicial map on all of
K
while preserving comparison with
the simplexwise data
\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)
.

Theorem 4.4.8

(Abstract rigidification from coherent comparison data)
.

Let
K
be a simplicial set, and set

L:=\sk_{1}(K).

Assume given abstract rigidification data as in
Notation
4.4.1
.

Let

\Phi_{m}:L\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

be a simplicial map.

Assume moreover that for every simplex

\tau\in K_{r}\qquad(r\leq 1),

we are given an equivalence

\epsilon^{(1)}_{\tau}:\Phi_{m}(\tau)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)

in
\Fun(\Delta^{r},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
, such that for every proper injective

\alpha:[s]\hookrightarrow[r]\qquad(r\leq 1),

the two morphisms

\alpha^{*}(\epsilon^{(1)}_{\tau})\qquad\text{and}\qquad c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}\circ\epsilon^{(1)}_{\tau\circ\alpha}

are connected by a chosen homotopy, compatible with further proper injectives.

Then there exist:

(1)

a simplicial map

\widetilde{F}_{m}:K\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

whose restriction to
L
is
\Phi_{m}
;

(2)

for every simplex

\tau:\Delta^{r}\to K,

an equivalence

\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}:\widetilde{F}_{m}(\tau)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)

in
\Fun(\Delta^{r},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
,

satisfying the same face-compatibility condition for every proper injective

\alpha:[s]\hookrightarrow[r].

Proof.

The construction is by skeletal induction on

K.

At stage
n
, the simplicial map has already been constructed on the

(n-1)
-skeleton, together with comparison equivalences to
\mathcal{G}_{m}
on all
simplices of dimension
<n
. For a nondegenerate
n
-simplex, the genuine
input is that these lower-dimensional comparison data determine a coherent
boundary comparison, which can then be filled using the Kan-fibration property
of the boundary restriction map. The rest of the proof consists of checking that
this extension is compatible with degeneracies and with the previously chosen
comparison data.

More precisely, we construct by induction on
n\geq 1
:

(1)

a simplicial map

F^{(n)}_{m}:\sk_{n}(K)\to\mathcal{C},

extending
\Phi_{m}
,

(2)

and for every simplex

\tau\in K_{r}\qquad(r\leq n),

an equivalence

{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}}:F^{(n)}_{m}(\tau)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)

in
\Fun(\Delta^{r},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
,

such that for every simplex
\tau\in K_{r}
with
r\leq n
and every proper injective

\alpha:[s]\hookrightarrow[r],

the two morphisms

\alpha^{*}({\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}})\qquad\text{and}\qquad c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}\circ{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau\circ\alpha}}

from

F^{(n)}_{m}(\tau\circ\alpha)=\alpha^{*}F^{(n)}_{m}(\tau)

to

\alpha^{*}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)

are connected by a chosen homotopy, compatible with further proper injectives.

Base step
n=1
.

Set

F_{m}^{(1)}:=\Phi_{m}.

For every simplex

\tau\in K_{r}\qquad(r\leq 1),

set

\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}:=\epsilon^{(1)}_{\tau}.

The required compatibility for
r\leq 1
is exactly the assumed compatibility
of the initial
1
-skeleton comparison data.

Inductive step.

Assume
n\geq 2
, and that
F^{(n-1)}_{m}
and the
{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}}
have been constructed.

For every simplex of dimension
<n
, the already constructed comparison data continues to serve for

F^{(n)}_{m}
, since the eventual extension will restrict to
F^{(n-1)}_{m}
on
\sk_{n-1}(K)
.

Let
\sigma\in K_{n}
be nondegenerate. Set

G_{\sigma}:=F^{(n-1)}_{m}|_{\partial\sigma}:\partial\Delta^{n}\to\mathcal{C}.

For every proper injective

\alpha:[r]\hookrightarrow[n],

define

\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\alpha}:=\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\sigma\circ\alpha}:G_{\sigma}|_{\alpha}=F^{(n-1)}_{m}(\sigma\circ\alpha)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma\circ\alpha).

By the inductive hypothesis, these equivalences satisfy the compatibility required in
Lemma
4.4.6
. Hence that lemma yields an equivalence

e_{\sigma}:G_{\sigma}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\rho_{n}\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)\bigr)

in
\Fun(\partial\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
.

By Lemma
4.4.3
, restriction induces a Kan fibration

\rho_{n}^{\simeq}:\Fun(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}\longrightarrow\Fun(\partial\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}.

Consider the lifting problem

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Lambda^{1}_{0}=\{1\}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta^{1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Fun(\partial\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{n}^{\simeq}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces e_{\sigma}}

Since
\rho_{n}^{\simeq}
is a Kan fibration, choose a lift

\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\sigma}:\Delta^{1}\to\Fun(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}.

Let

x_{\sigma}:=\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\sigma}(0)\in\Fun(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}_{0}.

Then
x_{\sigma}
is an
n
-simplex of
\mathcal{C}
, it restricts to
G_{\sigma}
on

\partial\Delta^{n}
, and
\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\sigma}
is an equivalence

\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\sigma}:x_{\sigma}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)

in
\Fun(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
whose restriction to the boundary is
e_{\sigma}
.

Doing this for every nondegenerate
\sigma\in K_{n}
, Lemma
4.4.4
gives a
unique extension

F^{(n)}_{m}:\sk_{n}(K)\to\mathcal{C}

of
F^{(n-1)}_{m}
sending each nondegenerate
n
-simplex
\sigma
to
x_{\sigma}
.

Now let
\tau\in K_{n}
be degenerate. Write

\tau=\mu\circ s

with

\mu\in K_{r},\qquad r<n,\qquad s:[n]\twoheadrightarrow[r].

Define
{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}}
by Lemma
4.4.7
. That lemma also supplies
the compatibility required in (b) for
\tau
.

For nondegenerate
\tau=\sigma
, the compatibility in (b) is given by the chosen
homotopies
h_{\alpha}
supplied by
Lemma
4.4.6
, applied to
G_{\sigma}
.

This completes the induction.

Finally, since

K=\operatorname{colim}_{n}\sk_{n}(K),

the compatible maps
F^{(n)}_{m}
assemble into a simplicial map

\widetilde{F}_{m}:K\to\mathcal{C}.

By construction,

\widetilde{F}_{m}|_{\sk_{1}(K)}=\Phi_{m},

and the equivalences
{\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\tau}}
constructed inductively for all simplices
\tau

satisfy the stated face-compatibility condition.

∎

4.5.
Application to the closed-immersion pullback simplices

We now apply Theorem
4.4.8
to the simplicial set

N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}
, with prescribed
1
-skeleton given by the degree-
m
closed-immersion pullback maps.

Remark 4.5.1

(Raw versus normalized degenerate edges)
.

For a vertex
X
, the value

F_{1,m}(s_{0}X)=s_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{m}(X))

is the normalized degenerate edge in
\mathcal{C}
, equivalently the identity edge

(\operatorname{id}_{X})_{m}^{*}=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)}

by Notation
2.4.5
.
By contrast, the simplex

\mathcal{G}_{m}(s_{0}X)

is the raw geometric
1
-simplex attached to the degenerate immersion

\operatorname{id}_{X}:X\hookrightarrow X
.
Lemma
4.1.8
identifies these two edges only up to the canonical
equivalence

\mathcal{G}_{m}(s_{0}X)\xrightarrow{\sim}s_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)).

Notations 4.5.2

(Concrete
1
-skeleton assignment in degree
m
)
.

Define

F_{1,m}:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

as the unique simplicial map whose values on vertices are

F_{1,m}(X):=\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)\qquad(X\in\Sm_{S}),

and on
1
-simplices
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
are

F_{1,m}(i):=i_{m}^{*}.

Remark 4.5.3

(Raw simplices versus normalized
1
-skeleton)
.

The simplex

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)\in\Fun(\Delta^{r},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}_{0}

is the raw geometric degree-
m
closed-immersion pullback simplex attached to
\tau
by
Definition
2.4.4
. In particular, for a degenerate edge
s_{0}(X)
,
the simplex
\mathcal{G}_{m}(s_{0}X)
is
not
identified on the nose with the
degenerate edge
s_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{m}(X))
; it is only canonically equivalent to it by
Lemma
4.1.8
.

By contrast, the simplicial map

F_{1,m}:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\to\mathcal{C}

is the normalized strict
1
-skeleton: on a closed immersion

i:Z\hookrightarrow X
it takes the value
i_{m}^{*}
, and for the identity
immersion this is the strict identity edge by
Notation
2.4.5
.

Lemma 4.5.4

(Initial comparison data for the normalized
1
-skeleton)
.

For every simplex

\tau\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}_{r}\qquad(r\leq 1),

represented by the corresponding geometric flag in
N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{r}
,
there is a canonical equivalence

\epsilon^{(1)}_{\tau}:F_{1,m}(\tau)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(\tau)

in
\Fun(\Delta^{r},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
, compatible with proper injectives

\alpha:[s]\hookrightarrow[r]\qquad(r\leq 1),

in the sense required by
Theorem
4.4.8
.

Proof.

If
\tau
is represented by a nondegenerate geometric closed immersion

i:Z\hookrightarrow X
, then define

\epsilon^{(1)}_{X}:=\operatorname{id}_{\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)}.

If
\tau=i:Z\hookrightarrow X
is a nondegenerate
1
-simplex, then

F_{1,m}(i)=i_{m}^{*}=\mathcal{G}_{m}(i),

so define

\epsilon^{(1)}_{i}:=\operatorname{id}_{i_{m}^{*}}.

If
\tau=s_{0}(X)
is the degenerate
1
-simplex at
X
, define

\epsilon^{(1)}_{s_{0}(X)}:=\bigl(\upsilon_{0,m}^{X}\bigr)^{-1}:F_{1,m}(s_{0}X)=s_{0}(\mathcal{G}_{m}(X))\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(s_{0}X),

where
\upsilon_{0,m}^{X}
is the degeneracy comparison of
Corollary
4.1.9
.

The compatibility with proper injectives is immediate for vertices and
nondegenerate edges, and for degenerate edges it follows from
Corollary
4.1.9
together with the dimension-
1

normalization built into Notation
4.4.1
.
∎

Theorem 4.5.5

(Strict closed-immersion pullback rigidification in degree
m
)
.

Let

F_{1,m}:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

be the normalized
1
-skeleton of Notation
4.5.2
.

Choose a section

s_{1,m}:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\longrightarrow E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}},

and, for this choice, a vertex

s_{m}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{s_{1,m}}(p_{m})_{0}.

These choices exist by Proposition
4.2.24
.

Then Construction
4.3.1
and
Lemma
4.3.2
produce coherent operator comparisons

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\alpha,m}.

Applying Theorem
4.4.8
with

K:=N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}},\qquad\Phi_{m}:=F_{1,m},

and with the initial comparison data of
Lemma
4.5.4
,
we obtain a simplicial map

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}:N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\longrightarrow\mathcal{C}

together with comparison equivalences

\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\sigma}:\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)

whose restrictions along proper injective operators are compatible with the

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\alpha,m}
.

In particular,

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}\big|_{\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})}=F_{1,m},

so for every
X\in\Sm_{S}
and every closed immersion
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
,

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(X)=\mathcal{G}_{m}(X),\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(i)=i_{m}^{*}.

Proof.

Choose

s_{1,m}:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\to E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}

and

s_{m}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{s_{1,m}}(p_{m})_{0}

as in Proposition
4.2.24
.

Construction
4.3.1
and
Lemma
4.3.2
supply coherent operator comparisons

c^{\operatorname{coh}}_{\sigma,\alpha,m}.

Together with the initial comparison data of
Lemma
4.5.4
, these satisfy the hypotheses of
Theorem
4.4.8
. Applying that theorem yields

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}
and the comparison equivalences
\epsilon^{\operatorname{rig}}_{\sigma}
.

The final assertions follow because

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}\big|_{\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})}=F_{1,m}

by construction, and

F_{1,m}(X)=\mathcal{G}_{m}(X),\qquad F_{1,m}(i)=i_{m}^{*}.

∎

Corollary 4.5.6

(Shared rigidification after identifying the input package)
.

Fix two constructions that,
after transport to the same target pointed
\infty
-category admitting finite colimits,
determine the same data
F_{1,m}
and the same coherent-choice projection

p_{m}:E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}\longrightarrow N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}.

Then the contractible Kan complex of
1
-skeleton sections furnished by
Proposition
4.2.24
is common to both constructions; choose one common section

s_{1,m}:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\longrightarrow E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}.

For this common choice, the contractible Kan complex

\operatorname{Sec}_{s_{1,m}}(p_{m})

is also common to both constructions. Choosing any vertex

s_{m}\in\operatorname{Sec}_{s_{1,m}}(p_{m})_{0}

and applying Construction
4.3.1
and
Theorem
4.4.8
once yields a simplicial map

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}:N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{C}

which is simultaneously a strict rigidification of the closed-immersion pullback package for both constructions.

Proof.

Since the two constructions define the same projection

p_{m}:E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}\to N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}},

they also share the same contractible space of
1
-skeleton sections

\operatorname{Sec}(p_{m,\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})}),

so we may choose a common section
s_{1,m}:\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})\to E_{m}^{\operatorname{coh}}
. For this common choice,
the section space

\operatorname{Sec}_{s_{1,m}}(p_{m})

is the same contractible Kan complex for both constructions. Choose one common vertex

s_{m}
. The subsequent constructions of the operator comparisons and of the strict immersion
rigidification depend only on this common choice and on the common input data, so applying
them once yields a rigidification that serves for both constructions.
∎

Remark 4.5.7

(Degenerate
2
-simplices encode the unit constraints)
.

Let
i:Z\hookrightarrow X
be a closed immersion in
\Sm_{S}
.
By Theorem
4.5.5
, the strict simplicial map

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}:N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}}\to\mathcal{C}

satisfies

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}\bigl(s_{0}(i)\bigr)=s_{0}\bigl(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(i)\bigr),\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}\bigl(s_{1}(i)\bigr)=s_{1}\bigl(\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(i)\bigr)

in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.

Since
\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}|_{\sk_{1}(N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})^{\operatorname{op}})}=F_{1,m}
strictly, one has

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}(i)=i_{m}^{*}.

Hence

\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}\bigl(s_{0}(i)\bigr)=s_{0}(i_{m}^{*}),\qquad\widetilde{\mathcal{G}}_{m}\bigl(s_{1}(i)\bigr)=s_{1}(i_{m}^{*})

in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
.

5.
Gluing closed immersions and smooth morphisms

5.1.
Strategy of the gluing proof

This section is a continuation of Sections
2
-
4
.
The aim is to deduce from a weak Borel-Moore theory
\mathcal{G}

a fully contravariant functor on
\Sm_{S}
which extends the case of closed immersions
treated in Theorem
4.5.5
.
Let us roughly explain our procedure. For each simplex

\tau=\bigl(X_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}X_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{n}}X_{n}\bigr)\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n},

we attach a canonical graph flag, deduced from the graph factorization of each
f_{i}
:

\Gamma(\tau)\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}.

For
n\geq 1
, the accepted simplicial package of
Sections
2
-
4
associates to

\Gamma(\tau)
a higher closed-immersion pullback simplex

\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{simp}}(\Gamma(\tau))\in\Map_{\mathcal{C}}\!\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{m}(P_{n}(\tau)),\,\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{0})\bigr)_{n-1}.

The object

\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{simp}}(\Gamma(\tau))\circ q_{\tau}^{*}

is useful only as a first approximation: it corrects the source vertex

\mathcal{G}_{m}(P_{n}(\tau))
to
\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n})
, but it leaves the intermediate vertices equal to

\mathcal{G}_{m}(P_{r}(\tau))
. Hence its simplicial faces are not the desired faces of a simplex over

\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n}),\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-1}),\ldots,\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{0}).

The correct object is instead a prismatic comparison between the closed-immersion graph simplex
on the product vertices
P_{r}(\tau)
and a new simplex on the original vertices
X_{r}
.

Concretely, the bottom face of the prism is the closed-immersion simplex attached to

\Gamma(\tau)
, while the vertical edges are the smooth pullbacks

q_{\tau,r}^{*}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{r})\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{m}(P_{r}(\tau)).

The upper face of a coherent choice of such a prism is denoted

\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau):\Delta^{n}\to\mathcal{C}.

The point of the prismatic construction is that the intermediate smooth pullbacks are not
silently cancelled inside the graph simplex; they are recorded as vertical data and cancelled only
through the section-cancelled base-change lemma below.

We then organize the simplices

\tau\longmapsto\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau)

and their operator comparisons coherently, and apply the abstract rigidification theorem of
Section
4
.

The remaining non-formal input is the prismatic graph-comparison package:
one must construct the upper prismatic simplices

\tau\longmapsto\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau)

and their operator comparisons coherently. Once this package is available, the passage to a
strict simplicial functor is exactly the abstract rigidification argument of
Section
4
.

5.2.
Canonical graph flags

Notations 5.2.1

(Initial interval products)
.

Let

\tau=\bigl(X_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}X_{1}\xrightarrow{f_{2}}\cdots\xrightarrow{f_{n}}X_{n}\bigr)\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n}.

For
0\leq r\leq n
, set

P_{r}(\tau):=X_{0}\times_{S}X_{1}\times_{S}\cdots\times_{S}X_{r},

with the convention
P_{0}(\tau)=X_{0}
.
Let

q_{\tau,r}:P_{r}(\tau)\to X_{r}

be the projection to the last factor, and write

q_{\tau}:=q_{\tau,n}:P_{n}(\tau)\to X_{n}.

Definition 5.2.2

(Canonical graph flag)
.

Let
\tau\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n}
be as in
Notation
5.2.1
.
If
n=0
, we set

\Gamma(\tau):=P_{0}(\tau)=X_{0}\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{0}.

Assume now
n\geq 1
.

For
1\leq r\leq n
, define

\gamma_{\tau,r}:P_{r-1}(\tau)\hookrightarrow P_{r}(\tau)=P_{r-1}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{r}

to be the graph immersion of the morphism

f_{r}\circ q_{\tau,r-1}:P_{r-1}(\tau)\to X_{r}.

Then

\Gamma(\tau):=\bigl(P_{0}(\tau)\xhookrightarrow{\gamma_{\tau,1}}P_{1}(\tau)\xhookrightarrow{\gamma_{\tau,2}}\cdots\xhookrightarrow{\gamma_{\tau,n}}P_{n}(\tau)\bigr)\in N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})_{n}

is called the canonical graph flag attached to
\tau
.

Lemma 5.2.3

(Basic properties of the canonical graph flag)
.

Let
\tau\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n}
.
Then:

(1)

each
\gamma_{\tau,r}
is a closed immersion in
\Sm_{S}
;

(2)

each
\gamma_{\tau,r}
is a regular immersion;

(3)

q_{\tau}:P_{n}(\tau)\to X_{n}
is smooth.

Proof.

For each
r
, the morphism
\gamma_{\tau,r}
is the graph over
S
of the morphism

f_{r}\circ q_{\tau,r-1}:P_{r-1}(\tau)\to X_{r}.

Since
X_{r}\to S
is separated, this graph is a closed immersion. Moreover

P_{r}(\tau)=P_{r-1}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{r}\to P_{r-1}(\tau)

is smooth, and
\gamma_{\tau,r}
is a section of this smooth morphism. Hence

\gamma_{\tau,r}
is a regular immersion.

If
n=0
, then
q_{\tau}=\operatorname{id}_{X_{0}}
. If
n\geq 1
, then

P_{n}(\tau)=P_{n-1}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{n}

and
q_{\tau}
is the second projection. Since
P_{n-1}(\tau)\to S
is smooth as a finite
product of smooth
S
-schemes,
q_{\tau}
is its base change along
X_{n}\to S
. Therefore

q_{\tau}
is smooth.
∎

5.3.
Smooth base change for higher Gysin simplices of flags

Definition 5.3.1

(Smooth cartesian morphism of flags)
.

Let

\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow Z_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n}),\qquad\sigma^{\prime}=(Y_{0}\hookrightarrow Y_{1}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Y_{n})

be simplices of
N(\Sm^{imm}_{S})
.
A
smooth cartesian morphism of flags

g:\sigma^{\prime}\to\sigma

means a natural transformation whose components

g_{r}:Y_{r}\to Z_{r}\qquad(0\leq r\leq n)

are smooth and such that for every
1\leq r\leq n
the square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{r-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{r-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{r-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{r}}

is cartesian.

Lemma 5.3.2

(Smooth naturality of localization cubes)
.

Let

g:(X^{\prime},H^{\prime}_{\bullet})\to(X,H_{\bullet})

be a smooth morphism of equation-equipped ordered SNC pairs of length
n
, compatible with the
chosen equations, and such that

H^{\prime}_{i}=X^{\prime}\times_{X}H_{i}\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

Fix
m\in\mathbf{Z}
.

Then smooth contravariance and smooth–closed Beck–Chevalley define a natural transformation of

n
-cubes

\eta_{g}:\mathcal{K}_{X,H_{\bullet}}(m)\Longrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{X^{\prime},H^{\prime}_{\bullet}}(m),

whose component at

K\subseteq[n-1]

is the smooth pullback

g_{K}^{*}:\mathcal{G}_{m-|K|}(H_{K})\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{m-|K|}(H^{\prime}_{K}).

Write

U^{\prime}:=X^{\prime}\setminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n-1}H^{\prime}_{i},\qquad U:=X\setminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n-1}H_{i},

and let

g_{U}:U^{\prime}\to U

be the induced smooth morphism. Under the identifications of
Lemma
2.3.3
,
the induced map on total fibers identifies canonically with

\TotFib(\eta_{g})=g_{U}^{*}[-n]:\mathcal{G}_{m}(U)[-n]\longrightarrow\mathcal{G}_{m}(U^{\prime})[-n].

Proof.

For every
K\subseteq[n-1]
, one has

H^{\prime}_{K}=X^{\prime}\times_{X}H_{K},

so the induced morphism

g_{K}:H^{\prime}_{K}\to H_{K}

is smooth. Hence Axiom
(wBM2)
gives a morphism

g_{K}^{*}:\mathcal{G}_{m-|K|}(H_{K})\to\mathcal{G}_{m-|K|}(H^{\prime}_{K}).

Let

K\subset K\cup\{i\}.

The square

\textstyle{H^{\prime}_{K\cup\{i\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota^{\prime}_{K,i}}
\scriptstyle{g_{K\cup\{i\}}}
\textstyle{H^{\prime}_{K}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g_{K}}
\textstyle{H_{K\cup\{i\}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\iota_{K,i}}
\textstyle{H_{K}}

is cartesian, with horizontal arrows principal smooth divisor immersions and vertical arrows smooth.
Therefore Axiom
(wBM4)
gives a specified homotopy

g_{K}^{*}\circ(\iota_{K,i})_{*}\;\simeq\;(\iota_{K,i}^{\prime})_{*}\circ g_{K\cup\{i\}}^{*},

which is exactly the naturality condition for a map of localization cubes. Thus the
g_{K}^{*}

assemble into the natural transformation
\eta_{g}
.

For
0\leq r\leq n
, write

U^{(r)}:=X\setminus\bigcup_{j<r}H_{j},\qquad U^{\prime(r)}:=X^{\prime}\setminus\bigcup_{j<r}H^{\prime}_{j},

and for
K\subseteq\{r,\dots,n-1\}
,

H_{K}^{(r)}:=H_{K}\cap U^{(r)},\qquad H_{K}^{\prime(r)}:=H^{\prime}_{K}\cap U^{\prime(r)}.

Then

H_{K}^{\prime(r)}=X^{\prime}\times_{X}H_{K}^{(r)},

and the induced maps

g_{K}^{(r)}:H_{K}^{\prime(r)}\to H_{K}^{(r)}

are smooth.

We claim by induction on
r
that, under the canonical identifications of
Lemma
2.3.3
,

\operatorname{Fib}_{r-1}\cdots\operatorname{Fib}_{0}(\eta_{g})

identifies with the natural transformation of
(n-r)
-cubes

K\longmapsto g_{K}^{(r)}{}^{*}[-r]:\mathcal{G}_{m-|K|}(H_{K}^{(r)})[-r]\to\mathcal{G}_{m-|K|}(H_{K}^{\prime(r)})[-r].

For
r=0
this is tautological.

Assume the claim for
r
, and fix
K\subseteq\{r+1,\dots,n-1\}
.
The
r
-direction in the two cubes is given by the localization fiber sequences for the principal
smooth divisor immersions

H_{K\cup\{r\}}^{(r)}\hookrightarrow H_{K}^{(r)},\qquad H_{K\cup\{r\}}^{\prime}{}^{(r)}\hookrightarrow H_{K}^{\prime(r)}.

Because these immersions are related by smooth base change along
g_{K}^{(r)}
, Axiom
(wBM5)

and Axiom
(wBM4)
imply that after taking the fiber in the
r
-direction,
the induced map identifies with

g_{K}^{(r+1)}{}^{*}[-r-1]:\mathcal{G}_{m-|K|}(H_{K}^{(r+1)})[-r-1]\to\mathcal{G}_{m-|K|}(H_{K}^{\prime(r+1)})[-r-1].

This proves the induction step.

At
r=n
, only
K=\varnothing
remains, and
H_{\varnothing}^{(n)}=U
,

H_{\varnothing}^{\prime}{}^{(n)}=U^{\prime}
. Therefore

\TotFib(\eta_{g})=g_{U}^{*}[-n],

as claimed.
∎

Lemma 5.3.3

(Smooth naturality of total boundary simplices)
.

Let

g:(X^{\prime},H^{\prime}_{\bullet})\to(X,H_{\bullet})

be a smooth morphism of equation-equipped ordered SNC pairs of length
n
, compatible with the
chosen equations, and such that

H^{\prime}_{i}=X^{\prime}\times_{X}H_{i}\qquad(0\leq i\leq n-1).

Write

U^{\prime}:=X^{\prime}\setminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n-1}H^{\prime}_{i},\qquad U:=X\setminus\bigcup_{i=0}^{n-1}H_{i},

and let

g_{\operatorname{deep}}:H^{\prime}_{[n-1]}\to H_{[n-1]},\qquad g_{U}:U^{\prime}\to U

be the induced smooth morphisms.
Then there is a canonical homotopy

{\underline{\partial}}^{\operatorname{simp}}_{X^{\prime},H^{\prime}_{\bullet}}(m)\circ g_{U}^{*}[-n]\ \simeq\ g_{\operatorname{deep}}^{*}\circ{\underline{\partial}}^{\operatorname{simp}}_{X,H_{\bullet}}(m)

in

\Map_{\mathcal{C}}\!\Bigl(\mathcal{G}_{m}(U)[-n],\,\mathcal{G}_{m-n}(H^{\prime}_{[n-1]})\Bigr)_{n-1}.

Proof.

By Lemma
5.3.2
, the smooth morphism

g:(X^{\prime},H^{\prime}_{\bullet})\to(X,H_{\bullet})

induces a natural transformation of localization cubes

\eta_{g}:\mathcal{K}_{X,H_{\bullet}}(m)\Longrightarrow\mathcal{K}_{X^{\prime},H^{\prime}_{\bullet}}(m),

with deepest-stratum component

(\eta_{g})_{[n-1]}=g_{\operatorname{deep}}^{*},

and such that

\TotFib(\eta_{g})=g_{U}^{*}[-n]

under the identifications of Lemma
2.3.3
.

Applying Lemma
2.3.5
(3) to
\eta_{g}
yields a canonical homotopy

{\underline{\partial}}^{\operatorname{simp}}_{X^{\prime},H^{\prime}_{\bullet}}(m)\circ\TotFib(\eta_{g})\ \simeq\ (\eta_{g})_{[n-1]}\circ{\underline{\partial}}^{\operatorname{simp}}_{X,H_{\bullet}}(m).

Substituting

\TotFib(\eta_{g})=g_{U}^{*}[-n],\qquad(\eta_{g})_{[n-1]}=g_{\operatorname{deep}}^{*},

gives the required homotopy.
∎

Lemma 5.3.4

(Smooth base change for higher Gysin simplices of flags)
.

Let

g:\sigma^{\prime}=(Y_{0}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Y_{n})\to\sigma=(Z_{0}\hookrightarrow\cdots\hookrightarrow Z_{n})

be a smooth cartesian morphism of flags in the sense of
Definition
5.3.1
.
Fix
m\in\mathbf{Z}
. Then there is a canonical homotopy

\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{simp}}(\sigma^{\prime})\circ g_{n}^{*}\ \simeq\ g_{0}^{*}\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{simp}}(\sigma)

in the Kan complex

\Map_{\mathcal{C}}\!\bigl(\mathcal{G}_{m}(Z_{n}),\,\mathcal{G}_{m}(Y_{0})\bigr)_{n-1}.

If in addition both endpoint components

g_{0}:Y_{0}\to Z_{0},\qquad g_{n}:Y_{n}\to Z_{n}

are identities, then the above homotopy translates canonically into an equivalence

\epsilon^{\mathit{sm}}_{g,\sigma,m}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma^{\prime})

in
\Fun(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
.

Proof.

We first show that
\sigma^{\prime}
is the flag obtained from
\sigma
by base change along
g_{n}
.
For
r=n
this is tautological. If
1\leq r\leq n
and

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{r-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{r}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{r-1}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{r}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{r-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{r}}

is cartesian, then from

Y_{r}\simeq Y_{n}\times_{Z_{n}}Z_{r}

one gets

Y_{r-1}\simeq Y_{r}\times_{Z_{r}}Z_{r-1}\simeq Y_{n}\times_{Z_{n}}Z_{r-1}.

By downward induction,

Y_{r}\simeq Y_{n}\times_{Z_{n}}Z_{r}\qquad(0\leq r\leq n).

Since
g_{n}
is smooth, all these pullback squares are Tor-independent.

Hence Lemma
1.6.2
applies and gives a canonical isomorphism

D(\sigma^{\prime})\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }D(\sigma)\times_{Z_{n}}Y_{n}

over
Y_{n}\times_{S}\mathbb{A}^{n}_{S}
, compatible with all ordered boundary divisors, all strata, the open strata,
and the deepest strata.

Write

g_{\operatorname{deep}}:H_{[n-1]}(\sigma^{\prime})\to H_{[n-1]}(\sigma),\qquad g_{U}:U_{\sigma^{\prime}}\to U_{\sigma}

for the induced smooth morphisms. Then the deepest-stratum projections fit into a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{[n-1]}(\sigma^{\prime})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H_{[n-1]}(\sigma)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{0}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{0},}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{\operatorname{deep}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\sigma^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{0}}

and the open-stratum identifications fit into a cartesian square

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\sigma^{\prime}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces U_{\sigma}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Y_{n}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces Z_{n}\times(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\varphi_{\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces g_{n}\times\operatorname{id}_{(\mathbb{G}_{m})^{n}}}

By Definition
2.4.4
,

\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{simp}}(\sigma^{\prime})=(\pi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{*})^{-1}\circ{\underline{\partial}}^{\operatorname{simp}}_{\sigma^{\prime},m}\circ\beta_{\sigma^{\prime},m},

\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{simp}}(\sigma)=(\pi_{\sigma}^{*})^{-1}\circ{\underline{\partial}}^{\operatorname{simp}}_{\sigma,m}\circ\beta_{\sigma,m}.

Now:

(1)

by Lemma
5.3.3
,

{\underline{\partial}}^{\operatorname{simp}}_{\sigma^{\prime},m}\circ g_{U}^{*}[-n]\ \simeq\ g_{\operatorname{deep}}^{*}\circ{\underline{\partial}}^{\operatorname{simp}}_{\sigma,m};

(2)

by Axiom
(wBM9)
(b) for the cartesian square of deepest strata,

(\pi_{\sigma^{\prime}}^{*})^{-1}\circ g_{\operatorname{deep}}^{*}\ \simeq\ g_{0}^{*}\circ(\pi_{\sigma}^{*})^{-1}.

(3)

by Axiom
(wBM11)
and the compatibility of
\varphi_{\sigma^{\prime}}
and

\varphi_{\sigma}
with
g_{U}
,

\beta_{\sigma^{\prime},m}\circ g_{n}^{*}\ \simeq\ g_{U}^{*}[-n]\circ\beta_{\sigma,m};

Composing these three homotopies gives

\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{simp}}(\sigma^{\prime})\circ g_{n}^{*}\ \simeq\ g_{0}^{*}\circ\mathcal{G}_{m}^{\operatorname{simp}}(\sigma).

If moreover
g_{0}=\operatorname{id}_{Y_{0}}
and
g_{n}=\operatorname{id}_{Y_{n}}
, then the source and target endpoints are fixed,
so the above homotopy translates by the standard mapping-space/simplex-coordinate correspondence into
an equivalence

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma)\xrightarrow{\sim}\mathcal{G}_{m}(\sigma^{\prime})

in
\Fun(\Delta^{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
.
∎

5.4.
Faces and degeneracies of graph flags

Lemma 5.4.1

(Graph faces as smooth-envelope comparisons)
.

Let

\tau=\bigl(X_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n}\bigr)\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n}.

For every
0\leq i\leq n
, the graph flag

\Gamma(\tau)\circ d_{i}

is related to
\Gamma(d_{i}\tau)
as follows.

(1)

If
i=n
, then there is a strict equality of flags

\Gamma(\tau)\circ d_{n}=\Gamma(d_{n}\tau).

(2)

If
i=0
, the projections forgetting the factor
X_{0}
define a smooth
cartesian morphism of flags

u_{0,\tau}:\Gamma(\tau)\circ d_{0}\longrightarrow\Gamma(d_{0}\tau).

Its
r
-th component is

u_{0,\tau,r}:P_{r+1}(\tau)=X_{0}\times_{S}\cdots\times_{S}X_{r+1}\longrightarrow P_{r}(d_{0}\tau)=X_{1}\times_{S}\cdots\times_{S}X_{r+1}.

(3)

If
1\leq i\leq n-1
, the projections forgetting the factor
X_{i}
define
smooth morphisms on vertices

u_{i,\tau,r}:P_{\delta^{i}(r)}(\tau)\longrightarrow P_{r}(d_{i}\tau),

where

\delta^{i}(r)=\begin{cases}r,&r<i,\\
r+1,&r\geq i.\end{cases}

All comparison squares are cartesian except the critical square at stage
i
.

At the critical stage, the pullback of the lower graph immersion along

u_{i,\tau,i}:P_{i+1}(\tau)\to P_{i}(d_{i}\tau)

is

Q_{i}:=P_{i+1}(\tau)\times_{P_{i}(d_{i}\tau)}P_{i-1}(d_{i}\tau)\simeq P_{i-1}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i}.

The upper composite immersion

P_{i-1}(\tau)\hookrightarrow P_{i+1}(\tau)

factors through
Q_{i}
as the section

P_{i-1}(\tau)\longrightarrow P_{i-1}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i},\qquad p\longmapsto\bigl(p,f_{i}(q_{\tau,i-1}(p))\bigr)

of the smooth projection
Q_{i}\to P_{i-1}(\tau)
.

Proof.

The case
i=n
is immediate from the definitions: deleting the terminal vertex of

\tau
deletes the last graph immersion and leaves the preceding graph flag unchanged.

For
i=0
, the flag
\Gamma(\tau)\circ d_{0}
has
r
-th vertex

P_{r+1}(\tau)=X_{0}\times_{S}\cdots\times_{S}X_{r+1},

whereas
\Gamma(d_{0}\tau)
has
r
-th vertex

P_{r}(d_{0}\tau)=X_{1}\times_{S}\cdots\times_{S}X_{r+1}.

The projection forgetting
X_{0}
is smooth because
X_{0}\to S
is smooth. For every
edge stage, the upper graph immersion is the pullback of the lower graph immersion along
this projection. Hence the displayed maps form a smooth cartesian morphism of flags.

Assume now that
1\leq i\leq n-1
. Away from the critical stage
i
, the upper and lower
graph immersions are defined by the same morphism after forgetting the
X_{i}
-factor, so the
comparison squares are cartesian.

At the critical stage, the upper immersion in
\Gamma(\tau)\circ d_{i}
is the composite

P_{i-1}(\tau)\xhookrightarrow{\gamma_{\tau,i}}P_{i}(\tau)\xhookrightarrow{\gamma_{\tau,i+1}}P_{i+1}(\tau),

whose image in

P_{i+1}(\tau)=P_{i-1}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i}\times_{S}X_{i+1}

is

p\longmapsto\bigl(p,f_{i}(q_{\tau,i-1}(p)),f_{i+1}f_{i}(q_{\tau,i-1}(p))\bigr).

The lower immersion in
\Gamma(d_{i}\tau)
is the graph

P_{i-1}(\tau)\longrightarrow P_{i}(d_{i}\tau)=P_{i-1}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i+1},

given by

p\longmapsto\bigl(p,f_{i+1}f_{i}(q_{\tau,i-1}(p))\bigr).

Pulling this lower graph back along

P_{i+1}(\tau)=P_{i-1}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i}\times_{S}X_{i+1}\longrightarrow P_{i}(d_{i}\tau)=P_{i-1}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i+1}

gives

Q_{i}\simeq P_{i-1}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i}.

The upper composite immersion factors through
Q_{i}
as the section

p\longmapsto\bigl(p,f_{i}(q_{\tau,i-1}(p))\bigr)

of the smooth projection
Q_{i}\to P_{i-1}(\tau)
. This proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 5.4.2

(Graph degeneracies as smooth-envelope comparisons)
.

Let

\tau=\bigl(X_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n}\bigr)\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n},

and let
0\leq i\leq n
. The projections forgetting the duplicated copy of
X_{i}
define smooth
morphisms on the vertices of the graph flags

v_{i,\tau,r}:P_{r}(s_{i}\tau)\longrightarrow P_{s_{i}(r)}(\tau).

All graph squares are cartesian except the critical square at stage
i+1
.

At the critical stage, the upper immersion in
\Gamma(s_{i}\tau)
is the diagonal section

P_{i}(\tau)\hookrightarrow P_{i}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i}

of the smooth projection

P_{i}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i}\longrightarrow P_{i}(\tau),

whereas the corresponding immersion in
\Gamma(\tau)\circ s_{i}
is the identity of

P_{i}(\tau)
.

Proof.

The degeneracy
s_{i}\tau
repeats the vertex
X_{i}
. Therefore
P_{r}(s_{i}\tau)
is obtained
from
P_{s_{i}(r)}(\tau)
by inserting one additional factor
X_{i}
exactly when the repeated
vertex has appeared. The projection forgetting that repeated factor is smooth.

If
r\neq i+1
, the graph immersion at stage
r
is, after forgetting the repeated factor, the
pullback of the corresponding graph immersion in
\Gamma(\tau)\circ s_{i}
. Thus the square is
cartesian.

For
r=i+1
, the new arrow in
s_{i}\tau
is the identity

X_{i}\xrightarrow{\operatorname{id}}X_{i}.

Hence the corresponding graph immersion is the diagonal

P_{i}(\tau)\hookrightarrow P_{i}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i}.

The matching immersion in
\Gamma(\tau)\circ s_{i}
is the identity of
P_{i}(\tau)
. Pulling the
identity immersion back along

P_{i}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i}\to P_{i}(\tau)

gives all of
P_{i}(\tau)\times_{S}X_{i}
, not the diagonal. Thus the square is not cartesian.
The diagonal is, however, a section of this smooth projection.
∎

5.5.
Prismatic raw lci simplices

Definition 5.5.1

(The graph-formula edge)
.

Let
f:Y\to X
be a morphism in
\Sm_{S}
. Write

\Gamma_{f}:Y\hookrightarrow Y\times_{S}X,\qquad q_{f}:Y\times_{S}X\to X

for the graph immersion and the projection to
X
. Define

E_{m}(f):=(\Gamma_{f})_{m}^{*}\circ q_{f}^{*}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(Y).

Notations 5.5.2

(Opposite simplex-coordinate operator)
.

Let

\alpha:[r]\to[n]

be a morphism in
\Delta
. We write

\alpha^{\vee}:[r]\to[n]

for the opposite simplex-coordinate operator

\alpha^{\vee}(j):=n-\alpha(r-j).

Thus

(\delta^{i})^{\vee}=\delta^{\,n-i},\qquad(\varsigma^{i})^{\vee}=\varsigma^{\,n-i}.

If

\beta:[s]\to[r]

is another simplicial operator, then

(\alpha\circ\beta)^{\vee}=\alpha^{\vee}\circ\beta^{\vee}.

If

\tau=\bigl(X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n}\bigr)\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n},

then
\tau\circ\alpha
has vertices

X_{\alpha(0)},\ldots,X_{\alpha(r)}.

Since the prismatic lci simplex attached to
\tau
is written in the opposite order

\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n}),\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-1}),\ldots,\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{0}),

the corresponding simplex-coordinate restriction of

\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau)

is

(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau).

Definition 5.5.3

(Complete graph-composition datum)
.

Fix
m\in\mathbf{Z}
, and let

\tau=\bigl(X_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n}\bigr)\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n}.

For
0\leq a\leq b\leq n
, set

f_{a,b}:=f_{b}\circ f_{b-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{a+1}:X_{a}\to X_{b},

with
f_{a,a}=\operatorname{id}_{X_{a}}
.

A complete graph-composition datum for
\tau
consists of:

(1)

an
n
-simplex

T_{\tau,m}:\Delta^{n}\to\mathcal{C}

whose simplex-coordinate vertex
a
is
\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a})
;

(2)

for every
0\leq a<b\leq n
, an equivalence in

\Fun(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
from the edge

T_{\tau,m}|_{\Delta^{\{a,b\}}}

to the graph-formula edge

E_{m}(f_{n-b,n-a}):\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a})\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-b});

(3)

a prismatic comparison

R_{\tau,m}:\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{C}

such that

R_{\tau,m}|_{\Delta^{n}\times\{0\}}=T_{\tau,m},\qquad R_{\tau,m}|_{\Delta^{n}\times\{1\}}=\mathcal{G}_{m}(\Gamma(\tau)),

and whose vertical edge over the simplex-coordinate vertex
a=n-r
is the smooth
pullback

q_{\tau,r}^{*}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{r})\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(P_{r}(\tau)).

Definition 5.5.4

(Prismatic raw lci simplex)
.

Let

\tau\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n}.

A prismatic raw lci simplex attached to
\tau
is the upper face

T_{\tau,m}:\Delta^{n}\to\mathcal{C}

of a complete graph-composition datum for
\tau
in the sense of
Definition
5.5.3
. We denote such an upper face by

\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau).

Thus its simplex-coordinate vertex
a
is

\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a}).

Lemma 5.5.5

(Two-faced spine-prism inclusion)
.

Let
{\mathbf{Spine}}_{n}\subset\Delta^{n}
denote the spine

0\to 1\to\cdots\to n.

Set

B_{n}:=\bigl(\Delta^{n}\times\partial\Delta^{1}\bigr)\cup_{{\mathbf{Spine}}_{n}\times\partial\Delta^{1}}\bigl({\mathbf{Spine}}_{n}\times\Delta^{1}\bigr)\subset\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1}.

Then the inclusion

B_{n}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1}

is inner anodyne.

Proof.

For
n=0
and
n=1
, the inclusion is an isomorphism. Assume
n\geq 2
.
The spine inclusion

{\mathbf{Spine}}_{n}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}

is inner anodyne. The displayed inclusion is its pushout-product with the monomorphism

\partial\Delta^{1}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{1}.

By the standard pushout-product stability of inner anodyne morphisms with respect to
monomorphisms, this pushout-product is again inner anodyne.
∎

Lemma 5.5.6

(Binary graph composition)
.

Let

Z\xrightarrow{g}Y\xrightarrow{f}X

be morphisms in
\Sm_{S}
. Then there is a canonical homotopy

E_{m}(g)\circ E_{m}(f)\simeq E_{m}(f\circ g)

in

\Map_{\mathcal{C}}(\mathcal{G}_{m}(X),\mathcal{G}_{m}(Z)).

Proof.

Write

q_{f}:Y\times_{S}X\to X,\qquad q_{g}:Z\times_{S}Y\to Y,

and let

\Gamma_{f}:Y\hookrightarrow Y\times_{S}X,\qquad\Gamma_{g}:Z\hookrightarrow Z\times_{S}Y

be the graph immersions. Put

P:=Z\times_{S}Y\times_{S}X.

Pull back
\Gamma_{f}
along the smooth morphism

q_{g}\times\operatorname{id}_{X}:P\to Y\times_{S}X.

The resulting closed immersion is

\widetilde{\Gamma}_{f}:Z\times_{S}Y\hookrightarrow P,\qquad(z,y)\longmapsto(z,y,f(y)).

By Lemma
3.2.14
, smooth base change for closed-immersion Gysin pullbacks gives

q_{g}^{*}\circ(\Gamma_{f})_{m}^{*}\simeq(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{f})_{m}^{*}\circ(q_{g}\times\operatorname{id}_{X})^{*}.

Therefore

E_{m}(g)\circ E_{m}(f)\simeq(\Gamma_{g})_{m}^{*}\circ(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{f})_{m}^{*}\circ(q_{g}\times\operatorname{id}_{X})^{*}\circ q_{f}^{*}.

Since

(q_{g}\times\operatorname{id}_{X})^{*}\circ q_{f}^{*}=\operatorname{pr}_{X}^{*}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(P),

and since the rigidified closed-immersion pullbacks are functorial for composition by
Theorem
4.5.5
,

(\Gamma_{g})_{m}^{*}\circ(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{f})_{m}^{*}\simeq(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{f}\circ\Gamma_{g})_{m}^{*}.

The composite closed immersion is

Z\hookrightarrow Z\times_{S}Y\times_{S}X,\qquad z\longmapsto(z,g(z),f(g(z))).

Let

\pi:Z\times_{S}Y\times_{S}X\to Z\times_{S}X

forget the
Y
-factor. The composite closed immersion lies over the graph

\Gamma_{f\circ g}:Z\hookrightarrow Z\times_{S}X

and factors as a section of the smooth pullback of
\pi
along
\Gamma_{f\circ g}
. Hence
Lemma
5.5.16
gives

(\widetilde{\Gamma}_{f}\circ\Gamma_{g})_{m}^{*}\circ\operatorname{pr}_{X}^{*}\simeq(\Gamma_{f\circ g})_{m}^{*}\circ q_{f\circ g}^{*}=E_{m}(f\circ g).

Combining the displayed homotopies proves the claim.
∎

Lemma 5.5.7

(Graph-formula interval edges as composites)
.

Let

\tau=\bigl(X_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n}\bigr)\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n}.

For every interval
0\leq a\leq b\leq n
, set

f_{a,b}:=f_{b}\circ f_{b-1}\circ\cdots\circ f_{a+1}:X_{a}\to X_{b},\qquad f_{a,a}:=\operatorname{id}_{X_{a}}.

Then the graph-formula edge

E_{m}(f_{a,b}):\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{b})\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{a})

admits a compositional structure with respect to the consecutive graph-formula edges

E_{m}(f_{b-1,b}),\,E_{m}(f_{b-2,b-1}),\,\ldots,\,E_{m}(f_{a,a+1}).

These compositional structures may be chosen simultaneously for all intervals, compatibly with
restriction to subintervals, up to the usual contractible coherent choice.

More precisely, for each interval
[a,b]
, let
\Comp_{a,b}
be the space of composites of the
corresponding consecutive-edge spine. Then
\Comp_{a,b}
is contractible. After choosing a point

\xi_{a,b}\in\Comp_{a,b}
whose long edge is
E_{m}(f_{a,b})
, the space of comparisons from
any other composite structure on the same consecutive spine to
\xi_{a,b}
is contractible.

Proof.

For
a=b
, the assertion is the identity normalization of
Lemma
5.5.19
. For
b=a+1
, the assertion is tautological.

Fix an interval
a<b
. Let
S_{a,b}:{\mathbf{Spine}}_{b-a}\to\mathcal{C}
be the spine diagram whose vertices are

j\longmapsto\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{b-j})\qquad(0\leq j\leq b-a),

and whose consecutive edge
j\to j+1
is

E_{m}(f_{b-j-1,b-j}):\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{b-j})\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{b-j-1}).

Define
\Comp_{a,b}
as the fiber over
S_{a,b}
of the restriction map

\Fun(\Delta^{b-a},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}\longrightarrow\Fun({\mathbf{Spine}}_{b-a},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}.

Since the spine inclusion
{\mathbf{Spine}}_{b-a}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{b-a}
is inner anodyne, this
fiber is a contractible Kan complex.

It remains to see that
E_{m}(f_{a,b})
can be chosen as the long edge of a point of

\Comp_{a,b}
. We argue by induction on
b-a
. The cases
0
and
1
have already been
handled. For
b-a\geq 2
, the induction hypothesis gives a composite structure on

E_{m}(f_{a+1,b})

for the interval
[a+1,b]
. Lemma
5.5.6
, applied to

X_{a}\xrightarrow{f_{a,a+1}}X_{a+1}\xrightarrow{f_{a+1,b}}X_{b},

gives a homotopy

E_{m}(f_{a,a+1})\circ E_{m}(f_{a+1,b})\simeq E_{m}(f_{a,b}).

Together with the chosen composite structure on
E_{m}(f_{a+1,b})
, this is precisely the
boundary datum for a composite structure on
E_{m}(f_{a,b})
; the required filler exists because
the relevant inclusion is obtained by successively attaching inner horns.

Applying the same construction to the finite diagram of all subintervals of
[0,n]
, and using
the fact that all restriction maps between the corresponding composite spaces are Kan fibrations,
gives simultaneous choices
\xi_{a,b}
compatible with subintervals. Finally, if

\theta\in\Comp_{a,b}
is any other composite structure, the path space

\operatorname{Path}_{\Comp_{a,b}}(\theta,\xi_{a,b})

is contractible because
\Comp_{a,b}
is contractible. Restricting such a path to the long edge
gives the asserted contractible space of comparisons.
∎

Lemma 5.5.8

(Top graph-composition simplices)
.

Let

\tau=\bigl(X_{0}\xrightarrow{f_{1}}X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n}\bigr)\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n}.

There is a nonempty contractible Kan complex of pairs

\bigl(T_{\tau,m},(\eta_{a,b})_{0\leq a<b\leq n}\bigr)

where:

(1)

T_{\tau,m}:\Delta^{n}\to\mathcal{C}
has simplex-coordinate vertex

a\longmapsto\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a});

(2)

the restriction of
T_{\tau,m}
to the spine

0\to 1\to\cdots\to n

has consecutive edge

E_{m}(f_{n-a-1,n-a}):\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a})\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a-1})\qquad(0\leq a<n);

(3)

for every
0\leq a<b\leq n
, the datum
\eta_{a,b}
is an equivalence

T_{\tau,m}|_{\Delta^{\{a,b\}}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }E_{m}(f_{n-b,n-a})

in
\Fun(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
.

Moreover, for every simplicial operator
\alpha:[r]\to[n]
, the restriction

(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}T_{\tau,m}

is naturally a top graph-composition simplex for
\tau\circ\alpha
, up to contractible coherent
choice.

Proof.

Let

s_{\tau,m}:{\mathbf{Spine}}_{n}\to\mathcal{C}

be the spine diagram whose vertices are

a\longmapsto\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a})

and whose consecutive edge
a\to a+1
is

E_{m}(f_{n-a-1,n-a}):\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a})\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a-1})\qquad(0\leq a<n).

Since
{\mathbf{Spine}}_{n}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}
is inner anodyne, the space of fillers

T_{\tau,m}:\Delta^{n}\to\mathcal{C}

of this spine is a nonempty contractible Kan complex.

Fix such a filler. For every interval
0\leq a<b\leq n
, the restriction

T_{\tau,m}|_{\Delta^{\{a,a+1,\ldots,b\}}}

is a composite structure for the consecutive graph-formula edges from

\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a})
to
\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-b})
. By
Lemma
5.5.7
, we have already chosen a
composite structure

\xi_{n-b,n-a}

on the graph-formula edge

E_{m}(f_{n-b,n-a}):\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-a})\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-b}).

The comparison datum
\eta_{a,b}
is defined to be a path, inside the contractible composite
space for the interval
[n-b,n-a]
, from the composite structure induced by

T_{\tau,m}
to
\xi_{n-b,n-a}
. Restricting this path to the long edge gives an equivalence

T_{\tau,m}|_{\Delta^{\{a,b\}}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }E_{m}(f_{n-b,n-a})

in
\Fun(\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}
. The space of such paths is contractible.

Doing this simultaneously for all intervals gives a nonempty contractible Kan complex of pairs

\bigl(T_{\tau,m},(\eta_{a,b})_{0\leq a<b\leq n}\bigr).

Indeed, the compatibility conditions among intervals are imposed by a finite diagram of
restriction maps between contractible composite spaces, and the relevant restriction maps are Kan
fibrations.

Now let
\alpha:[r]\to[n]
be a simplicial operator. By
Notation
5.5.2
, the simplex-coordinate restriction
relevant to
\tau\circ\alpha
is
\alpha^{\vee}
. The restriction

(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}T_{\tau,m}

has vertex
j
equal to

\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{\alpha(r-j)}),

which is the
j
-th simplex-coordinate vertex of the top simplex attached to

\tau\circ\alpha
.

For an interval
0\leq a<b\leq r
, if

\alpha(r-b)<\alpha(r-a),

the restricted comparison data identify the corresponding edge of

(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}T_{\tau,m}

with the graph-formula edge

E_{m}(f_{\alpha(r-b),\alpha(r-a)}).

If

\alpha(r-b)=\alpha(r-a),

the corresponding edge is degenerate, and the required comparison with

E_{m}(\operatorname{id}_{X_{\alpha(r-a)}})

is supplied by Lemma
5.5.19
.

Thus
(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}T_{\tau,m}
is top graph-composition data for

\tau\circ\alpha
, up to contractible coherent choice. Compatibility for identities, pairs of
composable operators, and triples of composable operators follows by applying the same finite
composite-space argument and using

(\alpha\circ\beta)^{\vee}=\alpha^{\vee}\circ\beta^{\vee}.

∎

Proposition 5.5.9

(Graph-composition theorem)
.

For every
m\in\mathbf{Z}
and every simplex

\tau\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n},

the space of complete graph-composition data of
Definition
5.5.3
is a nonempty contractible Kan complex.

Proof.

Choose first the top graph-composition simplex

T_{\tau,m}:\Delta^{n}\to\mathcal{C}

together with the edge comparisons

\eta_{a,b}:T_{\tau,m}|_{\Delta^{\{a,b\}}}\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }E_{m}(f_{n-b,n-a})

from the contractible space supplied by
Lemma
5.5.8
.

We now construct the prism. Let

B_{n}=\bigl(\Delta^{n}\times\partial\Delta^{1}\bigr)\cup_{{\mathbf{Spine}}_{n}\times\partial\Delta^{1}}\bigl({\mathbf{Spine}}_{n}\times\Delta^{1}\bigr)\subset\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1}

be the two-faced spine prism of
Lemma
5.5.5
.
We define a map

B_{n}\to\mathcal{C}

as follows.

On
\Delta^{n}\times\{0\}
, put the top simplex
T_{\tau,m}
. On

\Delta^{n}\times\{1\}
, put the closed graph simplex

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\Gamma(\tau)):\Delta^{n}\to\mathcal{C}.

On the vertical edge over the simplex-coordinate vertex
a=n-r
, put

q_{\tau,r}^{*}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{r})\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(P_{r}(\tau)).

Finally, on the square over the consecutive edge
a\to a+1
, use
Lemma
5.5.17
for the interval

n-a-1<n-a.

Explicitly, this gives the homotopy

(\gamma_{\tau,n-a-1,n-a})_{m}^{*}\circ q_{\tau,n-a}^{*}\simeq q_{\tau,n-a-1}^{*}\circ E_{m}(f_{n-a-1,n-a}),

which is precisely the required square between the corresponding top and bottom consecutive
edges.

These assignments agree on overlaps by construction. Since

B_{n}\hookrightarrow\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1}

is inner anodyne by Lemma
5.5.5
, restriction induces a
trivial Kan fibration

\Fun(\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}\longrightarrow\Fun(B_{n},\mathcal{C})^{\simeq}.

Thus the space of extensions

R_{\tau,m}:\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{C}

of this boundary datum is a nonempty contractible Kan complex.

The space of complete graph-composition data is obtained from the contractible space of top
graph-composition data by taking, over each top datum, the contractible fiber of such prism
extensions. Hence it is a nonempty contractible Kan complex.
∎

Remark 5.5.10

(Higher graph composition)
.

The proof of Proposition
5.5.9
has two logically distinct
parts. First, Lemma
5.5.6
identifies the graph formula with
composition in the first nontrivial case. Iterating this inside the contractible spaces of
quasicategorical composites gives the top graph-composition simplices of
Lemma
5.5.8
. Second, after the top face has been
constructed, the actual prism filler is formal: the two-faced spine-prism inclusion of
Lemma
5.5.5
is inner anodyne.

Proposition 5.5.11

(Prismatic graph-comparison package)
.

For every
m\in\mathbf{Z}
, the following data can be chosen coherently.
For every simplex

\tau=\bigl(X_{0}\to X_{1}\to\cdots\to X_{n}\bigr)\in N(\Sm_{S})_{n},

there is a prismatic raw lci simplex

\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau):\Delta^{n}\to\mathcal{C}

with vertices

\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n}),\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{n-1}),\ldots,\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{0}),

together with a prismatic comparison

R_{\tau,m}:\Delta^{n}\times\Delta^{1}\to\mathcal{C}

from
\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau)
to the closed graph simplex

\mathcal{G}_{m}(\Gamma(\tau)).

The vertical edge over the simplex-coordinate vertex
a=n-r
is the smooth pullback

q_{\tau,r}^{*}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{r})\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(P_{r}(\tau)).

These choices satisfy:

(1)

for
n=0
,
\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(X)=\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)
;

(2)

for
n=1
, the edge attached to
f:Y\to X
is the graph-formula edge

E_{m}(f)=\mathcal{G}_{m}(X)\xrightarrow{\ q_{f}^{*}\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(Y\times_{S}X)\xrightarrow{\ (\Gamma_{f})_{m}^{*}\ }\mathcal{G}_{m}(Y);

(3)

for every simplicial operator
\alpha:[r]\to[n]
, there are equivalences

c^{\operatorname{prism}}_{\tau,\alpha,m}:\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau\circ\alpha)\xrightarrow{\ \sim\ }(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau),

compatible with identities, composition, and triple compositions in the sense of
Notation
4.4.1
.

Proof.

For each simplex
\tau
, choose a complete graph-composition datum

(T_{\tau,m},R_{\tau,m})

from the contractible space supplied by
Proposition
5.5.9
, and set

\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau):=T_{\tau,m}.

We choose these data with the following normalizations in dimensions
0
and
1
. If

\tau=X
is a vertex, we set

\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(X)=\mathcal{G}_{m}(X).

If
\tau=f:Y\to X
is a nondegenerate edge, we take

\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(f)=E_{m}(f)

strictly. If
\tau=s_{0}X
is the degenerate edge, we take the edge
E_{m}(\operatorname{id}_{X})
, together
with the comparison to the degenerate edge supplied by Lemma
5.5.19
.

The prismatic comparison
R_{\tau,m}
is part of the complete datum, so

\mathcal{M}_{m}^{\operatorname{prism}}(\tau)
is the upper face of a graph prism. Its vertical edge over the
simplex-coordinate vertex
a=n-r
is, by construction,

q_{\tau,r}^{*}:\mathcal{G}_{m}(X_{r})\to\mathcal{G}_{m}(P_{r}(\tau)).

Let
\alpha:[r]\to[n]
be a simplicial operator. By
Lemma
5.5.8
, the restricted upper face

(\alpha^{\vee})^{*}T_{\tau,m}

is top graph-composition data for
\tau\circ\alpha
, up to contractible coherent choice. The
chosen upper face

T_{\tau\circ\alpha,m}
